# Indian Music - Live Learning Session

> **Learner-first lock:** This session teaches you to hear an Indian musical claim as four separable things - a **system** (how pitch and time are organised), a **form** (the shape a composition takes), a **carrier** (the lineage, community or institution that transmits it), and a **sound-producing object** (the instrument and how it makes sound). It runs from the smallest audible step of pitch to the national academy that now recognises performers, and it keeps three habits throughout: name the term before praising the music, keep the two classical systems distinct without turning them into opposites, and refuse every "invented by" sentence that no contemporaneous source supports. Dance owns choreography and the codified dance forms; theatre and puppetry own dramaturgy; religion owns devotional doctrine; acoustics and instrument engineering belong to science. What is owned here is the **musical system, its repertoire, its transmission, its instruments and its institutions**.

## How to read this session

Work down the lessons in order. Each begins with the question a learner actually has at that point, draws or tabulates before it names, and hands over the technical word only after the idea it names has been established. Four habits are built deliberately, because they are the four places this topic is lost.

1. **Separate the classification from the thing classified.** A *thaat* is a parent scale that is not sung; a *raga* is a melodic entity that is sung. A *melakarta* is a parent raga; a *janya* is derived from it. Most objective failures in this topic are failures to hold a two-level system apart.
2. **Do not let a comparison become an opposition.** Hindustani and Carnatic music are two developments of a shared older theory. Every source that helps you compare them will also tempt you into "improvised versus fixed" or "foreign versus indigenous". Both temptations are wrong, and this session says so at the exact places where the temptation arises.
3. **Treat attribution as evidence, not as decoration.** "Amir Khusrau invented the sitar", "Tansen invented ragas", "the tabla was invented in the thirteenth century" - these are popular attributions carried by later literature, not statements a contemporaneous source establishes. You will lose nothing by writing "credited with" and you will gain the examiner's confidence.
4. **Keep the family of an instrument apart from the genre it plays.** The *shehnai* is an aerophone; *Sufiana kalam* is a genre; the *santoor* accompanies it. Mixing a family with a genre is the single commonest instrument error.

Everything is drawn in text. No audio, notation plate or photograph is needed to follow any lesson here.

| 1 | What does this topic own, and what four questions should I ask of any claim about Indian music? | Foundation |
| 2 | How does a continuous sound become a countable set of notes? | Foundation |
| 3 | What exactly is a raga, and what was it before it was called a raga? | Foundation |
| 4 | How is musical time organised, and why do the two traditions count it differently? | Foundation |

| 9 | Khayal, tarana and the rest of the named Hindustani styles | Core |
| 10 | Thumri, tappa and ghazal: what "semi-classical" is actually claiming | Core |
| 11 | The gharana: how a household became a musical institution | Core |
| 12 | Carnatic music: the kriti, the anatomy of a composition, and the order of a concert | Core |
| 13 | Comparing the two systems without inventing a binary | Core |
| 14 | Treatises, composers and patrons: who fixed the theory and who paid for the music | Core |

**Boundary map.** Dance topics own the eight codified classical dance forms, choreography, *nritta-nritya-natya* and the *mudra* system; music appears there as accompaniment, and appears here only where a musical form is itself defined by a dance occasion. Theatre and puppetry topics own dramaturgy and the performance text. Religion topics own Bhakti and Sufi doctrine; what is owned here is the musical form that doctrine was carried in - the bhajan's melody type, the qawwali's tempo architecture, the shabad's raga organisation - not the theology. Language and literature topics own the poetic history of the ghazal as verse; here the ghazal is a sung form. Medieval and modern history own dynastic chronology and colonial policy; here a ruler appears only as a patron or an author of a music treatise. Science and technology own acoustics and instrument engineering. Heritage and cultural-policy topics own award administration, recognition policy and every changeable status claim; a dated record is cited once in this session, in Lesson 18, and nothing beyond that record is asserted.

## Lesson 1 - What this topic owns, and the four questions to ask of any claim about Indian music

### Visual: four questions, and the exact error each one prevents

```text
   A CLAIM ABOUT INDIAN MUSIC ARRIVES
                 |
   +-------------+--------------+--------------+---------------+
   |             |              |              |               |
 SYSTEM        FORM          CARRIER      INSTRUMENT      (only then) VALUE
 How is        What shape    Who holds    What object     Is it beautiful,

 organised?    take?         skill?
   |             |              |              |

 Prevents:     Prevents:      Prevents:      Prevents:      Prevents nothing.

 thaat a       gharana a      genre a        genre an       unsupported
 raga          form           gharana        instrument     praise enters
```

Read the diagram from left to right and notice the shape of it. The first four questions are answerable from evidence. The fifth is not, and it is deliberately placed last and marked as answering nothing, because an answer that opens with "Indian music is an ancient and divine art" has spent its first sentence on the only column that carries no marks.

### 1. The exact scope, stated once

This topic owns the **musical system, the repertoire built on it, the mechanisms that transmit it, the instruments that realise it and the institutions that now sustain it**. Stated as a checklist of what must be answerable by the end of the session:

- the vocabulary of pitch and time: *shruti, swara, saptak, raga, tala, laya*;
- the two classificatory systems: the Hindustani *thaat* and the Carnatic *melakarta-janya* scheme;
- the two classical traditions, their formation and their genuine differences;
- the named Hindustani vocal forms and the named Carnatic forms;
- devotional, fusion, folk and regional music as owned material, not as an appendix;
- the *gharana* as a concept, the major named gharanas, and the caution that attends attribution;
- *guru-shishya* transmission, notation and institutional teaching;
- the fourfold classification of instruments, with mechanism, region and context;
- the major treatises, the named composers and the historical transitions;
- modern institutions, broadcasting, documentation and safeguarding, kept inside this topic's boundary.

### 2. The four questions, each with its technical vocabulary

**Question one - system.** What organises pitch and what organises time? The answer is the same in both traditions at the level of principle: pitch is organised by *swara* grouped into a *saptak* and shaped into a *raga*; time is organised by *tala* and paced by *laya*. The written grammars that fix this are called *Lakshanagranthas*, literally books of grammar, and Indian classical music is standardly described as resting on three pillars - **raga, tala and swara**.

**Question two - form.** What shape does the composition take? A *dhrupad* and a *khayal* are both Hindustani vocal music in a raga and a tala, but they differ in the proportion of wordless elaboration to composed text, in the accompanying drum, in the number of performers and in the emotional register. A *kriti* and a *varnam* are both Carnatic compositions, but one is a fully evolved concert piece and the other opens the concert. Form is the level at which most descriptive questions actually sit.

**Question three - carrier.** Who holds the skill and passes it on? In the ancient and early medieval description this is the *gurukul*, also called the *ashram* or hermitage system, embodying the *guru-shishya* relationship. In the later Hindustani world it is the **gharana**, a musical lineage or school based on style and ideology that shapes teaching and performance. In the Carnatic world the same function is performed by *guru-shishya parampara* without the gharana label being used in the same way. In folk music the carrier is a community - the Manganiar, the Langha, the Baul.

**Question four - instrument.** What object makes the sound, and by what mechanism? Four families answer this, and Lesson 17 works through them: *Tata* (string), *Sushira* (wind), *Avanaddha* (struck membrane) and *Ghana* (struck solid, no membrane, no tuning).

### 3. The vocabulary set that will not be re-explained later

These six terms recur constantly. Fix them now; each is developed properly in its own lesson.

| Term | Working definition, fixed here |
|---|---|
| **Shruti** | The smallest audible unit of pitch, the microtone. Twenty-two are counted per octave; of these twelve are described as audible - seven *suddha* and five *vikrit* swaras |
| **Swara** | A note or scale degree. Seven are named: Shadja, Rishabha, Gandhara, Madhyama, Panchama, Dhaivata, Nishada - abbreviated Sa, Re, Ga, Ma, Pa, Dha, Ni |
| **Saptak / Sargam** | The seven swaras taken together as a set |
| **Raga** | A melodic entity with a defined ascent and descent, a hierarchy among its notes and a characteristic identifying phrase; it is sung |
| **Tala** | The rhythmic grouping of beats into a cycle, ranging from three to one hundred and eight beats; it is independent of the melody that accompanies it |
| **Laya** | The tempo of the tala, which keeps the time span uniform; three named speeds - *vilambit*, *madhya*, *drut* |

Two of these are already enough to prevent a common failure. If *tala* is the grouping and *laya* is the tempo, then "the tala became faster" is a confused sentence: the tala did not change, the laya did.

### 4. The three chronologies this topic keeps apart

An examiner can test a date in this topic in three different senses, and confusing them is expensive.

1. **The date of a text.** *Natyashastra*, *Dattilam*, *Brihaddeshi*, *Sangita Ratnakara*, *Chaturdandiprakashika* - these are datable works, and each fixed a concept. This is the safest chronology in the topic.
2. **The date of a person or a patronage event.** Tansen at Akbar's court, Sadarang under Muhammad Shah, Purandara Dasa's years, Annamacharya's years. These are datable and are directly examinable.
3. **The date of a form or an instrument.** This is the dangerous one. "Dhrupad consolidated as a classical form in the thirteenth century and reached its zenith at Akbar's court" is supportable; "the tabla was invented in year X by person Y" is not. The invention dates of the tabla and the sitar remain uncertain, and no date should be supplied for either.

### 5. The boundaries, stated so they never have to be re-argued

- **Dance** owns the codified classical forms and choreography. Music crosses that line only where a musical genre is itself defined by dance - Lavani, performed to the *dholki*; Dollu Kunitha, named after a drum; Kolattam, a stick dance; the Manipuri *Sankirtana*, which is singing, drumming and dancing together. In those cases the music is owned here and the dance form is routed.
- **Theatre and puppetry** own dramaturgy. Where a musical form narrates an epic - Pandavani, with its *gayan* and *vadan* - the musical structure is owned here.
- **Religion topics** own Bhakti and Sufi doctrine. The bhajan, the shabad and the qawwali are owned here as **forms**: their melodic type, their instruments, their performance setting and their tempo architecture.
- **Language and literature** own the ghazal as a poetic genre. Its *bayt* structure is stated here only because a sung ghazal is built on it.
- **Science and technology** own acoustics and instrument engineering.
- **Heritage and cultural policy** own award administration and every changeable status claim. This session cites exactly one dated record, in Lesson 18, and asserts nothing beyond it.

### Examination use

Three things in this lesson are directly testable: the three pillars of Indian classical music; the *Lakshanagranthas* as the name for the grammatical treatises; and the *gurukul* or hermitage description with its twelve years of residence. Worth more than all three is the four-question frame itself, because it converts an unstructured "write about Indian music" demand into four evidenced paragraphs. A comparison demand is answered by running the frame twice. A "significance" demand is answered by running the frame once and then adding the carrier argument.

### Traps introduced here

- Beginning an answer with an evaluative sentence about antiquity, divinity or greatness, which uses the one column that carries no evidence.
- Treating *tala* and *laya* as synonyms, and therefore writing that a tala speeds up.
- Assuming the gharana label applies symmetrically to both traditions.
- Supplying an invention date for a form or an instrument because a person's dates are known.
- Importing devotional doctrine into a music answer because the form is devotional.

### Revision notes

1. Four analytical questions: system, form, carrier, instrument. Value comes last and is not evidence.
2. Three pillars of Indian classical music: **raga, tala, swara**.
3. *Lakshanagranthas* = treatises on musical grammar and rules, developed over many centuries.
4. *Gurukul* = ashram or hermitage system; guru-shishya relationship; students resided twelve years serving the master; hermitages received royal and wealthy patronage; prince and commoner treated alike.
5. *Shruti* = smallest audible pitch unit; 22 counted, 12 described as audible = 7 suddha + 5 vikrit.
6. *Saptak / Sargam* = the seven swaras together; Sa and Pa are *achala* (fixed), the rest *chala*.
7. *Tala* = beat-cycle, 3 to 108 beats, independent of the melody; *laya* = its tempo.
8. Three chronologies: text date, person or patronage date, form or instrument date. The third is the unreliable one.
9. Tabla and sitar: invention dates uncertain; supply none.
10. Boundaries: choreography to dance, dramaturgy to theatre, doctrine to religion, verse history to literature, acoustics to science, award administration to heritage policy.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Indian classical music rests on the raga, which is a thaat sung in a particular tala at a particular laya, and is transmitted through gharanas in both the Hindustani and Carnatic traditions." Identify every error and give a corrected sentence.

**Model answer:** There are three errors. First, a raga is not a thaat that is sung. A thaat is a parent scale used for classification; it is stated only in ascending order, has no *vadi* or *samvadi*, carries no emotional quality and is not sung, whereas a raga has both ascent and descent, has *vadi* and *samvadi*, and is sung. Ragas are grouped under thaats; they are not thaats in performance. Second, the sentence collapses tala and laya into one attribute - the tala is the cycle of beats and the laya is the tempo at which that cycle moves, so a raga is sung *in* a tala *at* a laya, and the two cannot be listed as parallel properties of the same kind without saying which does what. Third, the gharana label is not symmetric: it is the characteristic Hindustani description of lineage-based transmission, while the Carnatic tradition is transmitted through *guru-shishya parampara* and is not conventionally organised into gharanas. A corrected sentence: "Indian classical music organises pitch through *swara* and *raga* and time through *tala*, whose tempo is the *laya*; ragas are classified under parent scales - *thaats* in Hindustani practice, *melakartas* in Carnatic practice - and are transmitted through guru-shishya teaching, which in the Hindustani world became formalised as the gharana."

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that because two traditions share a vocabulary they share an organisation. They share *raga*, *tala* and *swara* as principles. They do not share the number of parent scales, the derivation rule, the improvisation formats, the concert order or the language of transmission. A comparison answer earns its marks precisely at those non-shared points.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"An answer on Indian music is stronger when it is organised by system, form, transmission and instrument than when it is organised chronologically." Examine. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The claim is largely sound, and the test is what each organisation can explain.

A chronological account produces a procession - Vedic chant, treatises, dhrupad, khayal, thumri, the modern concert - and cannot explain why any step followed the last. An analytical account begins with the object. The system layer explains why a raga is neither a scale nor a mode and why two parent-scale schemes, ten thaats and seventy-two melakartas, are not interchangeable. The form layer explains why dhrupad needs a pakhawaj and a duo while khayal needs a bandish and taans. The transmission layer explains the gharana as a structure that concentrated a scarce skill in a household, and explains why lineages outlived the courts that created them. The instrument layer explains why the ghatam is not a drum.

The qualification is that some demands are genuinely chronological - treatise sequence, the fourteenth-century divergence - and the analytic frame must then carry the dates inside it.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = naming what a chronological account cannot explain 2 + running all four analytical layers with one concrete evidence each 5 + the qualification that treatise and divergence chronology must still be carried 2 + a verdict that adopts the frame rather than rejecting chronology 1.**

## Lesson 2 - Sound made countable: shruti, swara and the saptak
