```text
   Dattilam (Dattila)          -> confirms 22 shrutis as the only humanly
                                  possible sounds; ritual chant -> gandharva music
   Brihaddeshi (Matanga,       -> defines the term RAGA
     6th-8th c.)
   Sangeet Ratnakara           -> 264 RAGAS defined; microtones classified
     (Sarangadeva, 13th c.)
   Swaramela-Kalanidhi         -> deals with ragas
     (Ramamatya, 16th c.)
   Chaturdandiprakashika       -> key insights into Carnatic musicology;
     (Venkatamakhin, 17th c.)     the MELAKARTA system
                            |
   TRANSMISSION -> GURUKULS in ancient and early medieval India for intensive
                   musical training
                            |
   THE DIVERGENCE
   Historical roots of BOTH traditions traced to Bharata's Natyashastra
   ->  they DIVERGED IN THE 14TH CENTURY
   The terms "Carnatic" and "Hindustani" are found for the FIRST TIME in
   Haripala's Sangeeta Sudhakara (14th century AD)
                            |
        +-------------------+-------------------+
        |                                       |
   HINDUSTANI                              CARNATIC
   Dhruvapad -> DHRUPAD by the 15th c.      kriti-based; rooted in the Bhakti
   KHAYAL emerges as a new style by         movement; largely devotional; tied to
   the 17th c.; folk singing styles         temple and spiritual traditions of
   also develop in this period              South India
```

The diagram is built so that every arrow is labelled with what the stage **added**. That is the difference between a chronology that can be argued and a list of names that can only be recited.

### 1. The earliest material, stated exactly as it is recorded

Two objects anchor the beginning, and both must be stated with their recorded hedges intact.

- **A musical instrument, the seven-holed flute, has been recovered from the sites of the Indus Valley Civilisation.** This is an archaeological recovery statement and can be used as evidence.
- **Another instrument, the Ravanahatha, is believed to have originated from the Hela Civilisation in Sri Lanka and is one of the oldest known.** Note "believed to have originated" - the hedge belongs to the claim and should travel with it.

**Music in India dates to the Vedic era, with Sama Veda notes, Gandharva Veda, Veena in the Aitareya Aranyaka, Om, and references by Panini and Bharata's *Natyashastra* marking its early theory and practice.** That sentence is a list of six anchors; reproducing three of them precisely is worth more than paraphrasing all six.

### 2. The later Vedic stage and its two named genres

**Ritualistic music in the later Vedic period included *Sangama*, verse chanting, and *Jatigan*, narrative music.** Two named genres, one liturgical and one narrative - which is the earliest form of a distinction this topic keeps making, between music as ritual act and music as told story. Pandavani, twenty-five centuries later, is still narrative music with an instrument.

### 3. The treatise ladder read as a chronology of concepts

This is the safest chronology in the whole topic, and it should be learnt as a sequence of **contributions**, not of titles. Lesson 14 treats the treatises in full; what follows is the spine.

| Stage | Text and author | What it added |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Natyashastra**, Bharata | Elaborated musicology; identified the **octave and 22 shrutis** (microtones) |
| 2 | **Dattilam**, Dattila | Confirmed **22 shrutis per octave as the only humanly possible sounds**; discusses the transition from ritual chants to gandharva music |
| 3 | **Brihaddeshi**, Matanga, **6th-8th century** | **Focused on defining the term *raga*** |
| 4 | **Sangeet Ratnakara**, Sarangadeva, **13th century** | **Defined 264 ragas** and classified microtones; regarded as definitive by **both** traditions |
| 5 | **Swaramela-Kalanidhi**, Ramamatya, **16th century** | Dealt with ragas |
| 6 | **Chaturdandiprakashika**, Venkatamakhin, **17th century** | Key insights into Carnatic musicology; introduced the **melakarta** system |

Two observations convert the table into an argument. The ladder moves from **measuring** pitch, to **naming** the melodic entity, to **counting** the entities, to **generating** them by rule - a movement from description to system. And the pivot is the *Sangeet Ratnakara*: written in the thirteenth century, it is regarded as a definitive text by **both** Hindustani and Carnatic traditions, which places it before the divergence and makes it the single strongest piece of evidence for shared theoretical roots.

### 4. Transmission in the same period

**Gurukuls existed in ancient and early medieval India for intensive musical training.** The system is described in detail: also known as the **ashram or hermitage system**, embodying the **guru-shishya tradition** with a very close teacher-student relationship; in the ancient period the teachers were sages and **students lived in the hermitage for twelve years, gaining knowledge by serving their master**; **these hermitages received patronage from kings and other wealthy persons**; life was **rigorous and pensive, with in-depth knowledge obtained through direct experience**; and **all students, whether a prince or a commoner, were treated the same, with no discrimination**.

That last clause is worth quoting in an answer on transmission, because it sets up the contrast with the gharana of Lesson 11, whose reproduction was frequently by descent.

### 5. The divergence, stated with its evidence

**While the historical roots of both these music types can be traced to Bharata's *Natyashastra*, they diverged in the fourteenth century.** The textual evidence for that date is separate and specific: **the terms "Carnatic" and "Hindustani" are found for the first time in Haripala's *Sangeeta Sudhakara*, a fourteenth-century AD text.**

Those two statements together are what an answer needs. One gives the shared root; the other gives the earliest attestation of the two names. A candidate who can pair them is not reciting a date but citing the evidence for it.

### 6. The Persianate encounter, and how to write about it without a false causation

The recorded statement is: **Persian and Islamic influence transformed North Indian music from devotional Dhruvapad to Dhrupad by the fifteenth century. By the seventeenth century, Khayal emerged as a new style of Hindustani music. Various folk singing styles also developed during this period.** Elsewhere the same material summarises Hindustani influence as **Arab, Persian and Afghan** and Carnatic influence as **indigenous**.

This is exactly the point at which weak answers turn a historical encounter into an essentialist contrast - "Hindustani is the foreign one, Carnatic the pure one". The correction is firm and is part of what this topic owns.

- **Both traditions are historical formations.** Hindustani practice developed through North Indian courtly, devotional and Persianate encounters; Carnatic practice crystallised around South Indian temple, court and later *sabha* networks. Neither is an essence.
- **The contrast is of grammar, repertoire, ornament and performance sequence** - not of "foreign versus pure", and not of "improvised versus fixed".
- **Continuity is as strong as change.** Dhrupad is recorded as finding mention even in the *Natyashastra*, as evolving from *Prabandha* and *Dhruvapada*, as including Sanskrit syllables and as being **of temple origin** - and it is that form which reached its zenith at a Mughal court. A tradition of temple origin flourishing under imperial patronage is not a story of replacement.
- **The direction of traffic ran both ways.** Bhatkhande's northern classification was modelled on a southern scheme; the Bhendibazaar gharana is recorded as including Carnatic ragas; and named ragas standing outside the thaat system are recorded as Carnatic-derived.

The safe formulation, usable verbatim: *"North Indian music developed through sustained contact with Persianate court culture, which is recorded in the vocabulary, the patronage structures and the emergence of new forms; this was a redirection of an existing tradition with its own theoretical texts and temple repertoire, not the replacement of one music by another."*

### Examination use

Objective-ready: the seven-holed flute from Indus Valley sites; the Ravanahatha with its hedge; Sangama and Jatigan; the treatise-to-contribution pairs, especially Brihaddeshi for the term *raga* and Sangeet Ratnakara for 264 ragas; the twelve-year gurukul residence; the fourteenth-century divergence; and **Haripala's *Sangeeta Sudhakara* as the first text in which the terms Carnatic and Hindustani appear**. That last item is the highest-yield single fact in the lesson. In a descriptive answer, this lesson supplies the chronological spine that a "trace the development" demand requires, and the correction that a "Persian influence" demand requires.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing that Hindustani music was created by Persian influence, rather than transformed through an encounter.
- Treating "Carnatic is indigenous" as a claim about purity rather than about the record of influence.
- Attributing the term *raga* to the *Natyashastra*; the *Natyashastra* gives the octave and the twenty-two shrutis, while **Brihaddeshi** is the text that focuses on defining *raga*.
- Dating the divergence without naming *Sangeeta Sudhakara*, which is the evidence for it.
- Forgetting that dhrupad is recorded as of **temple origin** while also reaching its zenith at a Mughal court.
- Treating the gurukul and the gharana as the same institution.

### Revision notes

1. **Seven-holed flute recovered from Indus Valley Civilisation sites**; **Ravanahatha** believed to have originated in the **Hela Civilisation, Sri Lanka**, among the oldest known.
2. Vedic anchors: **Sama Veda notes, Gandharva Veda, Veena in the Aitareya Aranyaka, Om, Panini, Bharata's Natyashastra.**
3. Later Vedic ritual genres: **Sangama** (verse chanting) and **Jatigan** (narrative music).
4. Treatise ladder: Natyashastra - octave and 22 shrutis; Dattilam - confirms 22 as the only humanly possible sounds; **Brihaddeshi (Matanga, 6th-8th c.) - defines *raga***; **Sangeet Ratnakara (Sarangadeva, 13th c.) - 264 ragas, microtones classified, definitive for both traditions**; Swaramela-Kalanidhi (Ramamatya, 16th c.) - ragas; Chaturdandiprakashika (Venkatamakhin, 17th c.) - melakarta.
5. **Gurukul**: hermitage system, guru-shishya, **twelve years** of residence and service, royal and wealthy patronage, **no discrimination between prince and commoner**.
6. Both traditions traced to the *Natyashastra*; **diverged in the 14th century**.
7. **Haripala's *Sangeeta Sudhakara* (14th century AD): the terms Carnatic and Hindustani appear for the first time.**
8. Persian and Islamic influence: **Dhruvapad -> Dhrupad by the 15th century**; **Khayal emerges by the 17th century**; folk singing styles also develop.
9. Recorded influence summary: Hindustani - **Arab, Persian and Afghan**; Carnatic - **indigenous**. State as a record of contact, never as an essence.
10. Continuity evidence: dhrupad is of **temple origin**, mentioned in the *Natyashastra*, evolved from *Prabandha* and *Dhruvapada*, and uses Sanskrit syllables - yet reached its zenith at Akbar's court.
11. Two-way traffic: thaat modelled on melakarta; Bhendibazaar gharana includes Carnatic ragas; several non-thaat ragas are Carnatic-derived.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Hindustani music was created by Persian influence in the medieval period, while Carnatic music preserved the pure indigenous tradition." Rewrite this as a defensible sentence, and list the evidence that forces each change.

**Model answer:** A defensible rewriting: "Both classical traditions trace their theoretical roots to Bharata's *Natyashastra* and diverged in the fourteenth century, the terms Carnatic and Hindustani appearing for the first time in Haripala's *Sangeeta Sudhakara*; North Indian practice thereafter developed through sustained Persianate courtly contact, recorded in its vocabulary, its patronage and its new forms, while South Indian practice developed through temple, court and later sabha institutions." Four changes, each forced by evidence. "Created by" becomes "developed through", because dhrupad is recorded as of temple origin, as mentioned in the *Natyashastra* and as having evolved from *Prabandha* and *Dhruvapada* before any court encounter - an existing tradition was redirected, not manufactured. "Pure" is dropped, because purity is not a historical category and because the Carnatic scheme itself supplied the model on which Bhatkhande built the northern classification, while the Bhendibazaar gharana is recorded as including Carnatic ragas and several ragas outside the thaat system are recorded as Carnatic-derived. The shared root is added, because the divergence claim is meaningless without it. And the fourteenth-century date is given its textual evidence, because a date with a source is an argument and a date without one is a memory.

**Misconception to avoid:** Thinking that acknowledging Persianate influence concedes something. It does not. The influence is recorded, it is specific - vocabulary, patronage, new forms - and the strongest answer is the one that states it precisely and then shows what did not change.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Trace the evolution of Indian classical music from the Vedic period to the fourteenth-century divergence, identifying the contribution of each major stage. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

Indian classical music's evolution is best traced as a sequence in which each stage added a capacity the previous stage lacked.

The **material and Vedic base** supplies practice and vocabulary. A seven-holed flute has been recovered from Indus Valley sites, and music is recorded from the Vedic era through Sama Veda notes, the Gandharva Veda, the veena in the Aitareya Aranyaka, Om, and references in Panini and the *Natyashastra*. The **later Vedic stage** names genres: *Sangama*, verse chanting, and *Jatigan*, narrative music - a distinction between ritual act and told story that the tradition never loses.

The **treatise ladder** supplies theory in ordered instalments. Bharata's *Natyashastra* identified the octave and twenty-two shrutis; *Dattilam* confirmed those twenty-two as the only humanly possible sounds and recorded the movement from ritual chant to gandharva music; Matanga's *Brihaddeshi*, of the sixth to eighth centuries, focused on defining *raga*; Sarangadeva's thirteenth-century *Sangeet Ratnakara* defined two hundred and sixty-four ragas and classified microtones. The movement is from measuring pitch to naming the melodic entity to counting the entities.

**Transmission** ran through gurukuls - the hermitage system, twelve years of residence and service, royal and wealthy patronage, prince and commoner treated alike.

The **divergence** follows in the fourteenth century, with both traditions traced to the *Natyashastra* and the terms Carnatic and Hindustani first attested in Haripala's *Sangeeta Sudhakara*.

The qualification: the *Sangeet Ratnakara* remains definitive for both traditions, so the divergence separated practice, ornament and repertoire, not the theoretical foundation.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the Indus flute and at least three Vedic anchors 3 + Sangama and Jatigan named correctly 2 + four treatises each paired with its own contribution 6 + the gurukul described with the twelve-year residence and the no-discrimination clause 3 + the divergence dated and evidenced by Sangeeta Sudhakara 4 + the qualification that the shared theoretical base survives the divergence 2.**

## Lesson 8 - Dhrupad: the oldest named form, its four voices and the lineages that carry it

Progress: 8/19 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the ten named Hindustani styles, dhrupad's etymology and ancestry, its dated anchors, its performance architecture, the four vani-based gharanas, and the position of dhamar and hori

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: dhrupad, from word to performance to lineage

```text

                                                       decline: 18th century
                              |
   THE PERFORMANCE ARCHITECTURE
   [1] ALAP - wordless; tempo gradually rises to evoke the raga's mood
        |            (no drum yet)
        v
   [2] MAIN DHRUPAD begins ------> PAKHAWAJ accompaniment enters
        |
        v
   Sanskrit syllables | of TEMPLE ORIGIN | usually 4-5 stanzas |
   performed by a DUO | generally two male vocalists |
   TANPURA + PAKHAWAJ accompany
                              |
   THE FOUR VANIS, THE FOUR LINEAGES
   +--------------+---------------------------+----------------------------+
   | Dagar        | Dagar Vani (linked to     | Alaap emphasis, meditative,|
   | (Delhi/UP)   | Gauhar)                   | often sung in pairs        |
   | Darbhanga    | Khandar Vani + Gauhar     | Alaap AND Bandish equally; |
   | (Bihar)      | Vani                      | rich in LAYAKARI           |
   | Bettiah      | Gauhar Vani + Khandar     | BANDISH is the core;       |
   | (Bihar)      | Vani                      | Alaap given less weight    |
   | Talwandi     | Khandar Vani              | strong, forceful; nearly   |
   | (Punjab, now |                           | extinct after decline and  |
   | Pakistan)    |                           | partition                  |
   +--------------+---------------------------+----------------------------+
```

The diagram separates three things that weak answers merge: what the word means, when the form is attested, and how it is performed. An examiner can ask for any one of them alone.

### 1. The ten styles, stated once

**There are ten main styles of singing in Hindustani music: Dhrupad, Dhamar, Hori, Khayal, Tappa, Chaturang, Ragasagar, Tarana, Sargam and Thumri.** Committing this list is efficient, because three separate demands - "name the styles", "which are semi-classical", "which is the oldest" - all draw on it.

Two framing statements belong with the list. **The Hindustani branch of music focuses more on the musical structure and the possibilities of improvisation within it.** And **the Hindustani branch adopted a scale of *Shudha Swara Saptaka*, the "Octave of Natural Notes".**

Of the ten, four are developed in detail in the sources and therefore in this session - Dhrupad and Khayal here and in Lesson 9, Tappa and Thumri in Lesson 10, with Tarana in Lesson 9. **Chaturang, Ragasagar and Sargam are named among the ten but are not separately described in the owned material, and no description is supplied for them here.** Naming them correctly as members of the list is what the evidence supports; inventing their structure is not.

### 2. Dhrupad: word, ancestry and the four dated anchors

**Dhrupad is one of the oldest and grandest known forms of Hindustani classical music, and finds mention even in the *Natyashastra* (200 BC-200 AD).**

**Dhrupad, derived from *dhruva* (fixed) and *pada* (verse), evolved from *Prabandha* and *Dhruvapada*.** The etymology is itself an argument: a *fixed verse* names a form in which the composed text is stable and the elaboration happens around it - which is exactly the contrast with khayal, where the same *bandish* is rendered differently by every singer.

The four dated anchors, in order, and each doing different work:

| Anchor | Date | What it establishes |
|---|---|---|
| Mention in the ***Natyashastra*** | 200 BC-200 AD | Antiquity of the practice named in the tradition |
| ***Yugala Shataka***, written by **Shri Shribhatta** | **1294 CE** | **Contains lyrics in the Dhrupad style; one of the earliest known sources related to Dhrupad** |
| Consolidation as a classical form | **13th century** | The form's arrival as a classical genre |
| **Abu Fazl's *Ain-i-Akbari*** | **1593** | **The earliest source that names the music genre as Dhrupad** |

Read the second and fourth rows together and you have a lesson in evidence that is worth carrying into every art-and-culture answer: the earliest source that **contains** the style and the earliest source that **names** it are three centuries apart. A form can exist before its label is recorded.

**Dhrupad became the major form of singing in the medieval period but fell into decline in the eighteenth century.** Lesson 9 supplies what replaced it and why.

### 3. The patronage cluster at Akbar's court

**Although Dhrupad consolidated its position as a classical form of music in the thirteenth century, it reached its zenith in the court of Emperor Akbar.**

**Akbar employed and patronised musical masters including Swami Haridas and Mian Tansen.** **Tansen - one of the *Navaratna*, or nine gems, in Akbar's court - and Baiju Bawra were the students of Swami Haridas and were major proponents of dhrupad music during the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries.**

A compact teacher-pupil chain of this kind is examinable in both directions, so fix it as a shape: **Swami Haridas -> Tansen and Baiju Bawra**, with Akbar as patron of the school rather than of a single musician. Lesson 14 adds the full patronage detail and the attribution cautions that attach to Tansen.

### 4. The performance architecture

**Dhrupad is essentially a poetic form that is incorporated into an extended presentation style, marked by precise and overt elaboration of a raga.**

The sequence is exact and is the most answerable part of the lesson:

1. **Dhrupad begins with a wordless *Alap*, where the tempo gradually rises to evoke the raga's mood.**
2. **After the Alap, the main Dhrupad starts with *Pakhawaj* accompaniment.**

And the fixed features:

- **Dhrupad includes the use of Sanskrit syllables and is of temple origin.**
- **Dhrupad compositions usually have four to five stanzas and are performed by a duo.**
- **Generally two male vocalists perform the Dhrupad style, with *Tanpura* and *Pakhawaj* usually accompanying them.**

Notice the internal consistency. A form of temple origin, using Sanskrit syllables, opening without words and without a drum, and accompanied by the pakhawaj rather than the tabla, is a coherent object - austere, text-fixed, liturgically descended. Every later Hindustani form can be described by how far it departs from this baseline, which is why dhrupad is taught first.

### 5. The four vanis and their lineages

**Dhrupad singing can be further divided into four sub-forms on the basis of the *vanis* or *banis* that they perform.** Note the classification principle: the dhrupad lineages are distinguished by a **named style of delivery**, the *vani*, whereas the khayal gharanas of Lesson 9 are named by **place**. The two kinds of gharana are not organised on the same principle.

| Gharana (region) | Associated vani | Speciality and style | Famous exponents |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Dagar Gharana** (Delhi / UP) | **Dagar Vani**, linked to Gauhar | Emphasis on **Alaap**; meditative style; often sung in pairs; devotional texts, though mainly Muslim singers | **Gundecha Brothers** - Umakant and the late Ramakant - and the **Dagar family** |
| **Darbhanga Gharana** (Bihar) | **Khandar Vani and Gauhar Vani** | **Equal focus on Alaap and Bandish**; rich in **Layakari** (rhythmic improvisation) | **Ram Chatur Mallick, Pt. Siyaram Tewari**, the **Mallick family** |
| **Bettiah Gharana** (Bihar) | **Gauhar Vani and Khandar Vani** | Treats **Bandish as the core** of performance; Alaap given less importance | **Mishra family** (Indra Kishore Mishra) |
| **Talwandi Gharana** (Punjab, now in Pakistan) | **Khandar Vani** | Strong, forceful style; **nearly extinct due to historical decline and partition** | Few surviving exponents; **largely extinct** |

Three analytical points that convert the table into answer material:

- **The table is internally ordered.** Read the third column downwards: Dagar emphasises alaap; Darbhanga balances alaap and bandish; Bettiah makes bandish the core and reduces alaap. That is a single sliding scale from wordless elaboration to composed text, and stating it as a scale is worth more than listing three descriptions.
- **The vanis are shared, not exclusive.** Khandar Vani appears in three of the four rows and Gauhar Vani in three. A vani is a manner of delivery that lineages draw on, not a private property of one household. A fourth vani name, **Nauhar Vani**, appears in Lesson 9 as the origin of the Agra gharana, which completes the traditional set of four.
- **Talwandi records a political cause of musical loss.** Partition is named as a reason a lineage became nearly extinct. That is the clearest available evidence in this topic that transmission by household is fragile in a way that transmission by institution is not - the argument Lesson 18 completes.

A recorded variation to note without resolving: one canonical statement of the same material assigns Darbhanga the **Khandar Vani** alone and pairs it with an equal focus on alaap and layakari, while the fuller table above gives Darbhanga **both Khandar and Gauhar Vani** and describes the balance as between alaap and bandish. Both formulations are reported; neither is corrected here.

### 6. The gharana, defined here and developed in Lesson 11

The working definition belongs with this lesson because the dhrupad lineages are its first instance. **A gharana is a musical lineage or school, based on style and ideology, shaping teaching and performance. Famous Hindustani gharanas include Agra, Gwalior, Indore, Jaipur, Kirana and Patiala.**

Keep that list of six. It is the recorded roll of famous gharanas, and two of its members - **Indore** and **Jaipur** - are named there and nowhere else in the owned material. Lesson 11 returns to both.

### 7. Dhamar and Hori: what is owned, and what is not

**Dhamar** is named as one of the ten main styles of Hindustani singing. Beyond that naming, **the owned material supplies no separate description of dhamar's structure, tala or repertoire**, and none is invented here. What it does supply is one linkage, used in Lesson 9: the **Agra (Rangila) gharana is described as a blend of Khyal with Dhrupad-Dhamar**, which places dhamar firmly in the dhrupad family of forms rather than with khayal. The safe formulation is: *"Dhamar is named among the ten Hindustani styles and is associated with the dhrupad family, being paired with dhrupad in the description of the Agra gharana's blended style."*

**Hori** is in an unusual position and the position should be stated rather than smoothed over. It is named among the ten main styles of Hindustani singing. It is separately named as one of the lighter forms for which *thumri* is used as a generic name - alongside **Dadra, Kajari, Saavan, Jhoola and Chaiti** - which is Lesson 10's material. And it appears again as a folk music of **Uttar Pradesh**, **based on the love pranks of Radha and Krishna and mainly associated with the Holi festival**, which is Lesson 16's material. One name, three registers. The honest statement is that the name denotes a **seasonal Holi-associated song type that exists in classical, semi-classical and folk registers**, and that is exactly the kind of crossing Lesson 15's fusion discussion is about.

### Examination use

Objective-ready here: the ten styles; *dhruva* plus *pada*; *Prabandha* and *Dhruvapada* as ancestors; the *Natyashastra* mention; **Yugala Shataka, Shribhatta, 1294 CE**; the thirteenth-century consolidation; **Ain-i-Akbari, 1593, as the earliest source naming the genre**; the eighteenth-century decline; Swami Haridas as guru of Tansen and Baiju Bawra; the alap-then-pakhawaj sequence; four to five stanzas and a duo; tanpura and pakhawaj; and the four vani-gharanas with their regions. In a descriptive answer, dhrupad is the baseline against which every other Hindustani form is defined, so a "development of Hindustani vocal music" demand should open here.

### Traps introduced here

- Dating dhrupad's **naming** and its **existence** to the same source; the 1294 lyrics and the 1593 naming are three centuries apart.
- Attaching the tabla to dhrupad. Dhrupad's drum is the **pakhawaj**.
- Describing dhrupad as beginning with a composed text; it begins with a **wordless** alap.
- Saying dhrupad originated at the Mughal court. It reached its **zenith** there; it is of **temple origin**.
- Treating a *vani* as the exclusive property of one gharana; Khandar and Gauhar Vani each appear in three of the four rows.
- Supplying a structure for Dhamar, Chaturang, Ragasagar or Sargam from memory.

### Revision notes

1. Ten Hindustani styles: **Dhrupad, Dhamar, Hori, Khayal, Tappa, Chaturang, Ragasagar, Tarana, Sargam, Thumri.**
2. Hindustani branch: focuses on **musical structure and possibilities of improvisation**; adopted the **Shudha Swara Saptaka**, the Octave of Natural Notes.
3. **Dhrupad** = *dhruva* (fixed) + *pada* (verse); evolved from **Prabandha** and **Dhruvapada**; mentioned in the *Natyashastra*.
4. **Yugala Shataka, Shri Shribhatta, 1294 CE** - earliest known source containing Dhrupad-style lyrics.
5. **Abu Fazl's *Ain-i-Akbari*, 1593** - earliest source that **names** the genre as Dhrupad.
6. Consolidated as a classical form in the **13th century**; **zenith at Akbar's court**; **declined in the 18th century**.
7. **Swami Haridas -> Tansen and Baiju Bawra**; Tansen among Akbar's **Navaratna**; both major proponents in the 15th-16th centuries.
8. Performance: **wordless Alap first**, tempo rising to evoke the raga's mood; then the main dhrupad with **Pakhawaj**; **Sanskrit syllables**; **temple origin**; **4-5 stanzas**; performed by a **duo**, generally two male vocalists, with **Tanpura and Pakhawaj**.
9. Four vani-gharanas: **Dagar** (Delhi/UP, Dagar Vani, alaap-heavy, sung in pairs, Gundecha Brothers and the Dagar family); **Darbhanga** (Bihar, Khandar and Gauhar Vani, alaap and bandish equally, layakari, Ram Chatur Mallick and Pt. Siyaram Tewari); **Bettiah** (Bihar, Gauhar and Khandar Vani, bandish-core, Mishra family); **Talwandi** (Punjab, now Pakistan, Khandar Vani, forceful, nearly extinct after partition).
10. The alaap-to-bandish sliding scale runs **Dagar -> Darbhanga -> Bettiah**.
11. **Gharana** = musical lineage or school based on style and ideology, shaping teaching and performance; famous ones: **Agra, Gwalior, Indore, Jaipur, Kirana, Patiala**.
12. **Dhamar**: named among the ten, linked to the dhrupad family through the Agra gharana's Dhrupad-Dhamar blend; no separate structure supplied. **Chaturang, Ragasagar, Sargam**: named only.
13. **Hori**: appears as one of the ten styles, as a lighter thumri-type form, and as a Radha-Krishna Holi folk song of Uttar Pradesh.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using dhrupad as the baseline, state four features against which any other Hindustani vocal form can be measured, and show how the four dhrupad lineages already differ on one of them.

**Model answer:** The four features are: **the weight of wordless elaboration** - dhrupad opens with a wordless alap in which the tempo gradually rises to evoke the raga's mood; **the drum** - dhrupad is accompanied by the pakhawaj, entering only when the main composition begins, not by the tabla; **the text and its language register** - dhrupad uses Sanskrit syllables, is of temple origin and has compositions of usually four to five stanzas; and **the performing body** - dhrupad is performed by a duo, generally two male vocalists, with tanpura and pakhawaj. Any later form can be located by how far it moves on these four. The four dhrupad lineages already differ on the first. Reading their descriptions in order, the Dagar gharana places its emphasis on alaap and sings in a meditative style, often in pairs; the Darbhanga gharana gives equal focus to alaap and bandish and is rich in layakari; and the Bettiah gharana treats bandish as the core of performance and gives alaap less importance. That is a single sliding scale within one form, running from maximum wordless elaboration to maximum composed text - which is why khayal's later reduction of alap in favour of taans is a continuation of an internal tendency and not an imported novelty.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating dhrupad as monolithic because it is old and austere. The vani classification exists precisely because the form admitted several distinct manners of delivery, and the differences among its lineages are of the same kind as the differences between forms.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Dhrupad's four dated anchors show why the history of a musical form must distinguish practice from nomenclature." Discuss. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The claim is sound, and dhrupad supplies the clearest available demonstration.

Four anchors are recorded. The form is mentioned in the *Natyashastra*, dated between 200 BC and 200 AD. *Yugala Shataka*, written by Shri Shribhatta in 1294 CE, contains lyrics in the Dhrupad style and is one of the earliest known sources related to it. The form consolidated as a classical genre in the thirteenth century. And Abu Fazl's *Ain-i-Akbari* of 1593 is the earliest source that **names** the genre as Dhrupad.

The last two anchors are three centuries apart, and they are anchors of different kinds. One attests the practice; the other attests the label. A history that dates dhrupad to 1593 has dated a word, and a history that reads the *Natyashastra* mention as proof of the mature form has dated an ancestor - *Prabandha* and *Dhruvapada*, from which dhrupad is recorded as having evolved.

The qualification is that naming still matters: the point at which a tradition names a genre marks its recognition as a distinct classical form.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = all four anchors stated with their exact dates and sources 4 + the practice-versus-nomenclature distinction argued from the 1294-1593 gap 3 + the Prabandha and Dhruvapada ancestry used to qualify the Natyashastra mention 2 + the concession that naming marks recognition 1.**

## Lesson 9 - Khayal, tarana and the rest of the named Hindustani styles

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: khayal measured against the dhrupad baseline

```text
   FEATURE                 DHRUPAD (Lesson 8)          KHAYAL (this lesson)
   ------------------------------------------------------------------------
   name means              fixed verse                 idea or imagination
                           (dhruva + pada, Sanskrit)   (from Persian)
   opening                 long wordless ALAP          "Alap is given much less
                                                       room as compared to
                                                       Dhrupad"
   signature device        overt raga elaboration      frequent use of TAANS
   composed text           4-5 stanzas, fixed          BANDISH: short songs of
                                                       2 to 8 lines
   rendering               text and raga stable        "each singer renders the
                                                       same bandish differently,
                                                       keeping text and raga
                                                       constant"
   typical theme           devotional, temple origin   romantic; love, even of
                                                       divine creatures; praise of
                                                       God or a king; exceptional
                                                       compositions in praise of
                                                       Lord Krishna
   performers              duo, two male vocalists     not specified as a duo
   tempo design            single extended arc         TWO SONGS:
                                                       BADA KHYAL - slow tempo
                                                        (comprises most of the
                                                         performance)
                                                       CHHOTA KHYAL - fast tempo
   ------------------------------------------------------------------------
   PATRONAGE AND DATES
   origin attributed to Amir Khusrau  -> treat as ATTRIBUTION (Lesson 19)
   Hussain Shah, Sharqi ruler of Jaunpur Sultanate: most generous patronage
       to Khyal in the 15th century
   Khayal emerges as a new style of Hindustani music by the 17th century
   Sadarang (Nyamat Khan, 1670-1748), court musician of Muhammad Shah,
       shaped Khyal music (Lesson 14)
```

The visual is deliberately a comparison rather than a description, because khayal is best understood as a set of decisions taken against dhrupad. Every row is a departure, and the departures are consistent: less wordless elaboration, shorter text, more rhythmic and melodic display, a wider emotional range, and a two-tempo design.

### 1. The word and the attribution

**The word *Khyal* is derived from a Persian word and it means "idea or imagination".** **The origin of this style was attributed to Amir Khusrau.**

Reproduce the second sentence in its attributive form, because that is how the evidence stands. The same body of material records that Amir Khusrau is also credited with the invention of the sitar, is called the Father of Qawwali, is said to have introduced the ghazal, tarana and trivat, is believed to have invented a three-stringed instrument called the *Sehtar* - and, in the case of qawwali, that **the credit has been widely disputed**. When a single figure is credited with the invention of several instruments and several genres, the correct posture is attributive throughout. Lesson 19 develops the discipline; here it is enough to write "attributed to" and move on.

### 2. Why the form spread: improvisation and a short text

**This form is popular amongst the artists as it provides greater scope for improvisation.** **Khyal is based on a repertoire of short songs ranging from two to eight lines. Generally, a Khyal composition is also referred to as a *Bandish*.**

Those two facts are causally connected, and saying so is the analytical move the lesson makes available. A short composed text leaves most of the performance time to be filled by the performer. A four-or-five-stanza dhrupad composition does not. The consequence is recorded directly: **generally, each singer renders the same bandish differently, while keeping the text and raga constant** - which makes the performer, rather than the composition, the object of attention. That is the deepest difference between the two forms, and it explains both khayal's popularity and the rise of performer-centred lineages in Lesson 11.

### 3. Patronage and dating

**Hussain Shah, a Sharqi ruler of the Jaunpur Sultanate, gave the most generous patronage to Khyal in the fifteenth century.** And, from Lesson 7's chronology, **by the seventeenth century Khayal emerged as a new style of Hindustani music.**

A regional sultanate, not the imperial centre, is named as the form's most generous early patron. That is a useful corrective to an imperial-centre account of Indian music, and it pairs with the later evidence that khayal was shaped at the imperial court by **Sadarang (Nyamat Khan, 1670-1748)**, court musician of **Muhammad Shah**. Regional patronage first, imperial consolidation later - and the eighteenth-century decline of dhrupad coincides with that consolidation.

### 4. The structure: two songs, one performance

**One of the most unique features of Khyal is the frequent use of *taans* in the composition. Therefore, Alap is given much less room in Khyal music as compared to Dhrupad.**

**A typical Khyal performance uses two songs:**

1. **Bada Khyal** - sung in the **slow** tempo;
2. **Chhota Khyal** - sung in the **fast** tempo.

**Bada Khyal comprises most of the performance.**

Recall Lesson 4: this is a **laya** design, not a tala design. The slow-to-fast movement is the tempo architecture of the performance.

**Usually, the theme for these Khyal bandish is romantic in nature. They sing about love, even if the song is related to divine creatures. They also may praise God or a particular king. Exceptional Khyal compositions are composed in the praise of Lord Krishna.**

### 5. The five khayal gharanas

**The major gharanas under Khyal music** are the following. Note the classification principle announced in Lesson 8: these are named by **place**, while the dhrupad lineages were named by **vani**.

| Gharana | Region / founder | Speciality and style | Famous exponents |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Gwalior** | Gwalior, Madhya Pradesh; **among the oldest Khyal gharanas** | **Equal emphasis on melody and rhythm**; use of simple ragas, *sapaat* (straight) taans; rigorous and elaborate style | **Natthu Khan, Vishnu Digambar Paluskar** |
| **Kirana** | Kirana, Uttar Pradesh; **founded by Nayak Gopal**, popularised by **Abdul Karim Khan and Abdul Wahid Khan** in the 20th century | Focus on **precise tuning of swaras**, slow-tempo ragas, **melody over rhythm**, clear pronunciation | **Abdul Karim Khan, Abdul Wahid Khan, Pandit Bhimsen Joshi, Gangubai Hangal** |
| **Agra (Rangila)** | Origin in **Nauhar Vani**; founded by **Haji Sujan Khan / Khudabuksh** in the 19th century | **Blend of Khyal and Dhrupad-Dhamar**; stress on **Bandish**; lyrical style | **Faiyyaz Khan, Vilayat Hussain Khan** |
| **Patiala** | Punjab; founded by **Bade Fateh Ali Khan and Ali Baksh Jarnail Khan** in the 19th century; **patronised by the Maharaja of Patiala** | Famous for **Khyal, Thumri, Ghazal**; focus on rhythm and emotional expression; intricate taans, *gamak*, *tarana gayaki* | **Bade Ghulam Ali Khan** |
| **Bhendibazaar** | Mumbai, 19th century; founded by **Chhajju Khan, Nazir Khan and Khadim Hussain Khan** | **Breath-control technique** - long passages in one breath; **inclusion of Carnatic ragas** | **Khadim Hussain Khan** |

Four analytical points:

- **Agra's origin in *Nauhar Vani* completes the four-vani set** begun in Lesson 8 - Dagar, Khandar, Gauhar, Nauhar - and shows a khayal gharana carrying a dhrupad delivery style. Its description as a blend of khyal with dhrupad-dhamar is the same fact stated from the other side.
- **Gwalior and Kirana occupy opposite positions on one axis.** Gwalior gives equal emphasis to melody and rhythm; Kirana places melody over rhythm with slow-tempo ragas and precise tuning. Naming that axis is worth more than describing both gharanas separately.
- **Bhendibazaar records two-way traffic.** A Mumbai gharana that includes Carnatic ragas is direct evidence against sealed traditions, and pairs with Lesson 5's non-thaat Carnatic-derived ragas.
- **Patiala is the one gharana defined across registers.** It is famous for khyal, thumri **and** ghazal, which is why it reappears in Lesson 10. A gharana is not confined to one genre.

Two specific cautions belong with this table. **Vishnu Digambar Paluskar** appears here as a Gwalior exponent and reappears in Lesson 18 as the founder of a music school in 1901 - the same person in two roles, performer and institution-builder, which is itself the argument of Lesson 18. And **Pandit Bhimsen Joshi is Kirana**; he must not be attached to the Benaras gharana, and **Ustad Bismillah Khan**, who is associated with Benaras, must not be attached to Kirana. Crossing those two is the likeliest error the table has to prevent.

### 6. Tarana

**In this style, rhythm is crucial. The structure mainly consists of melody, which is usually short and repeated with variations. A contrasting higher-pitched melody appears once before returning to the main theme.**

**It uses many words that are sung at a fast tempo. The focus is on producing rhythmic matters and hence the singers need specialised training and skills in rhythmic manipulation.**

On origin and later use: **the tarana style was invented by Amir Khusrau in the thirteenth-fourteenth centuries and was also used by the tenth Sikh Guru, Guru Gobind Singh, in his compositions.** The first clause is an attribution of the kind discussed above and should be written as "credited to"; the second is a recorded use and can be stated directly.

On present practice: **one of the most renowned tarana singers is Pandit Rattan Mohan Sharma of the Mewati Gharana. The audience at the Pandit Motiram Sangeet Samaroh in Hyderabad gave him the title "Tarana ke Baadshah", King of Tarana.**

The **Mewati Gharana** is named here and nowhere else in the owned material - a seventh named Hindustani gharana beyond the six of the famous list. Record it as named; do not supply it a founder, a region or a style description.

### 7. What replaced dhrupad, stated as a mechanism

Dhrupad declined in the eighteenth century; khayal emerged by the seventeenth and was shaped at the imperial court in the first half of the eighteenth. The mechanism is visible in the features, and stating it is the highest-value sentence available in this lesson:

*A form with a short composed text, reduced wordless elaboration, frequent taans, a slow-to-fast two-song design and a romantic thematic range gives the individual performer more room, more display and a wider audience than a temple-descended form in Sanskrit syllables performed by a duo to a pakhawaj - and it is that shift of attention from the composition to the performer which made the performer's lineage, the gharana, the organising institution of North Indian music.*

### Examination use

Objective-ready: khayal from Persian meaning idea or imagination; the attribution to Amir Khusrau; **Hussain Shah of the Jaunpur Sultanate as the most generous fifteenth-century patron**; bandish as two to eight lines; bada and chhota khyal with bada comprising most of the performance; the five khayal gharanas with founders, regions and exponents; Agra's Nauhar Vani origin; Bhendibazaar's breath control and Carnatic ragas; tarana's rhythmic focus and Guru Gobind Singh's use of it; and the Mewati gharana with its named exponent and title. This lesson also supplies the second half of any "development of Hindustani vocal music" answer.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing that Amir Khusrau invented khayal, the tarana, the sitar and qawwali as settled fact.
- Reversing bada and chhota khyal, or forgetting that the **bada** khyal comprises most of the performance.
- Attaching khayal's most generous early patronage to a Mughal emperor rather than to **Hussain Shah of Jaunpur**.
- Saying khayal has no alap. Alap is given **much less room** than in dhrupad, not none.
- Attaching Bhimsen Joshi to Benaras or Bismillah Khan to Kirana.
- Treating a gharana as confined to one genre; Patiala is recorded for khyal, thumri and ghazal together.

### Revision notes

1. **Khyal** from Persian, meaning **idea or imagination**; origin **attributed to Amir Khusrau**.
2. Greater scope for improvisation; repertoire of **short songs of two to eight lines**; a khyal composition is a **bandish**.
3. **Each singer renders the same bandish differently, keeping text and raga constant.**
4. **Hussain Shah, Sharqi ruler of Jaunpur Sultanate: most generous patronage to Khyal in the 15th century.** Khayal emerged as a new style by the **17th century**.
5. **Frequent taans**; **alap given much less room than in dhrupad**.
6. **Bada Khyal - slow tempo, comprises most of the performance; Chhota Khyal - fast tempo.** A laya design.
7. Themes: usually **romantic**; love even of divine creatures; praise of God or a king; exceptional compositions in praise of **Lord Krishna**.
8. **Gwalior** - among the oldest khyal gharanas; equal emphasis on melody and rhythm; simple ragas, *sapaat* taans; Natthu Khan, V.D. Paluskar.
9. **Kirana** - founded by **Nayak Gopal**, popularised by Abdul Karim Khan and Abdul Wahid Khan; precise tuning, slow tempo, **melody over rhythm**; Bhimsen Joshi, Gangubai Hangal.
10. **Agra (Rangila)** - origin in **Nauhar Vani**; founded by Haji Sujan Khan / Khudabuksh, 19th century; **khyal blended with Dhrupad-Dhamar**, stress on bandish; Faiyyaz Khan, Vilayat Hussain Khan.
11. **Patiala** - founded by Bade Fateh Ali Khan and Ali Baksh Jarnail Khan, 19th century, patronised by the Maharaja of Patiala; **khyal, thumri and ghazal**; gamak and *tarana gayaki*; Bade Ghulam Ali Khan.
12. **Bhendibazaar** - Mumbai, 19th century; Chhajju Khan, Nazir Khan, Khadim Hussain Khan; **breath control**, long passages in one breath; **includes Carnatic ragas**.
13. **Tarana**: rhythm crucial; short melody repeated with variations; one contrasting higher-pitched melody; many words at fast tempo; credited to Amir Khusrau, 13th-14th centuries; used by **Guru Gobind Singh**; **Pandit Rattan Mohan Sharma, Mewati Gharana, "Tarana ke Baadshah"**.
14. The four vanis complete: **Dagar, Khandar, Gauhar (Lesson 8) + Nauhar (Agra)**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain, using recorded features only, why khayal displaced dhrupad as the dominant Hindustani form, and identify the feature that did most of the work.

**Model answer:** Five recorded features push in the same direction. Khayal's composed text is short - a bandish of two to eight lines - against dhrupad's four to five stanzas, so most of the performance time belongs to the performer. Alap is given much less room than in dhrupad, removing the long wordless opening that demands the most patience from an audience. Taans are used frequently, supplying audible display of technique. The performance is designed as two songs, a slow *bada khyal* comprising most of it and a fast *chhota khyal*, which builds a tempo arc rather than a single extended one. And the thematic range widens from dhrupad's Sanskrit-syllable, temple-descended repertoire to romantic themes, praise of God or of a king, and compositions in praise of Krishna. The feature that does most of the work is the recorded statement that **each singer renders the same bandish differently while keeping the text and raga constant**. That single sentence relocates the object of attention from the composition to the performer, and everything else follows from it - including, as Lesson 11 shows, why the performer's lineage rather than the repertoire became North Indian music's organising institution. Patronage confirms rather than causes the shift: Hussain Shah of Jaunpur gave khayal its most generous fifteenth-century patronage, and Sadarang shaped it at Muhammad Shah's court while dhrupad declined in the eighteenth century.

**Misconception to avoid:** Explaining the displacement by taste - that khayal was "more beautiful" or "more emotional". Taste is not evidence. The features listed are recorded, they are mutually reinforcing, and they explain the outcome without any appeal to preference.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Examine the statement that the Hindustani gharana system reflects the internal logic of the forms it transmits rather than merely regional difference. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The statement is well supported, and the evidence lies in how the two sets of gharanas are classified.

The dhrupad lineages are named by **vani** - a manner of delivery - not by place alone. Dagar carries the Dagar Vani, Darbhanga the Khandar and Gauhar Vani, Bettiah the Gauhar and Khandar Vani, Talwandi the Khandar Vani, and the Agra khayal gharana traces its origin to the Nauhar Vani. Their distinguishing features lie on a single internal axis of the form itself: Dagar emphasises alaap, Darbhanga balances alaap and bandish while specialising in layakari, Bettiah makes bandish the core and reduces alaap. That is dhrupad's own tension between wordless elaboration and composed text, distributed across households.

The khayal gharanas are named by place - Gwalior, Kirana, Agra, Patiala, Bhendibazaar - but their descriptions again track the form's internal variables. Gwalior gives equal emphasis to melody and rhythm with simple ragas and *sapaat* taans; Kirana places melody over rhythm with precise tuning and slow-tempo ragas; Patiala stresses rhythm, intricate taans, gamak and emotional expression; Bhendibazaar specialises in breath control and imports Carnatic ragas; Agra stresses bandish and blends in dhrupad-dhamar.

Region is therefore a label of origin, not the content of the distinction - which is why a Mumbai gharana can sing Carnatic ragas and a Punjab gharana can be defined by khyal, thumri and ghazal together.

The qualification is that region is not empty: patronage was territorial, as the Maharaja of Patiala's support shows, and Talwandi's near-extinction after partition was a political and geographical event.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the vani-versus-place classification contrast stated as the thesis 3 + three dhrupad lineages placed on the alaap-bandish axis 4 + four khayal gharanas each tied to a formal variable rather than to a region 5 + the qualification restoring territorial patronage with Patiala and Talwandi as evidence 3.**

### Visual: what "semi-classical" changes, variable by variable

```text
   THE SEMI-CLASSICAL REGISTER - four recorded departures from the classical
   +--------------------+-------------------------------------------------+
   | still based on     | SWARA (note) - the foundation is not abandoned  |
   | departs on         | RAGA: "lighter versions of ragas like Bhoopali  |
   |                    | or Malkaush are used"; deviates slightly from   |
   |                    | the standard structure of the raga              |
   | departs on         | TALA: "a lighter version of tala"               |
   | departs on         | LAYA: madhyam or drut - a FASTER tempo          |
   | departs on         | EMPHASIS: greater weight on BHAVA and LYRICS    |
   |                    | than on alap-jor-jhala                          |
   +--------------------+-------------------------------------------------+

   THE THREE PROMINENT SEMI-CLASSICAL STYLES
   +-----------+---------------+---------------+---------------------------+
   |           | THUMRI        | TAPPA         | GHAZAL                    |
   +-----------+---------------+---------------+---------------------------+
   | origin    | Uttar Pradesh | folk songs of | Iran, 10th century;       |
   |           |               | the camel     | to South Asia in the 12th |
   |           |               | riders of     | century via Sufi mystics  |
   |           |               | north-west    | and the courts of the new |
   |           |               | India         | Islamic sultanate         |
   | raga base | mixed simple  | -             | single raga (see Lesson   |
   |           | ragas         |               | 15 for qawwali contrast)  |
   | mood      | romantic or   | fast, subtle, | one subject: LOVE,        |
   |           | devotional;   | knotty        | unconditional and         |
   |           | inherent      | constructions;| superior; object often    |
   |           | sensuality    | very quick    | open to interpretation,   |
   |           |               | turns of      | divine or human           |
   |           |               | phrase        |                           |
   | language  | Hindi, Braj   | -             | Urdu (see performers)     |
   |           | Bhasha        |               |                           |
   | legitimated| Bhakti        | brought to    | zenith in the Mughal      |
   | by        | movement      | the Mughal    | period                    |
   |           | inspiration   | court of      |                           |
   |           |               | Muhammad Shah |                           |
   | status    | living        | FACING        | simplified over time;     |
   |           |               | EXTINCTION    | reached wider audiences   |
   +-----------+---------------+---------------+---------------------------+
```

The table is built to show that "semi-classical" is not a judgement of quality. It is a specification: same swara foundation, lighter raga and tala treatment, faster tempo, and a shift of weight from structural elaboration to *bhava* and text.

### 1. The definition, stated precisely

**The semi-classical style of music is also based on *swara*. However, it deviates slightly from the standard structure of the raga in the way that lighter versions of ragas like Bhoopali or Malkaush are used. They employ a lighter version of tala and use *madhyam* or *drut laya* - that is, they have a faster tempo. There is also greater emphasis on *bhava* and lyrics than on alap-jor-jhala.**

Four departures, one retention. Write it that way and the register is defined without evaluation. Note also what the definition tells you about the classical register by contrast: *alap-jor-jhala* names the extended instrumental and vocal elaboration that the semi-classical forms reduce.

### 2. Thumri

**Thumri originated in Uttar Pradesh, is usually based on mixed simple ragas, and is commonly considered to be semi-classical Indian music.**

- **Theme.** **The compositions can be either romantic or devotional in nature.**
- **Source of inspiration.** **This style was inspired by the Bhakti movement so much so that the text usually revolves around a girl's love for Krishna.** The devotional movement is the source of the thematic material; the doctrine belongs to the religion topic, the form belongs here.
- **Language.** **The language of the composition is usually Hindi in the Braj Bhasha dialect.**
- **Voice.** **The compositions are usually sung by a female, which is different to other forms, as thumri is characterised by its inherent sensuality.** Place this beside dhrupad's "generally two male vocalists" from Lesson 8 and the contrast becomes a structural fact about the repertoire, not an incidental detail.
- **As a generic label.** **Thumri is also used as a generic name for some other, even lighter, forms, such as Dadra, Hori, Kajari, Saavan, Jhoola and Chaiti.** Note that several of these are **seasonal or festival** song types - Kajari and Saavan belong to the rains, Hori to Holi, Chaiti to the month of Chaitra - which is precisely where the semi-classical register touches the folk repertoire of Lesson 16.
- **Dance link.** **Thumri is linked to the classical dance Kathak.** The dance form itself is routed to the dance topic; what is owned here is the linkage.
- **Gharanas and exponents.** **The main gharanas of thumri are based in Varanasi and Lucknow.** **One of the most timeless voices of thumri is that of Begum Akhtar, a household name for her husky voice and boundless range.** **Other famous proponents include Girija Devi and Chhannulal Mishra of the Purab Ang Thumri.**

Two cross-links belong here. The **Patiala gharana** of Lesson 9 is recorded as famous for khyal, thumri and ghazal together - so thumri is not confined to its own gharanas. And **Girija Devi** is separately recorded among the exponents produced by the **Benaras gharana** of Hindustani classical music, alongside Bismillah Khan and Sitara Devi - which is Varanasi under its other name, and which shows a single city functioning as a centre across vocal, instrumental and dance registers.

### 3. Tappa

**In this style, the rhythm plays a very important role, as the compositions are based on fast, subtle and knotty constructions.**

The origin story is unusually specific and is the most valuable thing in the section: **it originated from the folk songs of the camel riders of north-west India but gained legitimacy as a semi-classical vocal specialty once it was brought to the Mughal court of Emperor Muhammad Shah.**

That single sentence is a complete model of how a genre changes status. A folk idiom, tied to an occupation and a region, is carried to a court; the court's adoption is what confers the classification. Notice that the mechanism runs **upward** - folk to court - which is the opposite direction from the usual assumption that classical forms trickle down. Lesson 15 shows the traffic running both ways.

**There is a great use of very quick turns of phrases.**

The patronage history continues past the court: **Tappa was the genre of choice of the wealthy elite as well as those of more modest means. The *baithaki* style evolved under the direct patronage of the elites of the zamindari classes of the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries in their *baithak khanas* - assembly halls - and *jalsaghar*, literally halls for entertainment.**

Two named domestic venues, *baithak khana* and *jalsaghar*, are recorded here and nowhere else in the topic. They are the strongest available evidence for a stage of Indian musical patronage between the princely court and the modern concert hall - the private elite gathering. Lesson 18 needs this.

And the present status: **today, the style is facing extinction as it is rarely used by musicians any longer. Some of the very few expounders of this style include Laxmanrao Pandit of the Gwalior Gharana and Shanno Khurana of the Rampur-Sahaswan Gharana.**

The **Rampur-Sahaswan Gharana** is named here and nowhere else in the owned material. Record it as named; supply it no founder or style description.

### 4. Ghazal

**The ghazal is a short poem consisting of rhyming couplets, called *Bayt* or *sher*. Most ghazals have between seven and twelve *bayts*. However, a ghazal's couplets do not need a common theme or continuity.**

That last clause is the formal point most often missed: the couplet is the unit of sense, so a ghazal is not a narrative and need not be read as one.

**Transmission.** **Ghazal is said to have originated in Iran in the tenth century, from where it spread to South Asia in the twelfth century due to the influence of Sufi mystics and the courts of the new Islamic sultanate, reaching its zenith during the Mughal period.** **It is said that Amir Khusrau (1253-1325 AD) was one of the first expounders of the art of Ghazal in India.** **Many of the major historical Ghazal poets were either avowed Sufis themselves - like Rumi or Hafiz - or were sympathisers with Sufi ideas and beliefs.**

**Subject.** **Ghazal not only has a specific form, but traditionally deals with just one subject: love, specifically an unconditional and superior form of love. The object of love is often written in a manner that makes it open to interpretation, where it may be divine or humanly, depending on what the listener construes. Gods are often personified too.**

The ambiguity is **designed**, not accidental, and it is the feature that lets the same text function devotionally and secularly at once. This is the single best illustration in the topic of a form whose meaning is completed by the listener.

**Later history and performers.** **Over time, ghazals have simplified in language, reaching wider audiences beyond classical forms like khyal and thumri. Famous performers include Muhammad Iqbal, Mirza Ghalib, and Kazi Nazrul Islam, who popularised Bengali ghazals.** Ghulam Ali is separately named in the same treatment as a ghazal performer.

Two disciplines attach. First, the boundary: the literary history of the ghazal as verse belongs to the language and literature topic; what is owned here is the ghazal as a **sung form** - its raga treatment, its performers and its movement into a wider audience. Second, accuracy of category: Mirza Ghalib and Muhammad Iqbal are recorded here as **famous ghazal names**, and the recorded formulation should be reproduced rather than converted into a claim that they were singers.

### 5. Why the three belong together

They share the four departures of the definition, but they arrive in the register by three different routes, and saying so is the analytical payoff of the lesson:

- **Thumri** arrives from **devotion** - inspired by the Bhakti movement, with a text about a girl's love for Krishna, and a female singing voice.
- **Tappa** arrives from **folk occupation** - the songs of camel riders of north-west India, legitimated by a court and then sustained by zamindari drawing rooms.
- **Ghazal** arrives from **foreign poetic form** - Iranian in the tenth century, carried by Sufi mystics and sultanate courts in the twelfth, at its zenith under the Mughals.

Devotion, occupation, importation. Three routes into one register - which is why "semi-classical" is a description of musical treatment and not of origin.

### Examination use

Objective-ready: the four semi-classical departures with Bhoopali and Malkaush as the named lighter ragas; thumri's Uttar Pradesh origin, Braj Bhasha language, Bhakti inspiration, Kathak link, Varanasi and Lucknow gharanas, and Begum Akhtar, Girija Devi and Chhannulal Mishra; the six lighter forms under the thumri label; tappa's camel-rider origin and its legitimation at Muhammad Shah's court, the *baithaki* style with *baithak khana* and *jalsaghar*, and its present near-extinction; the ghazal's *bayt* or *sher* unit, the seven-to-twelve count, the no-common-theme rule, the tenth-century Iranian origin and twelfth-century arrival, and the named poets and performers.

### Traps introduced here

- Reading "semi-classical" as "lesser". The recorded meaning is lighter raga and tala treatment, faster laya and more weight on bhava and lyrics.
- Attaching thumri to a male performance convention, when the recorded convention is female.
- Forgetting that thumri is also a **generic label** for Dadra, Hori, Kajari, Saavan, Jhoola and Chaiti.
- Making tappa a court creation. It originated in the **folk songs of camel riders** and was legitimated at court.
- Giving a ghazal a continuous theme. Its couplets **do not need** a common theme or continuity.
- Dating the ghazal's arrival in South Asia to the Mughal period; it arrived in the **twelfth century** and reached its **zenith** under the Mughals.

### Revision notes

1. **Semi-classical** = based on swara; lighter versions of ragas (**Bhoopali, Malkaush**); lighter tala; **madhyam or drut laya**; greater emphasis on **bhava and lyrics** than on alap-jor-jhala.
2. **Thumri**: origin **Uttar Pradesh**; **mixed simple ragas**; romantic or devotional; inspired by the **Bhakti movement**, text usually about a girl's love for **Krishna**; language **Hindi in Braj Bhasha**; usually sung by a **female**; characterised by inherent sensuality.
3. Thumri as a generic name for lighter forms: **Dadra, Hori, Kajari, Saavan, Jhoola, Chaiti**.
4. **Thumri is linked to the classical dance Kathak.**
5. Thumri gharanas: **Varanasi and Lucknow**; exponents **Begum Akhtar**, **Girija Devi** and **Chhannulal Mishra** of the **Purab Ang Thumri**.
6. **Benaras Gharana** of Hindustani classical music produced **Bismillah Khan, Sitara Devi, Girija Devi**.
7. **Tappa**: rhythm central; fast, subtle, knotty constructions; **originated from the folk songs of the camel riders of north-west India**; legitimated at the Mughal court of **Muhammad Shah**; great use of very quick turns of phrase.
8. **Baithaki** style under **zamindari** patronage of the late 19th and early 20th centuries, in **baithak khanas** (assembly halls) and **jalsaghar** (halls for entertainment).
9. Tappa today: **facing extinction**; few exponents - **Laxmanrao Pandit** (Gwalior Gharana), **Shanno Khurana** (Rampur-Sahaswan Gharana).
10. **Ghazal**: rhyming couplets called **Bayt or sher**; most ghazals have **7 to 12 bayts**; couplets **need no common theme or continuity**.
11. Ghazal transmission: **Iran, 10th century -> South Asia, 12th century** through Sufi mystics and the courts of the new Islamic sultanate -> **zenith in the Mughal period**. **Amir Khusrau (1253-1325 AD)** said to be among the first expounders in India.
12. Ghazal subject: **one subject - love**, unconditional and superior; the object **open to interpretation, divine or human**; gods often personified.
13. Ghazal names recorded: **Muhammad Iqbal, Mirza Ghalib, Kazi Nazrul Islam** (popularised Bengali ghazals); **Ghulam Ali** named as a performer.
14. Three routes into one register: **devotion (thumri), folk occupation (tappa), imported poetic form (ghazal)**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using tappa, explain how a musical genre changes status, and state what the case shows about the direction of influence between folk and classical music.

**Model answer:** Tappa's recorded history supplies each step. It **originated from the folk songs of the camel riders of north-west India** - an idiom tied to an occupation and a region, with no classical apparatus. It **gained legitimacy as a semi-classical vocal specialty once it was brought to the Mughal court of Emperor Muhammad Shah**: the change of status is explicitly attributed to the change of venue and patron, not to a change in the music. It then acquired a further patronage base below the court - the *baithaki* style evolved under the direct patronage of the zamindari elites of the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries in their *baithak khanas* and *jalsaghar*. And it is now recorded as **facing extinction**, rarely used by musicians, with very few exponents remaining. So status was conferred by adoption, sustained by a second patronage class, and lost when both disappeared. The case shows that influence between folk and classical music runs **upward as well as downward**: a classical register can be enlarged by absorbing a folk idiom, and the resulting genre is classified by its treatment - lighter raga and tala, faster laya, emphasis on bhava - rather than by its origin. That is consistent with the broader statement that classical and folk music intermingle over time and that other forms emerge from the mixture.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that "semi-classical" marks an incomplete or degraded classical form. Tappa entered the register from below and thumri from devotional song; the label describes how the raga and tala are treated, not how far a form has fallen from some classical standard.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The semi-classical forms of Hindustani music are the meeting point of folk, devotional and courtly traditions." Elucidate. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The claim holds, because the three prominent semi-classical styles enter the register by three different routes and are then treated alike.

Thumri enters from **devotion**: it originated in Uttar Pradesh, was inspired by the Bhakti movement to the point that its text usually concerns a girl's love for Krishna, is sung in Braj Bhasha, and is linked to Kathak. Tappa enters from **folk occupation**: it originated in the folk songs of the camel riders of north-west India and gained legitimacy only when it was brought to Muhammad Shah's court, later surviving in zamindari *baithak khanas* and *jalsaghar*. Ghazal enters from a **courtly and imported poetic form**: Iranian in the tenth century, carried into South Asia in the twelfth by Sufi mystics and sultanate courts, at its zenith under the Mughals.

What unifies them is treatment, not origin - lighter ragas such as Bhoopali or Malkaush, a lighter tala, a faster laya, and weight placed on bhava and lyrics.

The qualification is that the meeting is uneven: thumri remains current while tappa is recorded as facing extinction.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the three-routes thesis 2 + one precise origin fact for each of thumri, tappa and ghazal 4 + the four shared treatment features named with at least one named lighter raga 3 + the unevenness qualification using tappa's recorded decline 1.**

## Lesson 11 - The gharana: how a household became a musical institution

Progress: 11/19 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the gharana definition and the two naming principles, the complete roll of named gharanas, the gharana as a patronage and labour structure, the Jaipur-Atrauli case, and the attribution discipline
