Book context: Queried - the gharana definition box and famous-gharana roll, the dhrupad vani table, the khayal gharana table, the thumri and tappa gharana references, the Benaras gharana passage and the instrument-gharana note
CA search: "Not required - transmission lesson; the one current linkage in this session is placed at Lesson 18"
CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the complete roll, grouped by what actually names each gharana

```text
   GHARANA = a musical lineage or school, based on STYLE and IDEOLOGY,
             shaping TEACHING and PERFORMANCE
                              |
       +----------------------+----------------------+
       |                      |                      |
   NAMED BY VANI          NAMED BY PLACE        NAMED BY INSTRUMENT
   (a manner of           (a town, city or      (a playing tradition)
    delivery)              region)
       |                      |                      |
   DHRUPAD                VOCAL - KHAYAL AND     SITAR GHARANAS
   Dagar    - Dagar Vani  SEMI-CLASSICAL        Jaipur
   Darbhanga- Khandar +   Gwalior               Varanasi
              Gauhar      Kirana                Etawah
   Bettiah  - Gauhar +    Agra (Rangila)
              Khandar     Patiala
   Talwandi - Khandar     Bhendibazaar
                          Indore
   the fourth vani,       Jaipur
   NAUHAR, survives in    Benaras
   the Agra gharana's     Mewati
   recorded origin        Rampur-Sahaswan
                          Varanasi + Lucknow
                          (thumri)
                              |
   THE RECORDED ROLL OF "FAMOUS HINDUSTANI GHARANAS":
   Agra | Gwalior | Indore | Jaipur | Kirana | Patiala
                              |
   WHAT A GHARANA DOES, AS A STRUCTURE
   concentrates a scarce skill in a household
        -> ties that household to a patron
        -> reproduces the skill by descent or long apprenticeship
        -> supplies a recognisable brand of style
        -> survives the patron by moving to concert, radio, teaching, recording
   AND ITS FRAGILITY: Talwandi, nearly extinct after historical decline
                      and partition
```

The diagram makes one correction immediately. A gharana is not simply "a school of music". It is a lineage defined by **style and ideology** that shapes both **teaching** and **performance** - and it is named on three different principles, which is why a single alphabetical list of gharanas teaches nothing.

### 1. The definition, and what it commits you to

**A gharana is a musical lineage or school, based on style and ideology, shaping teaching and performance.**

Four words in that definition are load-bearing.

- **Lineage** - transmission is through persons standing in a line, not through an institution with an enrolment.
- **Style** - the distinguishing content is musical and specifiable: alaap weight, taan type, tuning precision, breath control, bandish emphasis.
- **Ideology** - what the lineage holds to be the right way to sing, which is why gharanas differ in judgement and not merely in habit.
- **Shaping teaching and performance** - one institution performs both functions, which is exactly what separates a gharana from a music college.

### 2. The ancestor: the gurukul, and how the gharana differs from it

Lesson 7 established the older system. The **gurukul**, also called the ashram or hermitage system, embodied the **guru-shishya tradition**; students lived with the master for **twelve years**, gaining knowledge by service; the hermitages received **patronage from kings and other wealthy persons**; and **all students, whether a prince or a commoner, were treated the same, with no discrimination**.

Set this beside the gharana and three differences appear that an answer on transmission should state.

| Axis | Gurukul | Gharana |
|---|---|---|
| Basis of entry | Residence and service; **prince and commoner treated alike** | Lineage, household and long apprenticeship; **descent is a normal route** |
| What is transmitted | Knowledge through direct experience, over a fixed term of twelve years | A **named style and ideology**, without a fixed term |
| Relation to patronage | Hermitages **received** royal and wealthy patronage | The household **is attached to** a patron and serves a court or elite audience |

The gurukul is an institution of education that received patronage. The gharana is a unit of skilled labour that served it. The difference is not a matter of degree.

### 3. The complete roll of named gharanas

Every gharana named anywhere in the owned material is collected here, with what is recorded about it. Where a gharana is named without detail, that is stated rather than filled in.

**Recorded as the famous Hindustani gharanas:** **Agra, Gwalior, Indore, Jaipur, Kirana and Patiala.**

| Gharana | Kind | What is recorded |
|---|---|---|
| **Dagar** | Dhrupad, vani-named | Delhi / UP; **Dagar Vani** linked to Gauhar; alaap emphasis, meditative, often sung in pairs; devotional texts though mainly Muslim singers; **Gundecha Brothers** and the **Dagar family** |
| **Darbhanga** | Dhrupad, vani-named | Bihar; **Khandar and Gauhar Vani**; equal focus on alaap and bandish; rich in **layakari**; **Ram Chatur Mallick, Pt. Siyaram Tewari**, the **Mallick family** |
| **Bettiah** | Dhrupad, vani-named | Bihar; **Gauhar and Khandar Vani**; **bandish as the core**, alaap given less importance; **Mishra family (Indra Kishore Mishra)** |
| **Talwandi** | Dhrupad, vani-named | Punjab, now in Pakistan; **Khandar Vani**; strong forceful style; **nearly extinct due to historical decline and partition** |
| **Gwalior** | Khayal, place-named | **Among the oldest khyal gharanas**; equal emphasis on melody and rhythm; simple ragas, *sapaat* taans; **Natthu Khan, Vishnu Digambar Paluskar**; also **Laxmanrao Pandit**, a tappa exponent |
| **Kirana** | Khayal, place-named | Kirana, UP; **founded by Nayak Gopal**, popularised by **Abdul Karim Khan and Abdul Wahid Khan** in the 20th century; precise tuning of swaras, slow tempo, **melody over rhythm**, clear pronunciation; **Pandit Bhimsen Joshi, Gangubai Hangal** |
| **Agra (Rangila)** | Khayal, place-named, vani-descended | Origin in **Nauhar Vani**; founded by **Haji Sujan Khan / Khudabuksh**, 19th century; **khyal blended with Dhrupad-Dhamar**, stress on bandish, lyrical; **Faiyyaz Khan, Vilayat Hussain Khan** |
| **Patiala** | Khayal and semi-classical | Punjab; founded by **Bade Fateh Ali Khan and Ali Baksh Jarnail Khan**, 19th century; **patronised by the Maharaja of Patiala**; **khyal, thumri and ghazal**; intricate taans, *gamak*, *tarana gayaki*; **Bade Ghulam Ali Khan** |
| **Bhendibazaar** | Khayal, place-named | Mumbai, 19th century; founded by **Chhajju Khan, Nazir Khan and Khadim Hussain Khan**; **breath control**, long passages in one breath; **includes Carnatic ragas**; **Khadim Hussain Khan** |
| **Benaras** | Vocal and instrumental, city-named | The Benaras gharana of Hindustani classical music, at **Varanasi**, which produced **Bismillah Khan, Sitara Devi, Girija Devi** |
| **Varanasi and Lucknow** | Thumri | **The main gharanas of thumri are based in Varanasi and Lucknow**; **Girija Devi and Chhannulal Mishra** of the **Purab Ang Thumri** |
| **Mewati** | Vocal | Named only, through **Pandit Rattan Mohan Sharma**, renowned tarana singer, titled **"Tarana ke Baadshah"** |
| **Rampur-Sahaswan** | Vocal | Named only, through **Shanno Khurana**, one of the very few remaining tappa exponents |
| **Indore** | Vocal | **Named in the roll of famous Hindustani gharanas; no further detail is recorded in the owned material and none is supplied here** |
| **Jaipur** | Vocal and instrumental | Named in the roll of famous gharanas; **Jaipur** also appears among the **sitar gharanas**. See section 5 |
| **Sitar gharanas** | Instrumental | **Jaipur, Varanasi, Etawah** - the complete recorded list; do not extend it |

### 4. The gharana as a patronage and labour structure

This is the analytical core of the lesson, and it is the argument that turns a memorised table into a 15-mark answer.

A gharana is best understood as a **historically specific response to courtly and later urban patronage structures**: it concentrates specialised technique within a family or lineage in order to preserve a marketable, transmissible skill. Four consequences follow, and each has evidence in the table above.

1. **Concentration.** A scarce skill is held inside a household rather than diffused. This is why the exponent lists are so often families - the Dagar family, the Mallick family, the Mishra family, the Gundecha Brothers.
2. **Attachment to a patron.** The Patiala gharana is recorded as patronised by the Maharaja of Patiala. A named patron for a named lineage is the clearest instance available.
3. **Differentiation.** Because several households compete for the same patronage, each must be distinguishable. That is why gharana descriptions read as a list of contrasts - melody over rhythm, equal emphasis on both, breath control, bandish emphasis - rather than as a list of repertoires. Style is, among other things, a mark of identity in a market.
4. **Survival by relocation.** When princely patronage contracted, the same lineages moved to urban concert, radio, teaching and recording. This is why gharana identity outlived the courts that created it - and why the gharana names of the nineteenth century are still the operative vocabulary of twentieth-century performance.

And the counter-evidence, which must be stated for the argument to be honest: **Talwandi is recorded as nearly extinct owing to historical decline and partition.** Relocation is possible, not guaranteed. A household-based institution can be destroyed by a political event in a way a documented, notated, institutionally taught repertoire cannot - which is precisely the case for the notation and school-building of Lesson 18.

⚠️ This patronage reading is an **analytical framing** of the recorded evidence, not a sentence quoted from a source. Present it as an argument, support it with the four evidenced consequences above, and it is safe.

### 5. Jaipur, and the one case that has to be handled carefully

**Jaipur** is recorded twice in the owned material - once in the roll of famous Hindustani gharanas, and once among the three sitar gharanas alongside Varanasi and Etawah. No further description of the vocal Jaipur gharana appears in the owned sources.

A bounded verification undertaken for this session establishes the standard fuller identification, which is stated here as verified external material rather than as owned evidence: the vocal gharana is known as the **Jaipur-Atrauli gharana**, founded by **Ustad Alladiya Khan**; and **Pandit Mallikarjun Mansur (1910-1992)**, a vocalist from the Dharwad district of **Karnataka**, is identified with it, having trained under **Manji Khan and Bhurji Khan**, sons of Alladiya Khan, after earlier training in the **Gwalior** manner. He is associated with the revival of rare and composite ragas.

Three disciplines govern the use of that paragraph.

- It is recorded here because a Hindustani vocalist of this standing being absent from every local source would be a genuine gap in the topic, not because a fuller list of gharanas is desirable.
- **No award, honour, recording or institutional office is stated for him**, because the bounded check was closed at the lineage question and this session does not carry musician honours that its own sources do not supply.
- The same discipline applies to the **Indore** gharana, which is named in the famous roll and nowhere described. **It is left undescribed.** The presence of a verified paragraph for one gharana does not license reconstruction of another.

A further pattern worth noticing: Mansur's training runs **Carnatic first, then Gwalior, then Jaipur-Atrauli**, and he is a Kannada-speaking musician of the Hindustani tradition. Gharana affiliation is a matter of **training lineage**, not of birthplace, mother tongue or region of residence. An objective item that pairs a musician's home state with a gharana is testing exactly this, and region is the wrong inference.

### 6. What the Carnatic tradition does instead

The Carnatic tradition transmits through **guru-shishya parampara** and is **less rigidly gharana-labelled**. The functional equivalent is not a household brand but a **composer-centred repertoire** and a **sampradaya** of teaching - which is why the Carnatic canon is organised around named composers, as Lesson 14 shows, while the Hindustani canon is organised around named lineages.

State the contrast as a difference of **organising principle**, not as a presence and absence:

```text
   HINDUSTANI                              CARNATIC
   the lineage is the unit                 the composer's repertoire is the unit
   a singer is placed by gharana           a singer is placed by the compositions
                                           and the teaching line
   style is the inheritance                the kriti canon is the inheritance
   the institution that replaced the       the institution that replaced the
   court was the gharana's move into       court was the sabha, the festival and
   concert, radio and recording            the concert society
```

### 7. Attribution discipline for this lesson

Gharana material is exactly where unsupported claims enter an answer. Three rules:

- **Do not attach a musician to a gharana the sources do not attach them to.** Pandit Ravi Shankar is given **no gharana** in the owned material; supply none.
- **Do not cross the two most confusable pairs.** **Bhimsen Joshi is Kirana; Bismillah Khan is associated with Benaras.** **Gwalior is among the oldest khayal gharanas; Dagar is the dhrupad vani lineage.**
- **Do not extend a recorded list.** The sitar gharanas are **Jaipur, Varanasi and Etawah** - three, and no more.

### Examination use

Objective-ready: the gharana definition; the roll of six famous gharanas; every founder, region and exponent in the table; the three sitar gharanas; the Benaras trio; and the vani-versus-place naming distinction. For Mains, this lesson supplies the single strongest analytical argument in the topic - the gharana as a patronage and labour structure - together with its own counter-evidence in Talwandi. A "discuss the gharana system" demand is failed by answering "a gharana is a school of music" and is passed by naming the mechanism, evidencing it four ways, and then qualifying it.

### Traps introduced here

- Defining a gharana as a school of music, which loses style, ideology and the teaching-and-performance function.
- Treating the gharana as a purely artistic phenomenon, when it also functioned as a family-based labour and patronage structure.
- Assuming gharana affiliation follows region of birth; Mansur is a Karnataka-born Hindustani vocalist of the Jaipur-Atrauli lineage.
- Adding a fourth sitar gharana, or describing the Indore gharana from memory.
- Applying the gharana label symmetrically to Carnatic music.
- Assuming every lineage survived the end of courtly patronage; Talwandi did not.

### Revision notes

1. **Gharana** = musical lineage or school, based on **style and ideology**, shaping **teaching and performance**.
2. **Famous Hindustani gharanas: Agra, Gwalior, Indore, Jaipur, Kirana, Patiala.**
3. Three naming principles: **vani** (dhrupad), **place** (khayal and semi-classical), **instrument** (sitar gharanas).
4. Dhrupad vani lineages: **Dagar, Darbhanga, Bettiah, Talwandi**; the fourth vani, **Nauhar**, survives in Agra's recorded origin.
5. Khayal gharanas with founders: **Kirana - Nayak Gopal; Agra - Haji Sujan Khan / Khudabuksh; Patiala - Bade Fateh Ali Khan and Ali Baksh Jarnail Khan; Bhendibazaar - Chhajju Khan, Nazir Khan, Khadim Hussain Khan.**
6. **Benaras gharana - Bismillah Khan, Sitara Devi, Girija Devi.** **Thumri gharanas - Varanasi and Lucknow.**
7. Named-only gharanas: **Mewati** (Rattan Mohan Sharma), **Rampur-Sahaswan** (Shanno Khurana), **Indore** (no detail recorded).
8. **Sitar gharanas: Jaipur, Varanasi, Etawah** - the complete list.
9. Gurukul versus gharana: entry by residence and service with no discrimination, against entry by lineage and household; fixed twelve-year term against no fixed term; patronage received against patronage served.
10. Gharana mechanism: **concentrate a scarce skill -> attach to a patron -> differentiate to compete -> relocate to concert, radio, teaching and recording**.
11. Counter-evidence: **Talwandi nearly extinct after historical decline and partition.**
12. **Jaipur-Atrauli**, founded by **Alladiya Khan**; **Mallikarjun Mansur (1910-1992)** of Dharwad, Karnataka, trained under **Manji Khan and Bhurji Khan** after earlier Gwalior training. No honours are stated.
13. Carnatic contrast: **guru-shishya parampara**, less rigidly gharana-labelled; the **composer's repertoire** is the organising unit.
14. Attribution rules: no gharana for Ravi Shankar; **Bhimsen Joshi = Kirana, Bismillah Khan = Benaras**; do not extend recorded lists.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain the gharana as a mechanism rather than as a label, and state the one piece of evidence in this topic that limits the mechanism's explanatory power.

**Model answer:** As a mechanism, a gharana does four things. It **concentrates** a scarce musical skill inside a household, which is why the exponent lists are so often families - the Dagar family, the Mallick family, the Mishra family. It **attaches** that household to a patron, as the Patiala gharana's recorded patronage by the Maharaja of Patiala shows. It **differentiates** the household from its competitors, which is why gharana descriptions are lists of contrasts - Gwalior's equal emphasis on melody and rhythm against Kirana's melody over rhythm and precise tuning, Bhendibazaar's breath control, Agra's stress on bandish - since a distinguishable style is a mark of identity in a competitive patronage market. And it **relocates**: when princely patronage contracted, the same lineages moved into urban concert, radio, teaching and recording, which is why nineteenth-century gharana names remain the operative vocabulary of twentieth-century performance. The limiting evidence is the **Talwandi gharana**, recorded as nearly extinct owing to historical decline and partition. Relocation is a possibility the mechanism permits, not an outcome it guarantees. A lineage held in households can be destroyed by a political rupture, which is exactly the vulnerability that notation, published pedagogy and institutional teaching were later designed to reduce.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the gharana as a purely aesthetic phenomenon in which each household simply preferred a different sound. Style differences are real, but they are also functional: in a market where several skilled households competed for the same patrons, being recognisably different was part of how a lineage survived.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The gharana system shows that Indian classical music was reproduced by social organisation as much as by musical theory." Critically examine. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The statement is strong and largely defensible, provided the role of theory is not understated.

The case for social organisation is direct. A gharana is defined as a musical lineage or school based on style and ideology, shaping teaching and performance - one unit performing both functions that a modern system splits between a conservatory and a concert circuit. Its reproduction was by household: the Dagar, Mallick and Mishra families and the Gundecha Brothers are named as exponents of their own lineages. Its attachment was to patrons, the Maharaja of Patiala being the explicit instance. Its differentiation was competitive: Gwalior's balance of melody and rhythm, Kirana's precise tuning and melody over rhythm, Agra's bandish emphasis and dhrupad-dhamar blend, Bhendibazaar's breath control and Carnatic ragas. And when courtly patronage contracted the same lineages moved into concert, radio, teaching and recording, which is why the gharana vocabulary survived its original economic base.

The case against overstating it is equally evidenced. The dhrupad lineages are named by *vani* - Dagar, Khandar, Gauhar, Nauhar - which is a category of musical delivery, not of kinship, and the same vani appears across several households. Theory was also transmitted in writing: Bhatkhande's ten-thaat classification and modern pedagogy made systematic transmission possible outside any lineage.

And the system's fragility is recorded: Talwandi is nearly extinct after historical decline and partition.

The verdict: social organisation carried the music, theory made it teachable, and only institutional documentation made it survivable independently of both.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the definition used analytically rather than quoted 2 + household reproduction evidenced by at least three named families 4 + patronage attachment with a named patron 3 + competitive differentiation with four gharanas contrasted on style 4 + the counter-case from vani classification and written theory 4 + Talwandi as recorded fragility and a three-part verdict 3.**

## Lesson 12 - Carnatic music: the kriti, the anatomy of a composition, and the order of a concert

Progress: 12/19 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Carnatic emphasis on vocal music and sahitya, the kriti, the three-part composition anatomy, varnam, padam, javali and tillana, ragam-tanam-pallavi, manodharma and the concert sequence

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the Carnatic music section with its language, kriti and sahitya statements, the pallavi-anupallavi-charana anatomy, the other-forms list, the concert sequence and the swara-kalpana, tanam and ragam definitions
CA search: "Not required - repertoire lesson"
CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: a Carnatic concert, drawn as a sequence

```text
   THE FRAME
   Mainly associated with SOUTH INDIA | main emphasis on VOCAL music -
   most compositions are written to be sung | languages: TELUGU, KANNADA,
   TAMIL or SANSKRIT | KRITI-BASED, focusing on SAHITYA - the quality of the
   lyrics | based, like Hindustani music, on two elements: RAGA and TALA
                                   |
   THE ANATOMY OF ANY CARNATIC COMPOSITION
   +---------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | PALLAVI      first or second thematic lines; often repeated in each  |
   |              stanza; the "Piece de Resistance" or best part of the   |
   |              Carnatic composition called RAGAM THANAM PALLAVI, where |
   |              the artist has great scope for improvisation           |
   | ANU PALLAVI  two lines following the Pallavi; sung at the beginning  |
   |              and sometimes towards the end; NOT necessary to repeat  |
   |              after every stanza or Charanam                          |
   | CHARANA      the final and longest verse, which concludes the song   |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------------+
                                   |
   THE CONCERT ORDER
   [1] VARNAM        used for beginners and as the concert OPENER
        |
   [2] ALAPANA       raga improvisation
        |
   [3] NERAVAL       improvisation on ONE LINE
        |
   [4] KALPANA SWARAS
        |
   [5] ... and ENDS with lighter pieces such as a TILLANA
                                   |
   IMPROVISATION VOCABULARY (manodharma)
   SWARA-KALPANA  improvised section performed WITH A DRUMMER at medium
                  and fast paces
   TANAM          melodic improvisation in FREE RHYTHM, WITH mridangam
   RAGAM          pieces which do NOT have a mridangam
   KRITI          "a highly evolved musical song set to a certain raga and
                  fixed tala or rhythmic cycle"
   OTHER FORMS    Varnam | Padam | Javalis | Tillanas
```

Three things are visible in the diagram that are hard to see in prose. The concert has a **designed shape** - technical opener, elaboration, improvisation, light close. The improvisation vocabulary is finely differentiated by **what accompanies it**. And the *pallavi* is both a section of a composition and the name of a major improvisatory form, which is a genuine ambiguity the tradition itself carries.

### 1. The frame

**Carnatic music is mainly associated with South India. The main emphasis is on vocal music, where most compositions are written to be sung. Most Carnatic compositions are either in Telugu, Kannada, Tamil or Sanskrit.**

**Carnatic music is *kriti* based and focuses more on the *sahitya*, or the quality of the lyrics of the musical piece.** **Like Hindustani music, Carnatic music is also based on two elements - raga and tala.**

That last sentence is the one to carry into a comparison answer. The shared elements are stated in the same breath as the differences, by the same source that describes the differences. There is no basis for treating the two traditions as unrelated systems.

**Rooted in the Bhakti movement, Carnatic music is largely devotional in nature and remains closely tied to temple and spiritual traditions of South India, unlike Hindustani music, which flourished under Mughal courts.** This is the patronage contrast that Lesson 14 develops fully; note here that it is stated as a contrast of **institutional setting**, not of musical quality.

### 2. The kriti

**The *Kriti* is a highly evolved musical song set to a certain raga and fixed tala or rhythmic cycle.**

Two words in that definition do work. "Highly evolved" places the kriti at the end of a development from simpler devotional song types, without dating the development. "Fixed tala" marks the kriti as a composed piece with a settled rhythmic frame, against which improvisation is measured.

Combine it with the *sahitya* emphasis and the form's character is clear: a Carnatic concert foregrounds **what the composition says** as well as how it is elaborated, which is why the Carnatic canon is organised around named composers rather than around performing lineages.

A boundary that must be respected: the related term **kirtana** or **sankirtana** is used for devotional songs such as Annamacharya's compositions in praise of Venkateswara, and the same word separately names Manipur's ritual singing, drumming and dancing by the Vaishnava community. The owned material does **not** supply a formal structural definition of kirtana, nor a dated evolutionary sequence from kirtana to kriti. The safe formulation is that the sources use **kirtana for the earlier congregational-devotional song type and kriti for the evolved concert form**, and nothing further is asserted.

### 3. The anatomy of a composition

**Any composition in the Carnatic style has several parts to it:**

- **Pallavi** - **the first or second thematic lines of the composition. This portion is often repeated in each stanza and is considered to be the "Piece de Resistance" or the best part of the Carnatic composition called "Ragam Thanam Pallavi", where the artist has great scope for improvisation.**
- **Anu Pallavi** - **two lines, called Anu Pallavi, follow the Pallavi or first line. It is sung at the beginning and sometimes also towards the end of the song, but it is not necessary to repeat it after every stanza or Charanam.**
- **Charana** - **this is the final and longest verse, which concludes the song.**

This three-part anatomy is the safest single thing to write about any Carnatic composition. It is also the part most often garbled, because the three names are similar; fix them by **position and length**: Pallavi first and short and repeated; Anu Pallavi second and exactly two lines and not always repeated; Charana last and longest.

### 4. The other named forms

**Besides the Kriti, Carnatic music also includes forms like *Varnam* - used for beginners and as concert openers - *Padam*, *Javalis*, and *Tillanas*.**

| Form | What is recorded |
|---|---|
| **Kriti** | The central, highly evolved composed song, set to a certain raga and a fixed tala |
| **Varnam** | **Used for beginners and as concert openers** |
| **Padam** | Named as a Carnatic form; **Kshetrayya (1600-1680)** composed **Padams and Keertanas**, and **his Padams are still sung in Bharatanatyam and Kuchipudi** |
| **Javali** | Named as a Carnatic form; no further description is recorded and none is supplied |
| **Tillana** | Named as a Carnatic form and as one of the **lighter pieces with which a concert ends** |
| **Ragam Thanam Pallavi** | The composition in which the Pallavi is the "Piece de Resistance" and the artist has **great scope for improvisation** |

The Varnam is the most informative entry, because its double function - a beginner's exercise **and** a concert opener - tells you what it is: a piece that displays the raga's essential movements compactly. A form that teaches and opens is doing the same job in both roles.

The Padam entry is the clearest music-to-dance transmission in the topic. A seventeenth-century travelling poet's compositions are still performed in two classical dance repertoires. The dance forms are routed; the transmission is owned here.

### 5. The concert order, and why order is better evidence than a list

**A typical concert begins with a Varnam, followed by Alapana (raga improvisation), Neraval (improvisation on one line), Kalpana swaras, and ends with lighter pieces such as a Tillana.**

Writing the **order** rather than a list of forms is a stronger answer, for three reasons. It shows the concert as a designed arc from technical display through elaboration to lightness. It supplies the definitions of *alapana* and *neraval* in passing - raga improvisation and improvisation on one line respectively. And it demonstrates that improvisation is not an interruption of the Carnatic concert but a scheduled majority of it.

### 6. Manodharma: the improvisation vocabulary, differentiated by accompaniment

**There are several other components of Carnatic music - for example, the *Swara-Kalpana*, which is an improvised section performed with a drummer in medium and fast paces.**

**Carnatic music is usually played with mridangam. The piece of melodic improvisation in free rhythm with mridangam is called *Tanam*. However, those pieces which do not have a mridangam are called *Ragam*.**

Read those definitions carefully, because they are unusually precise and are testable exactly as stated:

| Term | Defining condition |
|---|---|
| **Alapana** | Raga improvisation, in the concert sequence after the varnam |
| **Neraval** | Improvisation on **one line** |
| **Kalpana swaras / Swara-Kalpana** | Improvised section performed **with a drummer**, at **medium and fast** paces |
| **Tanam** | Melodic improvisation in **free rhythm**, **with** mridangam |
| **Ragam** | Pieces which **do not have** a mridangam |

The distinction between *Tanam* and *Ragam* turns on the **presence or absence of the drum**, which is the kind of exact discrimination close-option items are built on.

This vocabulary is also the correction to a claim met in Lesson 13. A tradition with five differentiated improvisation formats, occupying most of the concert's running time, is not a tradition with "less scope for improvisation". The improvisation is organised differently - by named format rather than by free elaboration within a form - and that is a difference of grammar, not of quantity.

### Examination use

Objective-ready: the four composition languages; kriti-based and sahitya-focused; the kriti definition; the three-part anatomy with Anu Pallavi's exact two lines; Varnam for beginners and as opener; Padam, Javali and Tillana as the other forms; the full concert order; and the five improvisation terms with their defining conditions, especially Tanam against Ragam. In a comparison answer, the concert order is the single most useful Carnatic item, because Hindustani practice has no equivalent fixed sequence to set beside it.

### Traps introduced here

- Confusing Pallavi, Anu Pallavi and Charana; fix them by position and length.
- Saying Carnatic music has little improvisation, when five named improvisation formats occupy most of a concert.
- Reversing Tanam and Ragam; **Tanam is with mridangam, Ragam is without**.
- Giving the kriti a fixed number of sections beyond the Pallavi, Anu Pallavi and Charana structure that is recorded.
- Asserting a formal definition of *kirtana*, or a dated evolution from kirtana to kriti.
- Treating the Manipuri **Sankirtana** as the same object as the Carnatic **sankirtana** repertoire; the word names two different things.

### Revision notes

1. Carnatic music: mainly **South India**; main emphasis on **vocal music**; compositions in **Telugu, Kannada, Tamil or Sanskrit**.
2. **Kriti-based**; focuses on **sahitya**, the quality of the lyrics; **based, like Hindustani music, on raga and tala**.
3. **Kriti** = a highly evolved musical song set to a certain raga and **fixed tala**.
4. **Pallavi** - first or second thematic lines, often repeated in each stanza; the "Piece de Resistance" in **Ragam Thanam Pallavi**, with great scope for improvisation.
5. **Anu Pallavi** - **two lines** following the Pallavi; sung at the beginning and sometimes towards the end; **not necessary to repeat after every Charanam**.
6. **Charana** - the **final and longest** verse, concluding the song.
7. Other forms: **Varnam** (beginners and concert openers), **Padam**, **Javalis**, **Tillanas**.
8. **Concert order: Varnam -> Alapana -> Neraval -> Kalpana swaras -> lighter pieces such as a Tillana.**
9. **Alapana** = raga improvisation; **Neraval** = improvisation on one line; **Swara-Kalpana** = improvised with a drummer at medium and fast paces.
10. **Tanam** = melodic improvisation in free rhythm **with** mridangam; **Ragam** = pieces **without** mridangam. Carnatic music is usually played with **mridangam**.
11. **Kshetrayya (1600-1680)**: Padams and Keertanas; **Padams still sung in Bharatanatyam and Kuchipudi**.
12. Rooted in the **Bhakti movement**; largely devotional; tied to **temple and spiritual traditions** of South India.
13. **Kirtana / sankirtana**: used for the earlier devotional song type; **no formal structural definition or dated kirtana-to-kriti sequence is asserted**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Reconstruct the order of a typical Carnatic concert, explain what each stage contributes, and say what the order itself demonstrates.

**Model answer:** The concert **begins with a Varnam**, which is also used for beginners - a compact piece that lays out the essential movements of the raga and functions as both a technical exercise and a display. It is **followed by Alapana**, raga improvisation, in which the raga is expounded without a fixed composition. **Neraval** follows, improvisation on **one line** of text, which narrows the field from the whole raga to a single melodic-textual unit. **Kalpana swaras** come next - the *Swara-Kalpana*, an improvised section performed with a drummer at medium and fast paces, which adds rhythmic display to melodic invention. The concert **ends with lighter pieces such as a Tillana**. Each stage narrows or lightens: from a composed display of the raga, to free elaboration of it, to elaboration bound to a single line, to rhythmically driven improvisation, to a light close. What the order demonstrates is that improvisation is **scheduled** in Carnatic practice rather than incidental to it - three of the five named stages are improvisatory, and they are differentiated by exactly what constrains them. A further differentiation reinforces the point: *Tanam* is melodic improvisation in free rhythm with mridangam, while pieces without mridangam are called *Ragam*, so even the accompaniment is a defining variable.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a fixed concert order means a fixed concert. The order fixes the **kinds** of musical activity and their sequence; what happens inside the alapana, the neraval and the kalpana swaras is created in performance.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Describe the structure of a Carnatic composition and the sequence of a Carnatic concert, and explain what each reveals about the tradition's priorities. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

Both structures reveal the same two priorities: the primacy of the text and the scheduling of improvisation.

The **composition** has three recorded parts. The *Pallavi* is the first or second thematic lines, often repeated in each stanza, and is described as the piece de resistance of the composition called Ragam Thanam Pallavi, where the artist has great scope for improvisation. The *Anu Pallavi* is exactly two lines following the Pallavi, sung at the beginning and sometimes towards the end, and not necessarily repeated after every Charanam. The *Charana* is the final and longest verse, concluding the song. The architecture is therefore text-led: the thematic lines are stated, expanded and concluded, and the raga is elaborated around them. That matches the tradition's stated character - kriti-based, focused on *sahitya*, the quality of the lyrics, with most compositions written to be sung.

The **concert** begins with a Varnam, used also for beginners and as an opener; proceeds through Alapana, raga improvisation; then Neraval, improvisation on one line; then Kalpana swaras; and ends with lighter pieces such as a Tillana. Three of the five stages are improvisatory, each constrained differently, and the improvisation vocabulary itself is precise - Swara-Kalpana performed with a drummer at medium and fast paces, Tanam in free rhythm with mridangam, and Ragam for pieces without mridangam.

The qualification is that the sequence is a convention of concert practice and does not constrain what is created inside each stage.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the three-part anatomy with Anu Pallavi's two-line specification and Charana as longest 5 + the text-primacy argument tied to kriti and sahitya 3 + the complete five-stage concert order 4 + the improvisation vocabulary with the Tanam-Ragam discrimination 2 + the convention-not-constraint qualification 1.**

## Lesson 13 - Comparing the two systems without inventing a binary

Progress: 13/19 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the recorded difference table, the two claims in it that must be corrected, the genuine axes of difference, the shared foundations, and the safe formulations for a comparison answer

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the recorded table, with the two rows that need handling marked

```text
   POINT OF DIFFERENCE   HINDUSTANI                  CARNATIC
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   Influence             Arab, Persian and Afghan    Indigenous          [!]
   Freedom               Scope for artists to        Less scope for
                         improvise, hence scope      improvisation       [!!]
                         for variations
   Sub-styles            Several sub-styles which    Only one particular
                         led to the emergence of     prescribed style of
                         "Gharanas"                  singing
   Need for instruments  Instruments equally         More emphasis on
                         important as vocals         vocal music
   Ragas                 Six major ragas             Two types - melakarta
                                                     or parent ragas (72)
                                                     and janya or child ragas
   Main emphasis on      Mainly based on Ragas       Mainly based on Tala
   Major instruments     Tabla, sarangi, sitar,      Veena, mridangam,
                         santoor                     mandolin
   Association to        North India                 Usually south India
   parts of India
   COMMONALITIES         FLUTE AND VIOLIN            FLUTE AND VIOLIN
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   [!]  = record it, but never convert it into an essence
   [!!] = record it, but it must be read with the manodharma evidence, which
          the same body of material supplies at length
```

Notice the last row before you read anything else. The table's own final entry records a **shared** item: flute and violin appear on both sides. A comparison table that ends by naming what the two traditions have in common is telling you how to use it.

### 1. The table, and what each row is worth

Every row above is directly examinable and should be learnt. Four of them are straightforward and carry no interpretive risk: **sub-styles and gharanas; the relative weight of instruments and voice; the major instruments; and the regional association.** Two rows are the highest-yield items in the table: **the Carnatic raga scheme, with seventy-two melakarta or parent ragas and janya or child ragas**, and the **commonality row** naming flute and violin.

One row needs a note of its own. The table records the Hindustani side as having **"six major ragas"**. Read alongside the ten-thaat classification of Lesson 5 and the *Raga-Ragini* system of Lesson 3 - **five or six main ragas, each linked to six raginis, together forming about thirty to thirty-six** - the "six major ragas" figure corresponds to the **Raga-Ragini** scheme, not to the modern thaat classification. It is a record of an older framework set against a Carnatic scheme that is current, which is why the row is not a like-for-like comparison. State the ten thaats and the seventy-two melakartas when a question asks for the classificatory systems; state the raga-ragini count only when it is explicitly the older scheme under discussion.

### 2. The first row that must be handled: influence

The table records Hindustani influence as **Arab, Persian and Afghan** and Carnatic influence as **indigenous**.

That is a record of contact history and can be stated. What it cannot become is an essence. Lesson 7 established the correction and it is repeated here because this table is where the error actually occurs:

- Dhrupad, the oldest named Hindustani form, is recorded as **of temple origin**, using **Sanskrit syllables**, mentioned in the ***Natyashastra***, and evolved from ***Prabandha*** and ***Dhruvapada***.
- Both traditions trace their roots to Bharata's ***Natyashastra*** and diverged in the **fourteenth century**.
- The thirteenth-century ***Sangeet Ratnakara*** is **regarded as a definitive text by both Hindustani and Carnatic music traditions**.
- Bhatkhande's northern thaat classification was **modelled on the Carnatic melakarta system**.
- The **Bhendibazaar gharana includes Carnatic ragas**, and named ragas outside the thaat system - **Kirvani, Nat Bhairav, Charukeshi, Madhuvanti, Ahir Bhairav** - are recorded as Carnatic-derived.
- **Swathi Thirunal Rama Varma (1813-1846)**, Maharaja of Travancore, was a **composer in both Hindustani and Carnatic styles**, and **Ibrahim Adil Shah II** of Bijapur was a **great patron of both traditions**.

Six pieces of evidence, all from the owned material, against a foreign-versus-pure reading. The safe formulation: *"The traditions record different histories of external contact - Arab, Persian and Afghan on the Hindustani side, indigenous development on the Carnatic - but both descend from a shared theoretical foundation, exchanged repertoire and classification, and were patronised by the same rulers in some cases."*

### 3. The second row that must be handled: freedom

The table records Carnatic music as having **less scope for improvisation** and **only one particular prescribed style of singing**.

The same body of material describes, at length, a Carnatic improvisation apparatus: **Alapana** as raga improvisation; **Neraval** as improvisation on one line; **Kalpana swaras**; **Swara-Kalpana** as an improvised section performed with a drummer at medium and fast paces; **Tanam** as melodic improvisation in free rhythm with mridangam; and the **Ragam Thanam Pallavi**, in which the artist has **great scope for improvisation** and whose Pallavi is the piece de resistance of the composition. Three of the five stages of a typical concert are improvisatory.

So the table's claim, taken as a claim about quantity of improvisation, is contradicted within the same material. Taken as a claim about **codification of style**, it is consistent: "only one particular prescribed style of singing" against Hindustani's several sub-styles and gharanas is a statement about how many recognised manners of singing exist, not about how much is created in performance.

The safe formulation: *"Carnatic practice is more codified in style - the sources record one prescribed manner of singing against the several Hindustani sub-styles that produced gharanas - but it is not less improvisatory; manodharma, through alapana, neraval, kalpana swara, tanam and ragam-tanam-pallavi, occupies the greater part of a Carnatic concert."*

### 4. The genuine axes of difference

Strip the two problematic rows of their overstatement and six real axes remain. These are what a comparison answer should be built on.

| Axis | Hindustani | Carnatic |
|---|---|---|
| **Parent-scale system** | **Ten thaats** (Bhatkhande); a thaat is **not a raga** and is **not sung** | **Seventy-two melakarta or Janaka ragas** (Venkatamakhin); each **sampurna**; **janya** ragas derived by omission or vakra movement |
| **Rhythmic method** | Named whole cycles - dadra, kaharba, rupak, ektal, jhaptal, **teen-tal (16 beats)**, chautal; about 30 known, 10-12 used | Generated from **laghu, dhrutam, anudhrutam**; **35 talas x 5 ghatis = 175**; **aditala** most prominent |
| **Improvisational grammar** | **Alap, taan, murki, alankar, layakari**; bada and chhota khyal as a slow-fast design | **Manodharma**: alapana, neraval, kalpana swara, tanam, ragam-tanam-pallavi; three of five concert stages |
| **Repertoire** | **Dhrupad, dhamar, hori, khayal, tappa, chaturang, ragasagar, tarana, sargam, thumri**, with ghazal semi-classical | **Kriti, varnam, padam, javali, tillana, ragam-tanam-pallavi** |
| **Transmission unit** | The **gharana** - lineage named by vani, place or instrument | **Guru-shishya parampara**; the **composer's repertoire** is the organising canon |
| **Institutional ecology** | North Indian temple, devotional, Sultanate, Mughal and regional-court networks; later urban concert, radio and recording | South Indian temple, court and later **sabha** networks; the composer's festival |

### 5. What is genuinely shared

A comparison answer that names only differences has answered half the question. The shared items, all recorded:

- **Raga and tala as the two elements** - stated for Carnatic music in the same sentence that says "like Hindustani music".
- **Swara, shruti and saptak** as the pitch vocabulary.
- **A common theoretical root** in Bharata's *Natyashastra*, with divergence dated to the fourteenth century.
- **The *Sangeet Ratnakara*** as a definitive text for both.
- **Flute and violin** as instruments common to both, recorded in the difference table's own final row.
- **Sampurna** as a shared technical term - the seven-note jaati category in the general theory and the defining requirement of a melakarta.
- **Devotional foundations** in both: dhrupad of temple origin and thumri inspired by the Bhakti movement on one side, Carnatic music rooted in the Bhakti movement on the other.

### 6. The three sentences a comparison answer should contain

1. **The thesis:** "The two traditions are two developments of a shared theory, separated in the fourteenth century, and they differ in classification system, rhythmic method, improvisational grammar, repertoire and transmission unit - not in the presence or absence of improvisation, and not as foreign against indigenous."
2. **The strongest single contrast:** "Hindustani classification selects ten parent scales that are not themselves sung, while Carnatic classification generates seventy-two parent ragas that are, and derives the rest from them by rule."
3. **The qualification:** "The shared elements are not residual: raga and tala, the pitch vocabulary, a definitive thirteenth-century text and two common instruments belong to both, and composers and patrons crossed between them."

### Examination use

This is the highest-probability descriptive demand in the topic, and it has an exact historical precedent in the reproduced Main Examination list, which asks for the important **similarities and differences** between the two styles. The word "similarities" is doing the work: a script that lists only differences has lost half the marks before it starts. The recorded difference table supplies the objective content; sections 4 and 5 supply the analytical content; and sections 2 and 3 supply the corrections that distinguish a careful answer from a coaching-note answer.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing "Carnatic music has no improvisation" on the authority of a single table row.
- Converting the influence row into "Hindustani is foreign, Carnatic is pure".
- Comparing ten thaats with seventy-two melakartas as rival counts of the same thing.
- Quoting "six major ragas" as the modern Hindustani classification; it corresponds to the older raga-ragini scheme.
- Answering a similarities-and-differences demand with differences alone.
- Forgetting the flute and violin commonality, which the difference table itself records.

### Revision notes

1. Recorded difference rows: influence; freedom; sub-styles; need for instruments; ragas; main emphasis; major instruments; regional association; **commonalities - flute and violin**.
2. **Major instruments: Hindustani - tabla, sarangi, sitar, santoor; Carnatic - veena, mridangam, mandolin.**
3. **Carnatic ragas: 72 melakarta or parent ragas and janya or child ragas.** Hindustani "six major ragas" corresponds to the older **raga-ragini** scheme.
4. Recorded emphasis: Hindustani **mainly based on ragas**; Carnatic **mainly based on tala**.
5. Correction one - **influence**: dhrupad is of temple origin with Sanskrit syllables; both traditions trace to the *Natyashastra* and diverged in the 14th century; *Sangeet Ratnakara* is definitive for both; thaat was modelled on melakarta; Bhendibazaar includes Carnatic ragas; Swathi Thirunal composed in both styles; Ibrahim Adil Shah II patronised both.
6. Correction two - **freedom**: manodharma is extensive; "less scope" is about **codification of style**, not quantity of improvisation.
7. Six genuine axes: parent-scale system, rhythmic method, improvisational grammar, repertoire, transmission unit, institutional ecology.
8. Shared: raga and tala; swara, shruti, saptak; *Natyashastra* root; *Sangeet Ratnakara*; **flute and violin**; *sampurna* as a shared term; devotional foundations on both sides.
9. Thesis sentence: two developments of a shared theory, separated in the 14th century, differing in system and grammar - **not** in the presence of improvisation and **not** as foreign against indigenous.

### Concept check

**Question:** A source table states that Carnatic music has "less scope for improvisation" and "only one particular prescribed style of singing". Explain how to use both statements accurately in an answer.

**Model answer:** The two statements should be separated, because only one of them survives contact with the rest of the evidence. "Only one particular prescribed style of singing" is defensible and should be used as recorded: it contrasts with the Hindustani side, where several sub-styles led to the emergence of gharanas, and it is a statement about how many recognised manners of singing the tradition maintains. It explains why Carnatic musicians are placed by teaching line and repertoire rather than by a household style-brand. "Less scope for improvisation" cannot be used as recorded, because the same body of material sets out an extensive improvisation apparatus: *Alapana* as raga improvisation, *Neraval* as improvisation on one line, *Kalpana swaras*, *Swara-Kalpana* performed with a drummer at medium and fast paces, *Tanam* as melodic improvisation in free rhythm with mridangam, and the *Ragam Thanam Pallavi*, in which the artist is said to have great scope for improvisation - with three of the five stages of a typical concert being improvisatory. The accurate formulation converts the second claim into the first: Carnatic practice is more codified in **style**, not less free in **performance**. Written that way the table is used, the contradiction is resolved rather than concealed, and the answer demonstrates exactly the discrimination that a comparison demand is testing.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating a printed comparison table as a set of independent facts. A table is a summary, and summaries compress. When one cell conflicts with the detailed treatment elsewhere in the same material, the detailed treatment is the better evidence and the conflict should be stated, not hidden.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Hindustani and Carnatic music are best described as two grammars of a single tradition rather than as two traditions." Critically examine. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The formulation is largely defensible, and testing it requires weighing what is shared against what genuinely separates.

The case for a single tradition is strong. Both are traced to Bharata's *Natyashastra* and are recorded as having diverged only in the fourteenth century, the names Carnatic and Hindustani appearing for the first time in Haripala's *Sangeeta Sudhakara*. Both rest on raga and tala - stated of Carnatic music in the same sentence that says "like Hindustani music" - and on the same pitch vocabulary of shruti, swara and saptak. Sarangadeva's thirteenth-century *Sangeet Ratnakara* is regarded as definitive by both. The northern thaat classification was modelled on the southern melakarta scheme. Flute and violin are recorded as common to both, and composers and patrons crossed between them - Swathi Thirunal composed in both styles, and Ibrahim Adil Shah II patronised both.

The case for two traditions rests on real differences of grammar: ten parent scales that are not sung against seventy-two parent ragas that are; named tala cycles against talas generated from laghu, dhrutam and anudhrutam to a total of one hundred and seventy-five; alap, taan and layakari against alapana, neraval, kalpana swara and tanam; dhrupad, khayal and thumri against kriti, varnam, padam and tillana; the gharana against guru-shishya parampara and a composer-centred canon.

Two recorded claims must be handled rather than repeated - the influence row, which is a history of contact and not an essence, and the "less scope for improvisation" row, which is about codification of style.

The verdict: one tradition, two grammars - provided "grammar" is understood to include classification, rhythm, improvisation format, repertoire and transmission.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the thesis stated as grammar rather than as identity or difference 2 + at least five shared items with exact detail 6 + at least four grammatical differences stated precisely 6 + explicit handling of the influence row and the improvisation row rather than repetition 4 + a verdict that defines what "grammar" covers 2.**

## Lesson 14 - Treatises, composers and patrons: who fixed the theory and who paid for the music
