### Visual: the two banks, and the question each answers

```text
   BANK ONE - WHO FIXED THE THEORY?              answer by CONTRIBUTION
   ~1000 BC  Samaveda (Vyasa) .......... scriptures set to music
   4c BCE-2c CE Dattilam (Dattila) ..... ritual chant -> gandharva music
   2c BC     Natyasastra (Bharata) ..... earliest literature about music in
                                          Sanskrit; Shruti, Swara, Jati,
                                          instruments; octave and 22 shrutis
   2c AD     Silappadikaram (Illango) .. Tamil; the three southern kingdoms;
                                          rich musical material
   6-8c      Brihaddesi (Matanga) ...... Nada, Shruti, Swara, Tana, Alankara,
                                          RAGA - defines the term
   9c        Sangita Makaranda (Narada)  ragas categorised male/female/neuter
   1080 CE   Sarasvati Hridyalankara
             (Nanyadeva) ............... theory of music
   12c       Abhjilashitarta Chintamani
             (Someshwara, Chalukyan king) singer, composer, auditorium, voice,
                                          Alapana, Gamaka
   12c       Gita Govinda (Jayadeva) ... Krishna-Radhika; ASHTA NAYIKA; states
                                          the exact raga and tala for each song
   1200 CE   Sangita Samayasara
             (Parsvadeva) .............. theory of GAMAKAS
   13c       Sangita Ratnakara
             (Sarngadeva) .............. "Ocean of Music and Dance"; 264 ragas;
                                          DEFINITIVE FOR BOTH TRADITIONS
   14c AD    Sangeeta Sudhakara
             (Haripala) ................ the terms CARNATIC and HINDUSTANI
                                          appear for the FIRST TIME
   16-17c    Gita Prakasa (Krusnadasa
             Badajena Mahapatra) ....... major ODISSI treatise
   16c       Swaramela-Kalanidhi
             (Ramamatya) ............... deals with ragas
   17c       Sangita Darpana
             (Damodara Misra) .......... "Mirror of Music"; seven chapters
   17c       Sangita Narayana
             (Purusottama Misra) ....... ODISSI; raga, tala, performance
                                          practice; attributed to King Gajapati
                                          Narayana Deva
   17c       Chaturdandiprakashika
             (Venkatamakhin) ........... the MELAKARTA system
   18c       Sangraha Choodamani
             (Govindacharya) ........... the current system of Carnatic music

   BANK TWO - WHO PAID, AND WHO WAS PRAISED?     answer by ADDRESSEE
   HINDUSTANI: the addressee is a PATRON, the venue a COURT
   CARNATIC:   the addressee is a DEITY, the patron a TEMPLE or an EMPIRE
```

The two banks answer two different examination questions. A treatise question is answered by **contribution** - which text fixed which concept - never by a list of titles and authors. A patronage question is answered by **addressee** - who the music was sung to, which then explains who paid for it.

### 1. The treatise bank, with the four entries that matter most

The full bank is in the diagram. Four entries carry disproportionate weight and should be reproduced exactly.

| Text | Author and date | Why it is the highest-value entry |
|---|---|---|
| ***Natyasastra*** | **Bharata, 2nd century BC** | **The earliest literature about music written in Sanskrit**; covers **Shruti, Swara, Jati and various types of musical instruments**; identified the octave and the **22 shrutis** |
| ***Brihaddesi*** | **Matanga, 6th-8th centuries** | The Sanskrit work that **focused on defining the term *raga***; also explains **Nada, Shruti, Swara, Tana, Alankara** |
| ***Sangita Ratnakara*** | **Sarngadeva, 13th century** | **"Ocean of Music and Dance"**; **defined 264 ragas** and classified microtones; **regarded as a definitive text by both Hindustani and Carnatic traditions** |
| ***Sangeeta Sudhakara*** | **Haripala, 14th century AD** | **The terms Carnatic and Hindustani are found for the first time in this text** |

Four further entries are worth their own sentence because each is the only one of its kind in the bank:

- ***Silappadikaram*, Illango, 2nd century AD** - the only **Tamil** entry, a poem explaining the religious, social, political, cultural and economic set-ups of the three southern kingdoms, **Chera, Chola and Pandya**, with an endless stream of musical material. It is the earliest evidence in this bank for a southern musical culture described from inside.
- ***Gita Govinda*, Jayadeva, 12th century** - the only entry that is a **song text rather than a treatise**, describing the relationship between Krishna, Radhika and the gopikas of Vrindavana, elaborating the **eight moods of the heroine, the Ashta Nayika**, later frequently used in Indian classical dances - and, crucially for this topic, **indicating the exact raga and tala in which to sing each of the songs**. A twelfth-century text that prescribes raga and tala for each song is direct evidence that the raga-tala system was operational, not merely theoretical.
- ***Abhjilashitarta Chintamani*, Someshwara, Chalukyan king, 12th century** - a treatise by a **reigning king**, covering the qualities of a singer, a composer, the auditorium, the voice, **Alapana** and **Gamaka**. The inclusion of the auditorium is notable: performance conditions were a subject of theory.
- **Two Odissi treatises** - ***Gita Prakasa***, Krusnadasa Badajena Mahapatra, 16th-17th century, which systematises ragas, talas and compositions used in Odissi music and preserves early Odissi forms; and ***Sangita Narayana***, Purusottama Misra, 17th century, attributed to **King Gajapati Narayana Deva**, dealing with raga, tala and performance practice, among the most important works in the Odissi music tradition. These two establish a **third regional classical tradition with its own textual apparatus**, which matters because the Hindustani-Carnatic pair is not the whole of India's codified music.

A recorded conflict that must be reported rather than resolved: the treatise bank attributes ***Sangita Ratnakara*** to **Sarngadeva in the thirteenth century**, while the patron bank records **Maharana Kumbha** as having written treatises on music called ***Sangeet Raja***, ***Sangeet Mimansa***, ***Sangeet Ratnakar*** and ***Shuda Prabandha***. The same title therefore appears against two different authors in two different centuries. Both entries are stated here; neither is corrected. In an answer, attribute *Sangita Ratnakara* to **Sarngadeva**, which is the attribution made in the dedicated treatise bank and supported by the text's recorded status in both traditions, and, if Kumbha's output is being listed, name **Sangeet Raja** and **Sangeet Mimansa**, which are not in dispute.

### 2. The composer and patron bank: the Hindustani side

| Name | Period / dynasty | Contribution and style | Patronage and legacy |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Jayadeva** | **12th century**, poet in King Lakshmanasena's court | Composed the ***Gita Govinda*** - highly musical, devotional lyrics sung in temples | **Patronised by the Sena dynasty** |
| **Amir Khusrau** | Delhi Sultanate, **1253-1325** | Recorded as **Father of Qawwali**; **introduced Ghazal, Tarana, Trivat**; **credited with the invention of the sitar** | Linked with the **Sufi tradition**; descendants became **Qawwals** (devotional) and **Kalawants** (secular) |
| **Shree Maharana Kumbha**, ruler of Mewar | **1417-1468** | Wrote a commentary on Jayadeva's *Gita Govinda* and an explanation on *Chandisatkam*; wrote music treatises - **Sangeet Raja, Sangeet Mimansa**, and the titles noted in section 1 | **Himself well versed in playing the veena** |
| **Swami Haridas** | **1478-1573** | Spiritual poet; **Dhrupad compositions** | **Patronised by Akbar**; **guru of Tansen and Baiju Bawra**; poems centred on **Radha** |
| **Mian Tansen** (Ramtanu Pandey) | **16th century**, Akbar's court, **Navratna** | **Dhrupad master**; **created several new ragas**; compositions mainly related to **Hindu gods and goddesses and his patrons** | Patronised **first by Raja Ramchandra of Rewa, later by Akbar**; works include ***Sangita Sara*** and ***Sri Ganesh Stotra***; **tomb at Gwalior**; the **title "Mian" was given to him by Akbar** |
| **Baiju Bawra** (Baijnath Mishra) | **15th-16th century** | **Dhrupad musician** | **Pupil of Swami Haridas**; **court musician of Man Singh Tomar of Gwalior**; **samadhi at Chanderi** |
| **Raja Man Singh Tomar** | **1486-1516**, Tomar dynasty, Gwalior | **Patron of Dhrupad** | **Compiled the *Mankautuhal*** (music treatise); **advanced Gwalior as a centre of music** |
| **Naubat Khan** (Ali Khan Karori) | Akbar's court | **Rudra veena maestro**; **Mansabdar** | **Son-in-law of Tansen**; depicted in Mughal paintings |
| **Ibrahim Adil Shah II**, Bijapur | **1580-1627** | Wrote the ***Kitab-i-Nauras***, a collection of songs | Called **Sangeet Samrat**; **great patron of both Hindustani and Carnatic traditions** |
| **Lal Khan Gunsamundra** | Shah Jahan's court, **1637** | **Dhrupad musician** | Conferred the title **"Gunsamundra" by Shah Jahan** |
| **Sadarang** (Nyamat Khan) | **1670-1748** | **Khyal composer** | **Court musician of Muhammad Shah**; **shaped Khyal music** |
| **Pandit Bhimsen Joshi** | **1922-2011** | **Khyal exponent, Kirana Gharana** | **Bharat Ratna (2008)**; famous for **"Mile Sur Mera Tumhara"** |
| **Ustad Bismillah Khan** | **1916-2006** | **Shehnai maestro** | **Bharat Ratna (2001)**; **popularised the shehnai in classical and national events** |
| **Pandit Ravi Shankar** | **1920-2012** | **Sitar maestro** | **Globalised Indian classical music; collaborated internationally; Bharat Ratna (1999)**. **No gharana is recorded for him; supply none** |

### 3. The composer and patron bank: the Carnatic side

| Name | Dates | Contribution and form | Patronage and legacy |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Annamacharya** | **1408-1503** | **First known Carnatic composer**; **Sankirtanas in praise of Lord Venkateswara** | **Patronised by Vijayanagara rulers**; remembered as the **"Grandfather of Telugu songwriting"** |
| **Purandara Dasa** | **1484-1564** | **Founding father of Carnatic music**; composed **Dasa Sahitya**; separately recorded as the **Pitamaha**, the father or grandfather of Carnatic music | **Patronised by the Vijayanagara Empire**; revered as **"Pitamaha of Carnatic Music"**; a **devotee of Lord Krishna** |
| **Kshetrayya** | **1600-1680** | Composer of **Padams and Keertanas** | **Travelling poet**; **Padams still sung in Bharatanatyam and Kuchipudi** |
| **Bhadrachala Ramadasu** | **1620-1680** | **Vaggeyakara**; **Telugu devotional compositions for Lord Rama** | **Imprisoned by the Golconda Sultan**; revered as a saint-composer |
| **Venkatamakhin** | **17th century** | **Musicologist**; introduced the **Melakarta system**; authored the ***Chaturdandiprakashika*** | **Composed 24 Ashtapadis for the deity Tyagesha at Tiruvarur** |
| **Swathi Thirunal Rama Varma** | **1813-1846**, Maharaja of **Travancore** | **Composer in both Hindustani and Carnatic styles** | **Patron of Vadivelu of the Thanjavur Quartet**; **his death was mourned by the Royal Asiatic Society of Great Britain** |
| **Thanjavur Quartet** - **Chinnaiah, Ponniah, Sivanandam, Vadivelu** | **19th century** | **Codified the Bharatanatyam repertoire** | **Court musicians of the Thanjavur Maratha court**; **Vadivelu, the youngest, later served at Travancore under Swathi Thirunal** |
| **M.S. Subbulakshmi** | **1916-2004** | **Carnatic vocalist** | **Bharat Ratna (1998)**; **global ambassador of Carnatic music** |

**One correction the table carries, stated openly.** ⚠️ The standing account compresses the Quartet's patronage: it presents all four brothers as court musicians of Swathi Thirunal. The accurate distinction is narrower. The four brothers were **associated with the Thanjavur Maratha court**, where they debuted and were appointed court musicians, and their compositions are dedicated to the **Maratha rulers of Thanjavur**. **Vadivelu, the youngest brother, later served at Travancore under Swathi Thirunal**, who honoured him. Swathi Thirunal is therefore a patron of **Vadivelu**, not the employer of the group. Write it that way: the Quartet belongs to Thanjavur, one brother belongs additionally to Travancore. Nothing further is added about any brother, and no other court, composition or honour is attributed here.

**Vaggeyakara** - a term worth its own line - **is a musician and poet who composes lyrics as well as sets them to music.** The existence of the term tells you something structural about the Carnatic canon: the composer of the words and the composer of the music are expected to be the same person, which is why the tradition is organised around named composers rather than around performing lineages.

**The Trinity and its festival.** **Saint Tyagaraja, along with Muthuswami Dikshitar and Syama Sastri, comprise the Trinity of Carnatic music.** **The Tyagaraja Aradhana, held mainly at Thiruvaiyaru in Tamil Nadu, commemorates the saint-composer Tyagaraja's samadhi and features leading Carnatic musicians paying tribute.**

Three prohibitions attach to the Trinity and must be observed. **No birth or death years are recorded for Tyagaraja, Muthuswami Dikshitar or Syama Sastri** in the owned material, and none may be supplied. **No composition, raga or language preference may be attributed to Dikshitar or Syama Sastri**, who are named only as the other two members of the Trinity. And **Tyagaraja and Annamacharya must not be made contemporaries**: Annamacharya is dated **1408-1503**, and the Trinity belongs to a much later period. This last point is not a general caution; it is the exact discrimination an examiner has already tested.

### 4. The patronage argument, stated comparatively

This is the analytical payoff. The two banks differ systematically, and the difference can be argued from the evidence in them.

| Axis | Carnatic evidence | Hindustani evidence | The analytical point |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Primary patron type** | **Temple and empire**: Annamacharya and Purandara Dasa under **Vijayanagara**; Venkatamakhin composing for the deity **Tyagesha at Tiruvarur**; Bhadrachala Ramadasu's Rama compositions | **Court**: Tansen under **Akbar**, earlier under **Raja Ramchandra of Rewa**; **Sadarang** shaping khyal for **Muhammad Shah**; **Lal Khan** titled by **Shah Jahan**; **Naubat Khan** a mansabdar | Carnatic music is recorded as remaining tied to **temple and spiritual traditions** of South India, unlike Hindustani music, which flourished under Mughal courts |
| **Ruler as author** | **Swathi Thirunal**, a Maharaja who was himself a composer in both styles | **Maharana Kumbha**, who wrote music treatises and played the veena; **Raja Man Singh Tomar**, who compiled the *Mankautuhal*; **Ibrahim Adil Shah II**, who wrote the *Kitab-i-Nauras* and was called **Sangeet Samrat**; **Someshwara**, a Chalukyan king who wrote a music treatise | Rulers on both sides **authored** as well as funded. Patronage was not only money, and this row is the best evidence against a simple patron-and-client model |
| **Institutional afterlife** | The **Tyagaraja Aradhana** at Thiruvaiyaru - a **saint's samadhi** becoming an annual professional gathering | The **gharana** - a household concentrating a scarce skill, surviving the courts through concert, radio, teaching and recording | Both traditions replaced vanished patrons with a **new institution**. The Carnatic replacement is a **festival tied to a saint**; the Hindustani replacement is a **lineage tied to a household** |
| **Who is addressed** | The **deity**: Venkateswara, Krishna, Rama, Tyagesha | The **patron** and the divine together: Tansen's compositions are recorded as related to **Hindu gods and goddesses and his patrons**; khyal themes praise **God or a particular king** | The difference of addressee is the deepest one, and the musical differences - kriti and sahitya against khayal and gharana idiom - follow from it |

The verdict sentence for a comparison-through-patronage demand: *"The two traditions are best compared through who paid and who was praised. Carnatic composition grew where the addressee was a deity and the patron a temple or an empire, producing a saint-composer canon still organised around Tyagaraja's samadhi; Hindustani composition grew where the addressee was a patron as well as a god and the venue a court, producing lineages that outlived the courts by moving into concert and recording."*

### 5. The Aurangzeb correction

A specific historical claim recurs in weak answers - that Aurangzeb banned music - and the owned evidence corrects it precisely.

**Singing was removed from the imperial court, but instrumental music continued; royal women and nobles patronised music; Aurangzeb himself played the veena; and substantial music literature was produced. Sadarang and Adarang later strengthened khayal under Muhammad Shah.**

Four separate points, and each defeats a different version of the claim: the restriction was **on court singing**, not on music; **instrumental practice** continued; **other patrons within the same household and elite** continued; and **textual production** continued. The general principle, which is transferable to every patronage question in this topic: **the loss of one patronage node redirects a tradition rather than extinguishing it.**

### 6. Attribution and date discipline for this lesson

- **Amir Khusrau** is recorded as Father of Qawwali, as having introduced ghazal, tarana and trivat, as credited with the invention of the sitar, and as believed to have invented a three-stringed instrument called the *Sehtar*. The same material records that **his credit as originator of qawwali has been widely disputed**. Write "credited with" throughout.
- **Tansen** is recorded as having **created several new ragas**; the canonical caution is not to convert this into a claim that he **invented** the raga repertoire.
- **Invention dates for the tabla and the sitar remain uncertain**; supply none.
- **Ravi Shankar** is given **no gharana**; supply none.
- The **four Bharat Ratna years recorded** are M.S. Subbulakshmi **1998**, Ravi Shankar **1999**, Bismillah Khan **2001**, Bhimsen Joshi **2008**. **No other honour, birthplace, guru, disciple or award is stated for any musician in this session.**

### Examination use

This lesson is the evidence reservoir for the whole topic. Objective-ready: every text-to-contribution pair; every composer's dates; Tansen's two patrons, his title, his works and his tomb; Baiju Bawra's guru, patron and samadhi; Man Singh Tomar's *Mankautuhal*; Ibrahim Adil Shah II's *Kitab-i-Nauras* and title; Sadarang's dates and patron; the Carnatic dates, especially Annamacharya's 1408-1503 and Purandara Dasa's 1484-1564; the Thanjavur Quartet's four names and their patron; and the four Bharat Ratna years. For Mains, section 4 is the engine of any patronage or comparison answer, and section 5 supplies the correction that distinguishes a researched script.

### Traps introduced here

- Listing treatises by title and author without stating what each fixed.
- Attributing the definition of *raga* to the *Natyashastra* rather than to *Brihaddesi*.
- Making Tyagaraja and Annamacharya contemporaries, or supplying dates for any member of the Trinity.
- Saying Aurangzeb banned music.
- Converting "created several new ragas" into "invented the ragas of Hindustani music".
- Attributing *Sangita Ratnakara* to Maharana Kumbha without noting that the dedicated treatise bank attributes it to Sarngadeva.
- Adding any award or honour to a musician beyond the four recorded Bharat Ratna years.

### Revision notes

1. **Natyasastra, Bharata, 2nd century BC** - earliest literature about music in Sanskrit; Shruti, Swara, Jati, instruments.
2. **Brihaddesi, Matanga, 6th-8th centuries** - defines **raga**; also Nada, Shruti, Swara, Tana, Alankara.
3. **Sangita Ratnakara, Sarngadeva, 13th century** - "Ocean of Music and Dance"; **264 ragas**; **definitive for both traditions**.
4. **Sangeeta Sudhakara, Haripala, 14th century AD** - **Carnatic and Hindustani first named**.
5. **Silappadikaram, Illango, 2nd century AD** - Tamil; **Chera, Chola, Pandya**.
6. **Gita Govinda, Jayadeva, 12th century** - **Ashta Nayika**; **states the exact raga and tala for each song**; Jayadeva patronised by the **Sena dynasty**.
7. Odissi treatises: **Gita Prakasa** (Krusnadasa Badajena Mahapatra, 16th-17th c.) and **Sangita Narayana** (Purusottama Misra, 17th c., attributed to King Gajapati Narayana Deva).
8. Others: **Dattilam** (ritual chant to gandharva music); **Sangita Makaranda**, Narada, 9th c. (ragas as male, female, neuter); **Sarasvati Hridyalankara**, Nanyadeva, 1080 CE; **Abhjilashitarta Chintamani**, Someshwara, 12th c. (singer, composer, auditorium, voice, Alapana, Gamaka); **Sangita Samayasara**, Parsvadeva, 1200 CE (**gamakas**); **Swaramela-Kalanidhi**, Ramamatya, 16th c.; **Sangita Darpana**, Damodara Misra, 17th c. (Mirror of Music, seven chapters); **Chaturdandiprakashika**, Venkatamakhin, 17th c. (**melakarta**); **Sangraha Choodamani**, Govindacharya, 18th c. (current Carnatic system).
9. **Tansen**: Navratna; dhrupad master; created several new ragas; patronised first by **Raja Ramchandra of Rewa**, then **Akbar**; works **Sangita Sara** and **Sri Ganesh Stotra**; **tomb at Gwalior**; title **"Mian" given by Akbar**.
10. **Swami Haridas (1478-1573)** - guru of **Tansen and Baiju Bawra**, patronised by Akbar; **Baiju Bawra** - court musician of **Man Singh Tomar**, samadhi at **Chanderi**.
11. **Man Singh Tomar (1486-1516)** - compiled the ***Mankautuhal***; advanced Gwalior as a centre of music. **Naubat Khan** - rudra veena maestro, mansabdar, son-in-law of Tansen. **Lal Khan** - titled **Gunsamundra** by Shah Jahan, 1637.
12. **Ibrahim Adil Shah II (1580-1627)** - ***Kitab-i-Nauras***; **Sangeet Samrat**; patron of **both** traditions.
13. **Sadarang (Nyamat Khan, 1670-1748)** - khyal composer; court musician of **Muhammad Shah**.
14. Carnatic dates: **Annamacharya 1408-1503**; **Purandara Dasa 1484-1564**; **Kshetrayya 1600-1680**; **Bhadrachala Ramadasu 1620-1680**; **Swathi Thirunal 1813-1846**; **M.S. Subbulakshmi 1916-2004**.
15. **Vaggeyakara** = a musician and poet who composes the lyrics and sets them to music.
16. **Trinity of Carnatic music: Tyagaraja, Muthuswami Dikshitar, Syama Sastri**; **Tyagaraja Aradhana at Thiruvaiyaru, Tamil Nadu**, commemorating his samadhi. **No dates for any of the three.**
17. **Aurangzeb correction**: court singing removed, **instrumental music continued**, royal women and nobles patronised music, **Aurangzeb played the veena**, substantial music literature produced; **Sadarang and Adarang strengthened khayal under Muhammad Shah**.
18. Bharat Ratna years recorded: **Subbulakshmi 1998, Ravi Shankar 1999, Bismillah Khan 2001, Bhimsen Joshi 2008.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Using the composer and patron banks, argue the difference between the two classical traditions through the question "who was being addressed?", and state one piece of evidence that complicates the argument.

**Model answer:** On the Carnatic side, the addressee is consistently a **deity**. Annamacharya, dated 1408-1503 and described as the first known Carnatic composer, composed sankirtanas **in praise of Lord Venkateswara**. Purandara Dasa, 1484-1564, the founding father or Pitamaha of Carnatic music, was a **devotee of Lord Krishna**. Bhadrachala Ramadasu, 1620-1680, a *vaggeyakara*, wrote Telugu devotional compositions **for Lord Rama**. Venkatamakhin composed twenty-four Ashtapadis **for the deity Tyagesha at Tiruvarur**. The patron in each case is a temple or an empire - Vijayanagara for the first two - and the afterlife is a festival at a saint's samadhi, the Tyagaraja Aradhana at Thiruvaiyaru. On the Hindustani side, the addressee includes a **patron**. Tansen's compositions are recorded as mainly related to Hindu gods and goddesses **and his patrons**; khyal themes may praise God **or a particular king**; Lal Khan received a title from Shah Jahan; Sadarang was court musician to Muhammad Shah. The venue is a court and the afterlife is the gharana. The complicating evidence is **Swathi Thirunal Rama Varma, 1813-1846, Maharaja of Travancore**, who was a composer in **both Hindustani and Carnatic styles** and patron of the Thanjavur Quartet - a ruler-composer on the southern side who crosses the styles. **Ibrahim Adil Shah II**, a great patron of both traditions, complicates it from the other direction.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the addressee argument as a claim about religiosity - that Carnatic music is devotional and Hindustani music is not. Both are devotionally rooted: dhrupad is of temple origin and thumri was inspired by the Bhakti movement. The argument is about **who commissioned and received** the composition, which is an institutional question, not a spiritual one.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Indian music's history shows that the loss of one patronage node redirects a tradition rather than extinguishing it." Examine with evidence. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is well supported, with one important limit.

The clearest case is the seventeenth-century imperial court. Singing was removed from Aurangzeb's court, but the tradition was not extinguished: **instrumental music continued**, **royal women and nobles patronised music**, **Aurangzeb himself played the veena**, and **substantial music literature was produced**. Patronage moved within the same elite rather than disappearing. The redirection is then visible in what followed - under Muhammad Shah, **Sadarang (1670-1748)** and Adarang **strengthened khayal**, so the period of restricted court singing is immediately followed by the consolidation of the form that would dominate Hindustani music.

A second case is regional rather than imperial. **Hussain Shah of the Jaunpur Sultanate** gave khayal its most generous fifteenth-century patronage, and **Raja Man Singh Tomar (1486-1516)** advanced Gwalior as a centre of music and compiled the *Mankautuhal* - evidence that provincial courts absorbed what imperial patronage did not supply.

A third case is post-princely. Tappa, brought to Muhammad Shah's court from the folk songs of camel riders, survived afterwards in the **baithak khanas and jalsaghar of the zamindari elite**, while Hindustani lineages moved into concert, radio, teaching and recording.

The limit must be stated. Redirection is possible, not guaranteed. The **Talwandi gharana is recorded as nearly extinct owing to historical decline and partition**, and **tappa itself is now recorded as facing extinction**. Where no substitute patron exists, a household-borne tradition can be lost.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the Aurangzeb correction with all four of its components 5 + the Sadarang consolidation used as evidence of redirection rather than survival 3 + a second and third redirection case with named patrons or venues 4 + the Talwandi and tappa limit stated as a genuine counter-case 3.**

## Lesson 15 - Devotional and fusion music: where classical and folk meet

Progress: 15/19 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the fusion mechanism, bhajan, shabad and qawwali in detail, the five further fusion forms, and Rabindra Sangeet, Gana Sangeet and Haveli Sangeet

### Visual: the stated mechanism, and the forms it produces

```text
   THE RECORDED MECHANISM
   "Over time, classical music and folk music intermingle and other forms
    emerge, which take elements from both types. Usually, it takes DEVOTIONAL

    patronise the DEITIES, and hence the songs combine both genres."

        CLASSICAL                                   FOLK
        (royalty patronises)                  (commoners patronise)
              \                                     /
               \                                   /
                \         THE SHARED DEITY        /
                 +-------------------------------+
                                 |
                                 v
              ==================================
                    DEVOTIONAL FUSION FORMS
              ==================================
   BHAJAN        north India | Bhakti movement origin | lyrics set to simple
                 melodies, generally sung in one or more raga | stories of gods
                 and goddesses, Mahabharata, Ramayana | instruments: CHIMTA,
                 DHOLAK, DAFLI, MANJIRA | medieval expounders: Meerabai,
                 Tulsidas, Surdas, Kabir | modern: Anup Jalota, Anuradha Paudwal
   SHABAD        Sikhism | devotional songs to the gurus, sung in GURUDWARAS |
                 Guru Nanak and Mardana credited with its development and
                 popularity | three current types: raga-based, traditional as in
                 the Adi Granth, and lighter | the GURU GRANTH SAHIB is organised
                 BY CHAPTER IN RAGAS
   QAWWALI       praise of Allah, the Prophet Muhammad or a Sufi/Islamic saint |
                 composed of a SINGLE RAGA | Urdu, Punjabi or Hindi, with some
                 Brajbhasa and Awadhi | performed in SUFI SHRINES | solo or in
                 groups with TWO LEAD SINGERS and about EIGHT members | tabla,
                 dholak, harmonium | tempo BUILDS UP to induce a transcendental
                 state | Amir Khusrau credited as originator - WIDELY DISPUTED |
                 Sabri Brothers, Nusrat Fateh Ali Khan, Aziz Warsi
                                 |
   FIVE MORE FUSION FORMS              THREE "OTHER TYPES"
   Abhanga  - Maharashtra              Rabindra Sangeet - Bengal
   Bhatiali - West Bengal              Gana Sangeet - choral, patriotic, protest
   Tevaram  - Tamil Nadu               Haveli Sangeet - Rajasthan and Gujarat
   Kirtan   - West Bengal
   Sopana Sangeetham - Kerala
```

The mechanism statement at the top of the diagram is the most analytically useful sentence in the whole folk-and-devotional material, because it supplies a **reason** rather than a list: the deity is patronised by both royalty and commoners, so devotional song is where the two registers necessarily meet.

### 1. Bhajan

**These are amongst the most popular devotional singing types present in north India. They originated with the Bhakti movement, as the saints would take the message of God to the people orally - that is, through singing hymns.**

- **Musical description:** **the lyrics are set to simple melodies, generally sung in one or more raga.** A form that uses ragas but sets them to simple melodies is precisely the meeting point the mechanism predicts.
- **Subject matter:** **stories about the lives of gods and goddesses, or from the Mahabharata and the Ramayana, are popular subjects.**
- **Instruments:** **chimta, dholak, dafli and manjira.** Two of these - the **chimta**, literally fire tongs, and the **manjira** - are idiophones, and one is a membranophone; Lesson 17 supplies the families. The chimta is the clearest instance in the topic of an everyday object recruited as an instrument, which is itself evidence of the form's non-courtly base.
- **Expounders:** **the major expounders in the medieval period included Meerabai, Tulsidas, Surdas and Kabir.** **Currently, people gather in temples or have concerts at home to sing bhajans, and among the most famous bhajan singers of all time are Anup Jalota and Anuradha Paudwal.**

### 2. Shabad

**With the rise of Sikhism, many devotional songs dedicated to the gurus were sung in Gurudwaras. Historians suggest that Guru Nanak and his disciple Mardana were responsible for the development and popularity of Shabad.**

**Currently there are three types of Shabad singing: raga-based Shabad singing; traditional shabad as mentioned in the Adi Granth; and the lighter ones.**

And the structural fact that is the highest-value item here: **the holy text Guru Granth Sahib is organised by chapter in ragas, with each chapter containing many shabads of that raga.**

A scripture organised by raga rather than by subject, author or chronology is direct evidence that the raga system operated as a **general cultural framework**, not only as a concert apparatus. Pair this with the *Gita Govinda* indicating the exact raga and tala for each of its songs, and you have two texts from different traditions using raga as an organising principle for written devotional material. That pairing is worth a sentence in any answer about the reach of the raga system.

Note also that the attribution here is hedged in the source's own words - **"historians suggest"** - and the hedge should be reproduced.

### 3. Qawwali

- **Purpose:** **a kind of devotional music used in praise of Allah, the Prophet Muhammad, or any other major Sufi or Islamic saint.**
- **Musical structure:** **it is composed of a single raga.** This is a precise and examinable technical fact, and it contrasts with thumri's "mixed simple ragas".
- **Language:** **generally written in Urdu, Punjabi or Hindi; some words of Brajbhasa and Awadhi are also used.**
- **Venue:** **performed in Sufi shrines.**
- **Performing body:** **usually sung as a solo or in groups with two lead singers and a team comprising around eight members.**
- **Instruments:** **tabla, dholak and harmonium.**
- **Design:** **the intensity or tempo gradually builds up, seeking to induce a transcendental state.**

That last point is the form's defining feature and should be stated as a **design**, not as an effect: the tempo architecture is constructed for a purpose. Note the parallel with dhrupad's alap, where **the tempo gradually rises to evoke the raga's mood** - the same device serving two very different ends.

**Attribution:** **Amir Khusrau has been credited as the originator of Qawwali, but this has been widely disputed.** The source supplies its own caution; reproduce it. Separately, **it is believed that Amir Khusrau invented a musical instrument called the *Sehtar*, a three-stringed instrument** - another "it is believed" that must keep its hedge.

**Major Qawwals include the Sabri Brothers, Nusrat Fateh Ali Khan and Aziz Warsi.**

The Amir Khusrau entry of Lesson 14 adds a transmission detail that belongs here: his **descendants became Qawwals (devotional) and Kalawants (secular)**. A single lineage splitting into a devotional and a secular branch is a compact illustration of how the same musical training served two markets.

### 4. Five further fusion forms

| Name | State of origin | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| **Abhanga** | **Maharashtra** | In praise of the god **Vithobha**; songs composed and sung by **Tukaram, Namdev** and others |
| **Bhatiali** | **West Bengal** | About nature and daily life; **sung by boat drivers** |
| **Tevaram** | **Tamil Nadu** | Sung by the **Shaivite community**, such as the **Oduyars** |
| **Kirtan** | **West Bengal** | Involves **singing and dancing**; takes inspiration from the ***Gita Govinda*** |
| **Sopana Sangeetham** | **Kerala** | **Originated in temples**, with a religious theme |

Two observations. **Bhatiali** is the only entry whose defining feature is an **occupation** rather than a deity or a community - boat drivers - which places it closest to the folk end of the fusion band and next to tappa's camel riders. And **Kirtan** takes inspiration from the *Gita Govinda*, the twelfth-century text that prescribes raga and tala for its songs, so the chain from treatise to living devotional practice is documented rather than assumed.

### 5. The three "other types"

**Rabindra Sangeet.** **One of the most famous methods of composing music in Bengal, recreating the music produced by the Nobel Laureate Rabindranath Tagore.** **The music is a mixture of classical elements and Bengali folk strains** - the fusion mechanism again, this time through a single named composer rather than through devotion. **There are more than two thousand Rabindra Sangeet presently sung and performed by music lovers in Bengal.** **The themes range from worship of the one true God, devotion to nature and its beauty, love and a celebration of life**, and **one of the most prominent emotions in Rabindra Sangeet was also the strain of patriotism and placing the nation above one's own needs.**

**Gana Sangeet.** **A form of fusion music sung in choirs or groups and in large numbers. The most common form is songs reflecting patriotic feelings. They also include songs of protest against issues in society, and they generally try to carry a social message** - for example, urging people against the exploitation of women and children. **One of the most popular examples of Gana Sangeet is the Indian national song, Vande Mataram, which is sung in praise of the country.**

Gana Sangeet is the only form in this topic defined by a **social and political function**. It is therefore the natural evidence for any question that asks what music does in public life, and the pairing with Rabindra Sangeet's patriotic strain makes the point twice.

**Haveli Sangeet.** **This genre was developed mostly in Rajasthan and Gujarat but is now found in many parts of the country. It was originally meant to be sung in temples but can now be performed outside the temple. It is currently practised by a community known as the Pushtimarg Sampradaya - the community that believes in Pushtimarg as the way to achieve salvation.**

Note the movement recorded here: **from inside the temple to outside it**, while remaining the practice of a named devotional community. That is the same relocation of venue this topic keeps observing, and it is the reason the classical-folk-devotional boundaries are working distinctions rather than walls.

### 6. The boundary, stated once for this lesson

Everything in this lesson has a doctrinal dimension that belongs to the religion topic - Bhakti theology, Sufi practice, Sikh scripture, Pushtimarg soteriology. What is owned here is the **musical form**: its raga treatment, its instruments, its performing body, its venue and its tempo design. A bhajan is owned as a form with simple melodies set in one or more raga and accompanied by chimta, dholak, dafli and manjira; a qawwali is owned as a single-raga form for two lead singers and about eight performers, with tabla, dholak and harmonium and a rising tempo; a shabad is owned as a form whose scriptural corpus is organised by raga. The theology is routed.

### Examination use

Objective-ready: the fusion mechanism sentence; bhajan's Bhakti origin, its four instruments and its four medieval expounders; shabad's attribution to Guru Nanak and Mardana, the three current types and the raga organisation of the Guru Granth Sahib; qawwali's single raga, languages, shrine venue, two lead singers and roughly eight members, three instruments, rising tempo and three named qawwals; all five fusion forms with state and purpose; Rabindra Sangeet's more-than-two-thousand figure and its themes; Gana Sangeet with Vande Mataram; and Haveli Sangeet's two states and the Pushtimarg Sampradaya. In a descriptive answer, the mechanism sentence is the opening line of any "relationship between classical and folk music" demand.

### Traps introduced here

- Calling qawwali a multi-raga form; it is **composed of a single raga**.
- Presenting Amir Khusrau as the undisputed originator of qawwali, when the dispute is recorded in the same sentence.
- Treating the Guru Granth Sahib's raga organisation as incidental; it is the strongest evidence for the raga system's reach beyond concert music.
- Assigning the fusion forms to the wrong states - Abhanga is Maharashtra, Bhatiali and Kirtan are West Bengal, Tevaram is Tamil Nadu, Sopana Sangeetham is Kerala.
- Describing Gana Sangeet as devotional; it is defined by **patriotic, protest and social-message** content.
- Treating these forms as folk music; they are recorded as **fusions** of classical and folk.

### Revision notes

1. **Mechanism:** classical and folk intermingle over time; **devotional music brings them together** because both royalty and commoners patronise the deities.
2. **Bhajan**: north India; **Bhakti movement** origin; **simple melodies, generally in one or more raga**; subjects from the lives of gods and goddesses, Mahabharata and Ramayana; instruments **chimta, dholak, dafli, manjira**; medieval expounders **Meerabai, Tulsidas, Surdas, Kabir**; modern singers **Anup Jalota, Anuradha Paudwal**.
3. **Shabad**: Sikhism; sung in **Gurudwaras**; **Guru Nanak and Mardana** credited by historians with its development; **three types - raga-based, traditional as in the Adi Granth, lighter**; the **Guru Granth Sahib is organised by chapter in ragas**.
4. **Qawwali**: praise of **Allah, the Prophet Muhammad or a Sufi/Islamic saint**; **single raga**; **Urdu, Punjabi or Hindi** with Brajbhasa and Awadhi words; performed in **Sufi shrines**; solo or in groups with **two lead singers and about eight members**; **tabla, dholak, harmonium**; **tempo builds up to induce a transcendental state**.
5. Qawwali attribution: **Amir Khusrau credited as originator - widely disputed**; **Sehtar**, a three-stringed instrument, believed invented by him; his descendants became **Qawwals (devotional) and Kalawants (secular)**; major qawwals **Sabri Brothers, Nusrat Fateh Ali Khan, Aziz Warsi**.
6. Fusion forms: **Abhanga** - Maharashtra, Vithobha, Tukaram and Namdev; **Bhatiali** - West Bengal, nature and daily life, **boat drivers**; **Tevaram** - Tamil Nadu, Shaivite community, **Oduyars**; **Kirtan** - West Bengal, singing and dancing, inspired by the ***Gita Govinda***; **Sopana Sangeetham** - Kerala, originated in temples.
7. **Rabindra Sangeet**: Bengal; recreates Tagore's music; **mixture of classical elements and Bengali folk strains**; **more than 2,000** presently sung; themes of God, nature, love, celebration of life and **patriotism**.
8. **Gana Sangeet**: sung in **choirs or groups in large numbers**; **patriotic**, **protest** and **social-message** songs; example **Vande Mataram**.
9. **Haveli Sangeet**: developed mostly in **Rajasthan and Gujarat**; originally sung **in temples**, now performable outside; practised by the **Pushtimarg Sampradaya**.
10. Boundary: the form is owned here; the theology is routed to the religion topic.

### Concept check

**Question:** What does the raga organisation of the Guru Granth Sahib demonstrate about the Indian raga system, and what other evidence in this topic points the same way?

**Model answer:** It demonstrates that the raga system functioned as a **general cultural framework for organising and transmitting material**, not merely as a concert apparatus. The Guru Granth Sahib is recorded as organised **by chapter in ragas, with each chapter containing many shabads of that raga** - so the raga is the filing principle of a scripture, determining where a composition sits within a sacred text. A musical category is doing bibliographic and liturgical work. Two further pieces of evidence point the same way. Jayadeva's twelfth-century ***Gita Govinda*** does not merely contain songs: it **indicates the exact raga and tala in which to sing each of them**, which means raga and tala were prescriptive for a written devotional work four centuries before the classification systems of Bhatkhande and Venkatamakhin. And bhajan lyrics are recorded as **set to simple melodies, generally sung in one or more raga**, so even the most popular devotional register is described in raga terms. Taken together, a scripture organised by raga, a twelfth-century song text prescribing raga and tala, and a popular devotional form described in raga terms show that the system was the common musical language of Indian religious and literary culture.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that "devotional" means "musically simple and outside the system". The evidence runs the other way: the devotional repertoire is where the raga system is most explicitly documented, because devotional material was written down.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Devotional music is the point at which classical and folk traditions meet in India." Examine with examples. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The statement is well supported, and the reason is institutional rather than aesthetic.

The recorded mechanism is explicit: classical and folk music intermingle over time, and it is usually devotional music that brings them together, because **both royalty and commoners patronise the deities**, so the songs combine both genres. A shared addressee produces a shared repertoire.

The examples carry both registers at once. The bhajan sets its lyrics to **simple melodies, generally sung in one or more raga**, and is accompanied by the chimta - literally fire tongs - alongside dholak, dafli and manjira: raga vocabulary with household instruments. The shabad is sung in gurudwaras, yet its scriptural corpus, the Guru Granth Sahib, is **organised by chapter in ragas**. The qawwali is composed of a **single raga**, performed in Sufi shrines by two lead singers and about eight members. Sopana Sangeetham originated in temples; Haveli Sangeet moved from inside the temple to outside it.

The qualification is that the meeting is not only devotional: tappa entered the classical register from the folk songs of camel riders, and Rabindra Sangeet mixes classical elements with Bengali folk strains without a devotional intermediary.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the shared-addressee mechanism stated as the reason 3 + three devotional forms each showing classical and folk features together 4 + the Guru Granth Sahib's raga organisation as the strongest single evidence 1 + the qualification using tappa and Rabindra Sangeet as non-devotional meeting points 2.**

Organising the bank by **occasion** rather than by state is the single most useful decision in this lesson. Demands are set as "folk music associated with marriage", "songs of war and valour", "occupational songs" far more often than as "name the folk music of Manipur" - and a candidate who has the occasion columns can always recover the state, while a candidate who has only state pairs cannot recover the occasion.

### 1. The frame, and the non-hierarchical distinction

**Indian music reflects India's diversity, with classical bound by tradition and folk as the free voice of the people. Folk music is based on diverse themes and is full of musical rhythm. They are also set on beats so that they can be dance oriented. There are several notable folk music associated with each state.**

The distinction is one of **function and transmission**, not of value. Classical traditions are marked by codified theory, long apprenticeship and concert or temple settings; folk traditions by community transmission, occasion-bound performance - harvest, wedding, ritual - and regional language. An answer that ranks them has lost the analytical point, and an answer that calls folk music "less developed" has also contradicted the evidence, since the fusion lesson has already shown folk idiom entering the classical register from below.

Two structural observations follow from the frame itself. Folk music is described as **rhythm-led and dance-oriented**, which is why so many entries in the bank are dance-songs; and it is described as **thematically diverse**, which is why occasion is the natural organising key.

### 2. The four forms treated in detail

**Baul.** **This is not only a type of music but also a Bengali religious sect. The music of the Bauls, *Baul Sangeet*, is a particular type of folk song. Its lyrics carry influences of the Hindu Bhakti movements and the *Suphi*, a form of Sufi song exemplified by the songs of Kabir, and is called the *Baul Gaan* or Baul song.** **This music represents a long heritage of preaching mysticism through songs in West Bengal, Assam and Tripura. They mainly belong to either Vaishnava Hindu or Sufi Muslim sects.** **The prominent exponents include Yotin Das, Purno Chandra Das, Lalon Fakir, Naboni Das and Sanatan Das Thakur Baul.**

Baul is the most analytically valuable folk entry in the topic, because it is **simultaneously a sect and a genre**, and because its lyrics carry **both Bhakti and Sufi influence**. Any question that asks about syncretism in Indian music is answered with Baul first.

**Pandavani.** **This type of folk music is based on the grand epic Mahabharata, with Bhima as the hero. It includes *gayan* - singing - and *vadan* - playing an instrument - with the songs usually set to the rhythm of a *tambura*.** **One of the most well-known artists of this form is Teejanbai from Chhattisgarh.**

The two-part structure, *gayan* plus *vadan*, is the examinable technical content: a narrative folk form defined by the combination of singing and instrumental playing. On the honours: the core source records **Padma Shri (1987), Padma Bhushan (2003) and Padma Vibhushan (2019)** for Teejanbai, while the canonical owner records the official Padma Awards year for the Padma Shri as **1988** and logs the conflict explicitly. **Both years are reported here and neither is corrected**; if a year must be written, note the conflict or use the later awards, which are not in dispute.

**Panihari.** **This musical form is from the state of Rajasthan and is thematically related to water. The songs are generally about women fetching water from a nearby well and carrying it back to their households in *matkas* over their heads. The songs usually describe the scarcity of water and the long distance between the well and the village. Sometimes the songs also talk about the daily concerns of the village women who are clustered around the village well. The songs also occasionally concentrate on a chance encounter between lovers, hence they also can be romantic in nature. They also discuss the contentious relationship between the mother-in-law and daughter-in-law.**

Panihari is the best available evidence that folk music is a **record of material conditions**. Water scarcity, distance, labour, the well as a social space and household relations are all in the content. A question on what folk music tells us about society is answered from here.

**Lavani.** **This is one of the most famous folk dance forms from Maharashtra. It is also one of the most popular genres of music in Maharashtra. It is a combination of traditional dance and song that is performed usually to the beats of the *Dholki*, which is a percussion instrument. The music is unique, and it is also perfect for dancing as it has a powerful rhythm and beats.**

Lavani is the boundary case, and it should be handled explicitly rather than silently: it is named as **both** a dance form and a music genre. The dance is routed to the dance topic; what is owned here is the **song and its rhythmic accompaniment on the dholki**.

### 3. The complete regional bank

Every entry below is recorded with its state and theme. The occasion grouping is in the visual; this is the reference list.

| Form | State of origin | Major theme |
|---|---|---|
| **Wanawan** | Jammu and Kashmir | Wedding songs, auspicious |
| **Alha** | Madhya Pradesh, also Braj, Awadhi, Bhojpuri | Heroic ballads, Mahabharata link |
| **Ovi** | Maharashtra and Goa | Women's songs, marriage, lullabies |
| **Pai Songs** | Madhya Pradesh | Rain, harvest, festivals |
| **Maand** | Rajasthan | Courtly praise, Rajput rulers |
| **Powada** | Maharashtra | Heroic deeds, Shivaji |
| **Khongjom Parba** | Manipur | **Battle of Khongjom (1891)** |
| **Bhavageete** | Karnataka and Maharashtra | Love, nature, philosophy |
| **Mando** | Goa | Indo-Western blend, social themes |
| **Kolannalu / Kolattam** | Andhra Pradesh | Stick dance, community festivity |
| **Tamang Selo** | Sikkim | Love, sorrow, humour |
| **Sohar** | Bihar | **Sung during childbirth** |
| **Zikir** | Assam | Embodies the teaching of Islam |
| **Ja-jin-ja** | Arunachal Pradesh | Sung during marriages |
| **Nyioga** | Arunachal Pradesh | Sung at the **end** of the marriage ceremony |
| **Heliamleu** | Nagaland | Dancing songs |
| **Neuleu** | Nagaland | Songs about legends and myths |
| **Hereileu** | Nagaland | War songs |
| **Hekaileu** | Nagaland | Songs about oneself |
| **Dollu Kunitha** | Karnataka | Drum dance, named after the **dollu**, performed by the **Kuruba community** |
| **Nattupura Paattu** | Tamil Nadu | Village folk music and city folk music |
| **Pala and Daskathia** | Odisha | Ballads of a religious nature |
| **Manganiars** | North-west India | Songs about **Alexander**, local kings and battles |
| **Dhadi** | Punjab | Ballads about bravery |
| **Basant Geet** | Garhwal, Uttarakhand | Sung during the **Basant Panchami** festival |
| **Villu Pattu** | Tamil Nadu | Religious; conquest of good over evil |
| **Sukar ke Biah** | Bihar | Love between the cosmological deities **Shukra and Brihaspati** |
| **Saikuti Zai** | Mizoram | Praise of brave men, hunters |
| **Lai Haraoba Ishei** | Manipur | Sung by the **Meitei** people during the **Lai Haraoba** festival; related to **Sanamahism** |
| **Veeragase** | Karnataka | During the **Dusshera** procession |
| **Chhakri** | Kashmir | Fairy tales, love stories |
| **Bhuta song** | Kerala | Songs against evils and ghosts |
| **Khubakeshei** | Manipur | A song accompanied **entirely by clapping** |
| **Jhumair** | Eastern states | Popular among the **tea-tribes of Assam** |
| **Borgeet** | Assam | Composed by **Sankardeva and Madhavdeva** in the **15th-16th centuries**; associated with **Ekasarana Dharma** |
| **Jhoori** | Himachal Pradesh | Celebrates an extramarital romance |
| **Chai hla** | Mizoram | Songs of the **Chai** dance |
| **Laman** | Himachal Pradesh | Love songs from the **Kullu valley** |
| **Sankirtana** | Manipur | **Ritual singing, drumming and dancing**, mainly by the Vaishnava community |
| **Oi Nitom** | Assam | Love songs by the **Mising** tribal community |
| **Dihanaam** | Assam | Sung by women in praise of the Assamese saint **Sankardev** |
| **Pankhida** | Rajasthan | Sung by **farmers working in the fields** |
| **Ghasiyari Geet** | Uttarakhand | Sung by women going to forests; emphasis on the **importance of labour** |
| **Ammanaivari** | Tamil Nadu | Sung by women while playing with a ball; in praise of the **Chola monarch** |
| **Sana Lamok** | Manipur | Sung at a **coronation** by the **Maaiba** (priest), or when welcoming a new king |
| **Kajri** | Uttar Pradesh | Sung by women during the **rainy season** |
| **Hori** | Uttar Pradesh | Love pranks of **Radha-Krishna**; associated with the **Holi** festival |
| **Lotia** | Rajasthan | Sung in the month of **Chaitra**, when women bring *lotas* and *kalash* filled with water from ponds and wells |

Three high-yield items in this bank deserve separate notice.

- **Khongjom Parba** commemorates a **dated historical event**, the Battle of Khongjom of **1891**. It is the clearest case in the topic of folk music functioning as **historical memory**, and it is the answer to any question about music as a record of the past.
- **Khubakeshei** is **accompanied entirely by clapping** - a song with no instrument at all, which is a useful limiting case when instruments are under discussion.
- **Manganiars** appears twice in the owned material, once as a folk-music entry of north-west India with songs about Alexander, local kings and battles, and once as a **Muslim community of Rajasthan** that uses the **Kamaicha**. Both entries belong to the same object and should be used together.

### 4. The music-dance boundary, applied

Several entries in the bank are named as dances. The boundary rule is applied, not assumed:

| Entry | What is owned here | What is routed |
|---|---|---|
| **Lavani** | The song genre and its **dholki** accompaniment | The dance form |
| **Dollu Kunitha** | The song, its naming after the **dollu** drum, and the **Kuruba** community that performs it | The drum dance as choreography |
| **Kolannalu / Kolattam** | The community-festivity song | The stick dance |
| **Sankirtana** (Manipur) | The **ritual singing and drumming** by the Vaishnava community | The dancing component |
| **Chai hla**, **Heliamleu** | The songs | The Chai dance and the dancing occasions |
| **Thumri** (Lesson 10) | The vocal form | **Kathak**, to which it is linked |
| **Padam** (Lesson 12) | The Carnatic composition | **Bharatanatyam and Kuchipudi**, in which Kshetrayya's padams are sung |

The general statement: where music and dance are named in one form, this topic owns the **sound** - the song type, the metre, the accompanying instrument and the community - and the dance topic owns the **movement**. Both are named; neither is described into the other's territory.

### 5. The communities related to music

| Community | State | Details |
|---|---|---|
| **Langha** (Muslim tribal community) | **Gujarat and Rajasthan** | **Entertain Muslim Rajputs by praising them through songs** |
| **Manganiar or Merasi** (Muslim community) | **Rajasthan** | **Use an instrument, the *Kamaicha*, to accompany classical folk music and songs** |
| **Karta Bhajas** | West Bengal | **Four classes** which chant mantras leading to perfection, as per their belief |
| **Bauls** | West Bengal | **Mystic group of singers in the countryside; one of the classes of Kartabhaja** |
| **Jogi** | Rajasthan | Followers of the **Nath sect** |
| **Bhopa** | Rajasthan | A male artiste called **Bhopa** and a female artiste called **Bhopi**, singing **religious hymns** |
| **Barot, Charan, Gadhvi** | Gujarat | Mainly **storytelling**, with or without music |
| **Muria tribes** | Chhattisgarh | Sing songs called **Relo** |
| **Kamar community** | Chhattisgarh | **Marriage song** |
| **Baghela** | Madhya Pradesh | **Semi-historical songs devoted to the Hardaul deity** |
| **Pulayar hill tribes** | Tamil Nadu | Perform melodies called **talams** |
| **Idu Mishmis** | Arunachal Pradesh | Perform **ritual dances and fertility dances** |
| **Moken Tribe** | **Andaman and Nicobar** | **No religion; perform only ancestor worship; perform a ritual tribal dance** |
| **Bazigar (Goaar) community** | Punjab | Perform at festivals and other occasions, displaying **strength, balance and courage** |

Two analytical points. **Langha and Manganiar are hereditary professional performing communities attached to patron groups** - the Langha entertain Muslim Rajputs by praising them - which is the folk-register analogue of the gharana's attachment to a court. And the **Bauls are recorded as one of the classes of Kartabhaja**, which links the best-known folk genre in the topic to a named religious formation rather than leaving it as a free-floating mystical tradition.

### Examination use

Objective-ready: every state-theme pair in the bank; the four detailed forms with their technical content - *gayan* and *vadan*, the *tambura*, the *dholki*, *Baul Gaan*; Khongjom Parba's 1891 battle; Khubakeshei's clapping accompaniment; Borgeet's composers and century; Lai Haraoba Ishei's Meitei and Sanamahism link; and every community with its state and detail. For Mains, the occasion grouping converts a memory list into an argument about what folk music records - labour, water, birth, marriage, war, kingship and memory.

### Traps introduced here

- Ranking folk music below classical music, when the recorded distinction is of **function and transmission**.
- Attaching a form to the wrong state - the Nagaland quartet, the Manipur cluster and the Rajasthan cluster are the usual casualties.
- Forgetting that **Ja-jin-ja** is sung **during** marriages and **Nyioga** at the **end** of the ceremony.
- Treating the Manipuri **Sankirtana** as the Carnatic **sankirtana** repertoire of Annamacharya.
- Writing about Lavani or Dollu Kunitha as dance forms in a music answer without owning the song and the instrument.
- Using Teejanbai's Padma Shri year without noting the recorded conflict between 1987 and 1988.

### Revision notes

1. Frame: **classical bound by tradition, folk as the free voice of the people**; folk is **theme-diverse, rhythm-full, set on beats, dance-oriented**.
2. The distinction is of **function and transmission**, not of value.
3. **Baul**: a Bengali **religious sect and a music type**; *Baul Sangeet* / *Baul Gaan*; lyrics carry **Hindu Bhakti and Sufi (Suphi)** influence, exemplified by the songs of **Kabir**; **West Bengal, Assam, Tripura**; mainly **Vaishnava Hindu or Sufi Muslim**; exponents **Yotin Das, Purno Chandra Das, Lalon Fakir, Naboni Das, Sanatan Das Thakur Baul**.
4. **Pandavani**: Mahabharata with **Bhima as hero**; **gayan + vadan**; rhythm of the **tambura**; **Teejanbai** of Chhattisgarh - Padma Shri recorded as **1987** in the core source and **1988** in the official record, conflict noted; Padma Bhushan **2003**, Padma Vibhushan **2019**.
5. **Panihari**: Rajasthan; water; women fetching water in **matkas**; scarcity, distance, village-well sociability, romance, mother-in-law and daughter-in-law relations.
6. **Lavani**: Maharashtra; **dance and song combined**, performed to the **dholki**, a percussion instrument; powerful rhythm.
7. Occasion keys: marriage - **Wanawan, Ovi, Ja-jin-ja, Nyioga**; birth - **Sohar**; war - **Powada, Alha, Hereileu, Saikuti Zai, Dhadi, Khongjom Parba**; water and season - **Panihari, Lotia, Kajri, Pai, Basant Geet**; work - **Pankhida, Ghasiyari Geet**; kingship - **Maand, Sana Lamok, Ammanaivari**.
8. **Khongjom Parba** - **Battle of Khongjom, 1891**: folk music as historical memory.
9. **Khubakeshei** - accompanied **entirely by clapping**.
10. **Borgeet** - **Sankardeva and Madhavdeva, 15th-16th centuries, Ekasarana Dharma**; **Dihanaam** - women's songs in praise of **Sankardev**.
11. Communities: **Langha** (Gujarat and Rajasthan, praise Muslim Rajputs); **Manganiar or Merasi** (Rajasthan, **Kamaicha**); **Karta Bhajas** (West Bengal, four classes); **Bauls** (one of the classes of **Kartabhaja**); **Jogi** (Nath sect); **Bhopa and Bhopi** (religious hymns); **Barot, Charan, Gadhvi** (Gujarat, storytelling); **Muria** (**Relo** songs); **Kamar** (marriage song); **Baghela** (**Hardaul** deity); **Pulayar** (**talams**); **Idu Mishmis**; **Moken** (Andaman and Nicobar, ancestor worship); **Bazigar/Goaar** (Punjab).
12. Music-dance boundary: own the song, the metre, the instrument and the community; route the choreography.

### Concept check

**Question:** What does the folk bank, read by occasion rather than by state, establish about the social function of music in India? Support the answer with four different occasions.

**Model answer:** It establishes that folk music is **occasion-bound and functional** - it marks transitions, accompanies labour, records events and sustains belief - and is therefore evidence about social life rather than only about musical practice. Four occasions demonstrate the range. **Life-cycle transitions**: Sohar is sung in Bihar during childbirth; Wanawan is the auspicious wedding song of Jammu and Kashmir; and Arunachal Pradesh distinguishes Ja-jin-ja, sung during marriages, from Nyioga, sung at the end of the marriage ceremony - a distinction fine enough to show that the occasion, not the theme, is the organising principle. **Labour and resource**: Pankhida is sung by farmers working in the fields in Rajasthan, Ghasiyari Geet by Uttarakhand women going to the forests with an emphasis on the importance of labour, and Panihari records water scarcity, the distance to the well, and the sociability of the women clustered around it. **Historical memory**: Khongjom Parba commemorates the Battle of Khongjom of 1891, and Powada the heroic deeds of Shivaji. **Ritual and belief**: Lai Haraoba Ishei is sung by the Meitei people during the Lai Haraoba festival and is related to Sanamahism, while Borgeet, composed by Sankardeva and Madhavdeva in the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries, is associated with Ekasarana Dharma.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the folk bank as a list of state-wise curiosities. Organised by state it is a memory exercise; organised by occasion it is evidence about what Indian communities marked with song - and it is the second organisation that answers questions.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Folk music is a record of the material and social conditions of the communities that make it." Examine with Indian examples. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition holds, and the evidence is in the content of the songs rather than in their form.

**Material conditions** are recorded most directly by Panihari of Rajasthan, which is about women fetching water from a nearby well and carrying it back in *matkas*, and which describes the scarcity of water and the long distance between the well and the village. Labour is recorded by Pankhida, sung by Rajasthani farmers working in the fields, and by Ghasiyari Geet, sung by Uttarakhand women going to the forests with an emphasis on the importance of labour. Occupation shapes genre elsewhere too: Bhatiali of West Bengal is sung by boat drivers, and tappa originated in the folk songs of the camel riders of north-west India.

**Social relations** are recorded in the same corpus. Panihari also treats the contentious relationship between mother-in-law and daughter-in-law and the daily concerns of village women clustered around the well. Ovi of Maharashtra and Goa is a women's genre of marriage songs and lullabies. Ammanaivari of Tamil Nadu is sung by women at ball-play in praise of the Chola monarch, and Maand of Rajasthan is courtly praise of Rajput rulers - both showing music carrying deference upward.

**Historical events** are recorded by Khongjom Parba of Manipur, on the Battle of Khongjom of 1891, and by Powada of Maharashtra on Shivaji.

The qualification is that the record is selective and conventional: these are composed genres with inherited themes, so they preserve what a community chose to sing about, not a complete account of how it lived.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = material conditions with Panihari's water scarcity and two occupational genres 5 + social relations with at least two named forms including a deference genre 4 + historical memory with Khongjom Parba dated to 1891 3 + the selectivity qualification distinguishing sung convention from social description 3.**

### Visual: the classification as a decision tree

        v                                +--------------+--------------+
