```text
   "They are string instruments"    AIR COLUMN?                       |
        |                                |                            |
   HOW IS THE STRING SET                 v                            v
   IN MOTION?                       SUSHIRA VADYA          IS THERE A STRETCHED
   (a) BOWED                        (Aerophones)           MEMBRANE OVER A
   (b) PLECTRAL (plucked)           "They include all      RESONATOR?
   (c) STRUCK - "played with        the wind instruments"        |
       hammers or sticks"                            +-----------+-----------+
                                                     |                       |
                                                    YES                      NO
                                                     |                       |
                                                     v                       v
                                            AVANADDHA VADYA          GHANA VADYA
                                            (Membranophones)         (Idiophones)
                                            "percussion              "non-drum
                                            instruments, and one     percussion
                                            has to strike to         instruments
                                            generate musical         which DO NOT
                                            sound"                   REQUIRE TUNING"

   THE SINGLE DISCRIMINATOR between the two struck families is the MEMBRANE.
   Both are struck. Only AVANADDHA has a skin. GHANA needs no tuning.

   > Recall aid: A-S-G-T, in the order the classification is printed -
   > Avanaddha (strike a skin) | Sushira (blow through a hollow) |
   > Ghana (strike a solid) | Tata (pluck, bow or strike a string)
```

The tree is built around **mechanism**, not around appearance, because appearance is exactly what the close options exploit. A ghatam looks like a pot and is struck like a drum; the decision tree places it correctly in one question.

### 1. The four families, with the recorded sound mechanisms

**There are four major categories of musical instruments.** The mechanism sentences are quoted because they are the definitions and are directly examinable.

| Sanskrit family | Western equivalent | Recorded mechanism |
|---|---|---|
| **Awanaddha / Avanaddha Vadya** | **Membranophones** | **"They are percussion instruments, and one has to strike to generate musical sound"** |
| **Sushira Vadya** | **Aerophones** | **"They include all the wind instruments"** |
| **Ghana Vadya** | **Idiophones** | **"They are non-drum percussion instruments which do not require tuning"** |
| **Tata Vadya** | **Chordophones** | **"They are string instruments"** |

### 2. Avanaddha Vadya - membranophones

| Sub-type | Instruments | Usage as recorded |
|---|---|---|
| **Classical** | **Tabla, Drum, Dhol, Congo, Mridangam, Bhumi Dundubhi** (ancient earth drum) | **Tabla - Hindustani; Mridangam - Carnatic** |
| **Folk** | **Ghumot** (Goa, Ganesh festival), **Idakka** (Kerala, Damru-like), **Udukai** (Tamil Nadu, Damru-type), **Sambal** (Maharashtra, Konkan), **Tamak** (Santhals, two-headed drum), **Diggi** (Uttar Pradesh) | Regional and tribal drums |

**Tabla for Hindustani and mridangam for Carnatic is the single most repeated instrument pairing in objective papers.** It also connects to Lesson 12's improvisation vocabulary, where the presence or absence of the mridangam distinguishes *Tanam* from *Ragam*.

**Bhumi Dundubhi** is glossed as an **ancient earth drum**. Report the gloss; do not convert it into an excavated object or a date.

### 3. Sushira Vadya - aerophones

| Sub-type | Instruments | Usage as recorded |
|---|---|---|
| **Classical** | **Bansuri (Flute), Shehnai, Pungi, Ninkirns** | **Shehnai - Ustad Bismillah Khan; Flute - Krishna, Hariprasad Chaurasia** |
| **Folk** | **Pungi / Been** (snake charmers), **Algoza** (Punjab, double flute), **Tangmuri** (Meghalaya), **Titti** (South India, bagpipe), **Mashak** (Uttarakhand, Rajasthan, UP), **Gogona** (Assam, Bihu), **Ejuk Tapung** (Assam, rare) | Wind-based folk instruments |

Note that the usage column mixes a **historical performer** (Bismillah Khan, Hariprasad Chaurasia) with a **mythic association** (Krishna and the flute). Both are recorded; keep them apart in an answer. And note **Titti**, a South Indian bagpipe, and **Mashak**, its northern counterpart - a bag-and-pipe aerophone in two separate Indian regions is a useful item against the assumption that the bagpipe is a foreign instrument.

### 4. Ghana Vadya - idiophones

| Sub-type | Instruments | Usage as recorded |
|---|---|---|
| **Classical** | **Manjira / Jhanj, Jaltarang, Kanch-tarang, Ghungroo, Ghatam, Khartal** | **Rhythm-keepers, ancient (Harappan)** |
| **Folk** | **Chimta** (Punjab, fire tongs), **Gharha** (Punjab, earthen pots), **Andelu** (Burra-Katha, hollow metal rings) | Used in folk songs and storytelling |

This family produces the topic's best close options, because three of its members look like something else. The **ghatam** is a pot. The **jaltarang** and **kanch-tarang** are sets of bowls. The **chimta** is a pair of fire tongs and the **gharha** an earthen pot. None has a membrane; none requires tuning; all are **Ghana Vadya**.

The "ancient (Harappan)" gloss on the classical Ghana set should be reported as a gloss and not converted into an archaeological claim.

Three of these instruments have already appeared elsewhere in this session: **chimta** and **manjira** accompany the bhajan of Lesson 15, and **ghungroo** belongs to the dance boundary. That overlap is worth noticing, because it shows the idiophone family sitting where music meets ritual, storytelling and dance.

### 5. Tata Vadya - chordophones, with three sub-types

| Sub-type | Instruments | Usage as recorded |
|---|---|---|
| **(a) Bowed, classical** | **Sarangi, Esraj / Dilruba, Violin** | **Sarangi pioneers - Bhangash family** |
| **(a) Bowed, folk** | **Chikara** (Rajasthan / UP / MP), **Dilruba / Esraj** (Punjab and Bengal), **Sarinda** (Santhals, Assam, Rajasthan), **Tingteila** (Nagaland), **Kamaicha** (Rajasthan, **Manganiyars**) | Tribal and folk bowed strings |
| **(b) Plectral, classical** | **Sitar, Veena, Tanpura, Guitar** | **Sitar gharanas - Jaipur, Varanasi, Etawah; Veena - Saraswati's instrument** |
| **(b) Plectral, folk** | **Tumbi** (Punjab, Bhangra), **Ektara / Tun Tuna** (wandering monks), **Dotara** (**Bauls**), **Onavillu** (Kerala, bamboo) | Devotional and regional string instruments |
| **(c) Struck, classical** | **Gotuvadyam, Swaramandal** | **Played with hammers or sticks** |

The **struck chordophones** are the sleeper category. **Gotuvadyam** and **Swaramandal** are struck with hammers or sticks, and a close option that files them as percussion is testing precisely this: **the string, not the striking, fixes the family.**

Two cross-links to earlier lessons: the **Kamaicha** is the instrument of the **Manganiar or Merasi** community of Rajasthan, recorded in Lesson 16 as using it to accompany classical folk music and songs; and the **Dotara** belongs to the **Bauls**, whose sect and genre were treated in the same lesson. Instrument, community and genre are one object in the folk register.

**The violin appears among the bowed classical chordophones**, and Lesson 13 recorded **flute and violin as common to both traditions** - the one row of the difference table that names a shared item.

### 6. The santoor

**The Santoor is a 100-stringed instrument and has been a traditional instrument of Jammu and Kashmir since ancient times. Sufiana kalam music is accompanied by a Santoor.**

Three examinable facts in two sentences: the **string count of 100**, the **region**, and the **genre it accompanies**. And one discipline: **Sufiana kalam is a genre, not an instrument**. The santoor **accompanies** it. Confusing the instrument with the music it plays is the second of the four traps below.

Note also that the santoor is a **struck chordophone** by mechanism, which places it with Gotuvadyam and Swaramandal in sub-type (c) rather than with the percussion families. The difference table of Lesson 13 lists it among the **major Hindustani instruments**, alongside tabla, sarangi and sitar.

### 7. The four close-option traps

| Trap | Wrong | Right |
|---|---|---|
| **Ghatam and jaltarang filed as drums** | "Percussion, therefore membranophone" | Both are **Ghana Vadya (idiophones)** - **non-drum** percussion, **no tuning required**. A ghatam is a **pot**; a jaltarang is a set of **bowls**. No membrane, so not Avanaddha |
| **Family confused with genre** | "Shehnai is a genre"; "Dhrupad is an instrument"; "Sufiana kalam is an instrument" | **Family** = how the sound is made. **Genre** = what is performed - dhrupad, khayal, thumri, Sufiana kalam, Rabindra Sangeet, Haveli Sangeet. The **santoor accompanies** Sufiana kalam; the instrument and the genre are different objects |
| **Region attached to the wrong instrument** | Santoor placed in Rajasthan; Kamaicha in Kashmir | **Santoor - Jammu and Kashmir; Kamaicha - Rajasthan (Manganiyars); Tumbi - Punjab (Bhangra); Gogona - Assam (Bihu); Idakka - Kerala; Udukai - Tamil Nadu; Sambal - Maharashtra/Konkan; Tangmuri - Meghalaya** |
| **Tradition attached to the wrong drum** | "Mridangam is Hindustani" | **Tabla - Hindustani; Mridangam - Carnatic** |

### 8. Instrument prohibitions

- **Do not assign an inventor or an invention date to any instrument.** The tabla and sitar invention dates remain uncertain; Amir Khusrau's credit for the sitar and for the *Sehtar* is recorded as a credit and a belief, not as a settled fact.
- **Do not state string counts other than the santoor's 100**, which is the only count the sources give.
- **Do not add sitar gharanas beyond Jaipur, Varanasi and Etawah.**
- **Do not convert the "ancient (Harappan)" gloss on the Ghana set, or "ancient earth drum" for Bhumi Dundubhi, into an excavated-object or dated claim.** The one archaeological instrument statement this session carries is the **seven-holed flute recovered from Indus Valley Civilisation sites** in Lesson 7.
- **Do not attribute an instrument to a performer the sources do not name**, and do not translate a folk instrument's name or assign it a community the sources do not give.

### Examination use

Instrument identification is among the highest-frequency objective demands in this subject, and there is a recorded Main Examination question asking precisely for **the groups into which musical instruments in India have traditionally been classified**. Objective-ready here: the four Sanskrit family names with their Western equivalents and mechanisms; the classical and folk instruments of each family; every region pairing; the tabla-mridangam split; the sitar gharanas; the santoor's three facts; and the struck-chordophone sub-type. For a descriptive answer, the classification supplies the "material and technique" leg of any fifteen-mark music answer.

### Traps introduced here

- Using a three-way Western classification - string, wind, percussion - and losing the fourth family and the Sanskrit names.
- Filing ghatam, jaltarang, chimta or gharha as membranophones.
- Filing gotuvadyam or swaramandal as percussion because they are struck.
- Reversing the tabla and mridangam traditions.
- Treating Sufiana kalam as an instrument.
- Giving an instrument an inventor or an invention date.

### Revision notes

1. Four families: **Tata (chordophones), Sushira (aerophones), Avanaddha (membranophones), Ghana (idiophones)**.
2. Mechanisms: Tata - **string instruments**; Sushira - **all the wind instruments**; Avanaddha - **percussion, one has to strike**; Ghana - **non-drum percussion which do not require tuning**.
3. The discriminator between the two struck families is the **membrane**.
4. **Avanaddha classical**: Tabla, Drum, Dhol, Congo, Mridangam, **Bhumi Dundubhi** (ancient earth drum). **Tabla - Hindustani; Mridangam - Carnatic.**
5. **Avanaddha folk**: Ghumot (Goa, Ganesh festival), Idakka (Kerala), Udukai (Tamil Nadu), Sambal (Maharashtra, Konkan), Tamak (Santhals), Diggi (UP).
6. **Sushira classical**: Bansuri, **Shehnai**, Pungi, Ninkirns. **Shehnai - Bismillah Khan; Flute - Krishna, Hariprasad Chaurasia.**
7. **Sushira folk**: Pungi/Been (snake charmers), Algoza (Punjab, double flute), Tangmuri (Meghalaya), **Titti** (South India, bagpipe), **Mashak** (Uttarakhand, Rajasthan, UP), Gogona (Assam, Bihu), Ejuk Tapung (Assam, rare).
8. **Ghana classical**: Manjira/Jhanj, **Jaltarang**, Kanch-tarang, Ghungroo, **Ghatam**, Khartal - rhythm-keepers, ancient (Harappan). **Ghana folk**: Chimta (Punjab, fire tongs), Gharha (Punjab, earthen pots), Andelu (Burra-Katha, hollow metal rings).
9. **Tata (a) bowed classical**: Sarangi, Esraj/Dilruba, Violin - **Sarangi pioneers, Bhangash family**. **(a) bowed folk**: Chikara, Dilruba/Esraj, Sarinda, Tingteila, **Kamaicha (Rajasthan, Manganiyars)**.
10. **Tata (b) plectral classical**: **Sitar, Veena, Tanpura, Guitar** - **sitar gharanas Jaipur, Varanasi, Etawah; Veena - Saraswati's instrument**. **(b) plectral folk**: Tumbi (Punjab, Bhangra), Ektara/Tun Tuna (wandering monks), **Dotara (Bauls)**, Onavillu (Kerala, bamboo).
11. **Tata (c) struck classical**: **Gotuvadyam, Swaramandal** - played with hammers or sticks. The **string**, not the striking, fixes the family.
12. **Santoor**: **100-stringed**; traditional instrument of **Jammu and Kashmir** since ancient times; **Sufiana kalam is accompanied by the Santoor**.
13. Shared instruments across both traditions: **flute and violin**.
14. Prohibitions: no inventor, no invention date, no string count other than 100, no sitar gharana beyond three, no archaeological conversion of the Harappan gloss.

### Concept check

**Question:** Four instruments are placed before you - a ghatam, a swaramandal, a santoor and an idakka. Assign each to its family, state the decisive test in each case, and identify which pair is the likeliest to be confused.

**Model answer:** The **ghatam** is **Ghana Vadya**, an idiophone. The decisive test is the absence of a stretched membrane: it is struck, but it is a pot, and the family is defined as non-drum percussion which does not require tuning. The **swaramandal** is **Tata Vadya**, a chordophone, in the **struck** sub-type, recorded as played with hammers or sticks alongside the gotuvadyam. The decisive test is that a string vibrates; the method of setting the string in motion selects the sub-type but not the family. The **santoor** is also **Tata Vadya** by the same test - a hundred-stringed instrument of Jammu and Kashmir that accompanies Sufiana kalam - and it is struck rather than bowed or plucked. The **idakka** is **Avanaddha Vadya**, a membranophone, recorded as a Damru-like folk drum of Kerala; the decisive test is the stretched membrane over a resonator. The likeliest confusion is between the **ghatam and the swaramandal**, because both are struck and neither looks like a conventional drum: the ghatam has no string and no membrane, so it is an idiophone, while the swaramandal has strings, so it is a chordophone despite being struck. The general rule the pair establishes is that **what vibrates fixes the family; how it is set vibrating fixes only the sub-type**.

**Misconception to avoid:** Using the Western three-way division of string, wind and percussion. It collapses two distinct Indian families into one, loses the Sanskrit terms that are directly examinable, and makes the ghatam, jaltarang, chimta and gharha unclassifiable.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The traditional Indian classification of musical instruments works by sound mechanism, while the familiar three-way division works by appearance." Examine, using instruments that the three-way division cannot place. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The proposition holds, and the test is the set of instruments each scheme can place.

The Indian scheme classifies by what vibrates. **Tata Vadya** are string instruments, sub-divided by how the string is set in motion - bowed, plectral, or **struck, played with hammers or sticks**. **Sushira Vadya** include all the wind instruments. **Avanaddha Vadya** are percussion instruments which one has to strike, over a stretched membrane. **Ghana Vadya** are **non-drum percussion instruments which do not require tuning**.

Three instruments expose the difference. The **ghatam** is a pot and the **jaltarang** a set of bowls: both are struck, neither has a membrane, so both are idiophones - a category the three-way division lacks entirely, forcing them in with drums. The **swaramandal** and **gotuvadyam** are struck with hammers, yet remain chordophones, because the string and not the striking fixes the family. The **chimta**, literally fire tongs, is an instrument only by mechanism.

The qualification is that both schemes are descriptive: neither dates or locates an instrument.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = all four Sanskrit families with their recorded mechanisms 3 + the ghatam and jaltarang case showing the missing idiophone category 3 + the struck-chordophone case showing mechanism overriding appearance 2 + the chimta as an everyday object recruited by mechanism 1 + the descriptive-not-historical qualification 1.**

## Lesson 18 - Notation, schools, broadcasting and recognition: music becomes an institution

Progress: 18/19 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the two modern systematisers and their notation and classification work, the four named institutions with their dates, the outreach movement, the festival, the patronage-to-recognition argument, and the topic's single current linkage

### Visual: what replaced the court, stage by stage

The diagram's three numbered answers are the lesson's structure, and they are in dependency order: you cannot build a school without a notated syllabus, and you cannot build a national academy without schools producing teachers.

### 1. The two systematisers

**Pandit Vishnu Narayan Bhatkhande (1860-1936).** **He was an eminent musicologist. He composed the first modern treatise on Hindustani classical music. He introduced the modern *thaat* system of Hindustani classical music. His major contributions include *Hindustani Sangeet Paddhati*, *Sreemallaksya Sangeetam*, *Abhinava Raga Manjari* and *A Short Historical Survey of the Music of Upper India*.**

**Pandit Vishnu Digambar Paluskar (1872-1931).** **He was an eminent Hindustani musician who sang the original version of the bhajan *Raghupati Raghava Raja Ram*. He set up the Gandharva Mahavidyalaya in 1901. Pandit Paluskar has been credited for arranging India's national song, *Vande Mataram*, and composing the song in Raga Kafi. He also introduced the scheme of Raga classification in Indian music.**

Read the two side by side and the division of labour is clear.

| | Bhatkhande | Paluskar |
|---|---|---|
| **Primary identity** | **Musicologist** | **Musician** |
| **Central contribution** | The **modern thaat system**; the **first modern treatise** on Hindustani classical music | The **Gandharva Mahavidyalaya, 1901**; a **scheme of raga classification** |
| **Named works** | *Hindustani Sangeet Paddhati*, *Sreemallaksya Sangeetam*, *Abhinava Raga Manjari*, *A Short Historical Survey of the Music of Upper India* | The original version of the bhajan *Raghupati Raghava Raja Ram*; credited with arranging *Vande Mataram* in **Raga Kafi** |
| **Where else he appears** | Lesson 5, as the author of the ten-thaat classification | Lesson 9, as a **Gwalior gharana** exponent |

Paluskar's double appearance is the whole argument of this lesson in one person: a gharana-trained performer who founded a school. The institutional turn was made from inside the lineage system, not against it.

Note also what each did to the **ownership of knowledge**. A gharana's style was held by a household and transmitted by long apprenticeship. A published treatise, a printed notation and a classification scheme make the same material available to anyone who can read - which widens access and, at the same time, standardises what had been plural. Both consequences should be stated; an answer that names only the first has praised rather than analysed.

### 2. The four institutions

| Institution | Year | Founder / authority | Recorded purpose and detail |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Gandharva Mahavidyalaya** | **1901** | **V.D. Paluskar** | **Set up with the express purpose of teaching and transmitting the knowledge of Indian classical music and dance to future generations. Initially opened in Lahore, moved to Mumbai in 1915. Focus on the Hindustani and Carnatic classical forms of music** |
| **Prayag Sangeet Samiti** | **1926** | - | **Established at Allahabad, now Prayagraj, to impart education on Hindustani classical music** |
| **Marris College of Music** | **1926** | **Vishnu Narayan Bhatkhande** | **One of the premier institutes for studying classical music in India. He returned to his hometown of Lucknow to establish it, to concentrate on the theory of music as well as the practice of singing and playing instruments. Later renamed the Bhatkhande Music Institute Deemed University** |
| **Sangeet Natak Akademi** | **1952** | **Government of India** | **The first national academy set up for the performing arts by the Government of India**, and the **National Academy of Music, Dance and Drama**. Its remit covers **music, dance and theatre, including traditional, folk and tribal forms and puppetry, together with allied performing arts**; it is the **primary body for showcasing the performing arts in the country**, and it carries the stated task of **promoting the enormous intangible heritage of India as demonstrated through its music, dance and drama**. The standing source's three-word formula, "music, drama and dances", names this same remit in compressed form |

Four observations that turn the table into an argument.

- **The 1901 school was opened in Lahore and moved to Mumbai in 1915.** A founding city now outside India, and a relocation, sit inside the institutional history of Indian classical music - the same kind of dislocation that Lesson 11 recorded for the Talwandi gharana, this time survived because the institution was movable in a way a household lineage was not.
- **The 1901 school's remit covers both Hindustani and Carnatic forms.** An institution founded by a Gwalior-trained Hindustani musician to teach both traditions is evidence that the institutional turn worked against, not with, the hardening of the two-tradition divide.
- **Two institutions were founded in the same year, 1926, in two cities.** Prayag Sangeet Samiti at Allahabad and Marris College at Lucknow. The date pairing is directly examinable and the two must not be merged.
- **Bhatkhande's college was designed to teach theory as well as practice.** That is the notation project becoming a curriculum: what had been written down could now be taught to a class rather than to an apprentice.

### 3. Outreach, festival and the audience problem

**SPIC MACAY.** **Dr Kiran Seth established the community of SPIC MACAY in 1977. The full name of this organisation is the "Society for the Promotion of Indian Classical Music and Culture Amongst Youth". It began as a voluntary youth movement that grew into a platform to showcase the culture of India to the general public, especially the younger generations, which had been losing touch with their Indian classical roots.** **The basic reasoning behind this organisation is to promote Indian classical music, dance, yoga, meditation and other aspects of Indian culture. The organisation targets laypeople and the youth by holding many free-entry events. It has grown into a large organisation with a large number of chapters or branches across the globe, with its headquarters in Delhi.**

Note what is being solved. Schools solved the **transmission** problem; an academy solved the **recognition** problem; SPIC MACAY addresses the **audience** problem - a tradition that has teachers, performers and honours but no listeners is still in difficulty. The recorded rationale says so directly: younger generations had been losing touch with their classical roots. The instrument chosen is **free entry**, which is a statement about the economics of access.

**Chaturprahar.** **An annual Indian classical music festival held at the National Centre for Performing Arts in Mumbai. It is based on the concept of association of time with the ragas.**

This is the neatest single item in the lesson, because it closes a loop opened in Lesson 6. The *samay* system - Poorva Bhaag and Uttar Bhaag, the shift of the saptak from Sa-to-Ma to Pa-to-Sa - is not merely a historical convention: a contemporary urban festival is **programmed on it**. A theoretical association has become an organising principle of a concert calendar.

### 4. The argument: from employment to recognition

State the sequence, then evidence it.

```text
   EMPLOYMENT      a musician is retained by a court
                   evidence: Tansen under Akbar; Sadarang under Muhammad Shah;
                   Naubat Khan a mansabdar; Lal Khan titled by Shah Jahan;
                   the Thanjavur Quartet at the Thanjavur Maratha court, and
                   Vadivelu of that Quartet later at Swathi Thirunal's court
        |
   ELITE HOUSEHOLD a musician performs for a private gathering
                   evidence: the baithaki style in the baithak khanas and
                   jalsaghar of the zamindari elite, late 19th-early 20th c.
        |
   ENROLMENT       a musician is trained by an institution
                   evidence: 1901 Gandharva Mahavidyalaya; 1926 Prayag Sangeet
                   Samiti; 1926 Marris College, later a Deemed University
        |
   RECOGNITION     a musician is honoured by the state
                   evidence: the national academy for the performing arts,
                   1952; the recorded Bharat Ratna years 1998, 1999, 2001, 2008
                   across two traditions and two instruments
        |
   AUDIENCE        a musician reaches a public
                   evidence: SPIC MACAY 1977 with free-entry events and global
                   chapters; Chaturprahar at the NCPA; and the recorded move of
                   gharana lineages into urban concert, radio, teaching and
                   recording
```

The four recorded **Bharat Ratna** years are the strongest single piece of evidence for the recognition stage, and they should be used as a **sequence** rather than as four separate facts: **M.S. Subbulakshmi 1998, Ravi Shankar 1999, Bismillah Khan 2001, Bhimsen Joshi 2008** - a dated official sequence spanning **two traditions**, Carnatic vocal and Hindustani vocal, and **two instruments**, sitar and shehnai. After the courts ended, the state did not employ musicians; it **recognised** them. Recognition, not employment, became the reward structure.

⚠️ The five-stage sequence is an **analytical framing** of recorded evidence, not a formulation quoted from a source. The evidence for each stage is recorded; the arrangement is the argument.

### 5. Documentation, broadcasting and archives - what is owned and what is not

This is the point at which the topic's boundary has to be respected precisely.

**Owned, because the sources supply it:**

- **Notation and published pedagogy** as instruments of transmission - Bhatkhande's first modern treatise, his named works, his thaat system, and Paluskar's raga-classification scheme.
- **Institutional teaching** - the three schools with their dates, cities, founders and stated remits, and the later conversion of one into a deemed university.
- **A national academy for the performing arts**, its 1952 founding, its remit across **music, dance and theatre, including traditional, folk and tribal forms and puppetry, and allied performing arts**, and its stated task of **promoting the enormous intangible heritage of India as demonstrated through its music, dance and drama**.
- **Relocation of lineages into urban concert, radio, teaching and recording** - the recorded survival route for gharanas after princely patronage contracted.
- **Public outreach and festival programming** - SPIC MACAY and Chaturprahar.

**Not owned, and therefore routed:**

- **Award administration, awardee lists, eligibility rules and recognition policy**, which belong to the heritage and cultural-policy topic. Awardee lists change; cite an awardee only with an award year from a dated source.
- **UNESCO intangible-cultural-heritage inscription**, which is a **separate international instrument** and must never be equated with national academy recognition. The two are different statuses conferred by different bodies under different criteria.
- **Broadcasting policy, archival law and institutional structure** beyond the recorded fact that radio and recording became transmission routes for gharana lineages.

### 6. The one current linkage in this session

✅ **Fact, from a dated report.** A report published on **11 June 2026** records that **on Wednesday, 10 June 2026, the Sangeet Natak Akademi selected seven eminent personalities in the field of performing arts as Akademi Fellows (Akademi Ratna) and awarded 108 artistes from the fields of music, dance and other performing arts for the year 2024-25.** The same report describes the Akademi as **the National Academy of Music, Dance and Drama, India's apex national body for the performing arts, established in 1953 and functioning under the Ministry of Culture, dedicated to preserving, promoting and documenting the diverse intangible cultural heritage of India.**

⚠️ **A disclosed conflict, reported and not resolved.** The core source records the Akademi as **set up by the Government of India in 1952**; the dated 2026 report gives its establishment as **1953**. Both are stated here. If a year must be written, write the **1952** figure that the standing source supplies and note that a **1953** date is also current, or avoid the bare year and write "the first national academy for the performing arts, set up in the early 1950s".

**What the item is used for, and where it stops.** It is used for exactly one analytical purpose: to show that the recognition stage of section 4 is **operative and continuing**, with the honours cycle distinguishing a small **Fellowship** from a much larger annual award list, and that the body administering it describes its own function in terms of **preserving, promoting and documenting intangible cultural heritage** - the same three verbs that the safeguarding argument needs. **No awardee is named, no award money, category list, eligibility rule or ceremony detail is asserted, and nothing is claimed about recognition policy, which is routed.**

**Every other lesson in this session truthfully records that no current linkage is required.**

### Examination use

Objective-ready: Bhatkhande's and Paluskar's dates and their four and two contributions respectively; **Gandharva Mahavidyalaya 1901, Lahore, moved to Mumbai 1915, Hindustani and Carnatic**; **Prayag Sangeet Samiti 1926, Allahabad**; **Marris College 1926, Lucknow, later Bhatkhande Music Institute Deemed University**; **the first national academy for the performing arts, 1952, music, drama and dance, intangible heritage**; **SPIC MACAY 1977, Dr Kiran Seth, full form, free-entry events, Delhi headquarters**; and **Chaturprahar at the NCPA, based on the association of time with the ragas**. There is a recorded objective demand on the first Gandharva Mahavidyalaya, its founder and its year, which makes this the single most directly tested lesson in the session. For Mains, section 4 is the closing movement of any patronage answer.

### Traps introduced here

- Merging the two 1926 institutions; they are **Prayag Sangeet Samiti at Allahabad** and **Marris College at Lucknow**.
- Attributing the Gandharva Mahavidyalaya to Bhatkhande or the thaat system to Paluskar.
- Forgetting that the 1901 school was opened in **Lahore** and moved to **Mumbai in 1915**.
- Equating national academy recognition with UNESCO intangible-heritage inscription.
- Naming a recent awardee without an award year from a dated source.
- Presenting institutionalisation as an unmixed good; it widened access and standardised what had been plural.

### Revision notes

1. **Bhatkhande (1860-1936)**: musicologist; **first modern treatise on Hindustani classical music**; **modern thaat system**; works - ***Hindustani Sangeet Paddhati, Sreemallaksya Sangeetam, Abhinava Raga Manjari, A Short Historical Survey of the Music of Upper India***.
2. **Paluskar (1872-1931)**: Hindustani musician; sang the original version of the bhajan ***Raghupati Raghava Raja Ram***; set up the **Gandharva Mahavidyalaya in 1901**; credited with arranging ***Vande Mataram*** and composing it in **Raga Kafi**; introduced a **scheme of raga classification**.
3. **Gandharva Mahavidyalaya, 1901**, V.D. Paluskar; **opened in Lahore, moved to Mumbai in 1915**; focus on **Hindustani and Carnatic** classical forms; express purpose of teaching and transmitting Indian classical music and dance to future generations.
4. **Prayag Sangeet Samiti, 1926, Allahabad (now Prayagraj)** - Hindustani classical music.
5. **Marris College of Music, 1926, Lucknow**, founded by **Bhatkhande**; **theory and practice**; later renamed **Bhatkhande Music Institute Deemed University**.
6. **Sangeet Natak Akademi, 1952** - **the first national academy for the performing arts set up by the Government of India**, the **National Academy of Music, Dance and Drama**; remit across **music, dance and theatre, including traditional, folk and tribal forms and puppetry, and allied performing arts**; primary body for showcasing the performing arts; task of **promoting the enormous intangible heritage of India**.
7. **SPIC MACAY, 1977, Dr Kiran Seth** - **Society for the Promotion of Indian Classical Music and Culture Amongst Youth**; voluntary youth movement; classical music, dance, yoga, meditation; **free-entry events**; chapters across the globe; **headquarters in Delhi**.
8. **Chaturprahar** - annual Indian classical music festival at the **National Centre for Performing Arts, Mumbai**; **based on the association of time with the ragas**.
9. The five-stage argument: **employment -> elite household -> enrolment -> recognition -> audience**.
10. Recognition evidence: the four recorded **Bharat Ratna** years - **1998, 1999, 2001, 2008** - across two traditions and two instruments.
11. Boundary: award administration, awardee lists and recognition policy are routed; **UNESCO intangible-heritage inscription is a separate international instrument** and is never equated with national academy recognition.
12. Current linkage, dated: a report of **11 June 2026** records the Akademi's selection on **10 June 2026** of **seven Akademi Fellows (Akademi Ratna)** and awards to **108 artistes** from music, dance and other performing arts for **2024-25**; the same report describes the Akademi as established in **1953** under the **Ministry of Culture** and dedicated to **preserving, promoting and documenting** intangible cultural heritage. The **1952 / 1953** conflict with the standing source is recorded and not resolved.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain what problem each stage of institution-building in twentieth-century Indian music was designed to solve, and state the cost that accompanied the solution.

**Model answer:** Three problems, three solutions. The **transmission** problem came first: a tradition held in households and reproduced by long apprenticeship was vulnerable, as the Talwandi gharana's near-extinction after historical decline and partition shows. It was answered by **writing the music down** - Bhatkhande composed the first modern treatise on Hindustani classical music, introduced the modern thaat system and published *Hindustani Sangeet Paddhati* and other works, while Paluskar introduced a scheme of raga classification - and then by **building schools**: the Gandharva Mahavidyalaya in 1901, opened in Lahore and moved to Mumbai in 1915 with a remit covering both Hindustani and Carnatic forms; Prayag Sangeet Samiti at Allahabad in 1926; and Marris College at Lucknow in 1926, teaching theory as well as practice and later becoming a deemed university. The **recognition** problem followed: with courts gone, no one conferred status. It was answered by the first national academy for the performing arts in 1952, covering music, drama and dance and charged with promoting India's intangible heritage, and by an official honours sequence - the recorded Bharat Ratna years of 1998, 1999, 2001 and 2008 across two traditions and two instruments. The **audience** problem came last and was answered by SPIC MACAY in 1977, with free-entry events aimed at a younger generation described as losing touch with its classical roots, and by festivals such as Chaturprahar at the National Centre for Performing Arts. The cost is uniform across all three: making the tradition transmissible outside hereditary lineages **standardised what had been plural**. A published notation and a single national classification make one version authoritative, and the household variations that gave the gharanas their distinct identities have no comparable institutional protection.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating institutionalisation as the replacement of a dying tradition by the state. The turn was made from inside the lineage system - Paluskar was a Gwalior-trained performer who founded a school - and the gharanas themselves survived by moving into the very concert, radio, teaching and recording circuits that institutionalisation created.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"After the decline of princely patronage, the Indian state replaced the patron with the award rather than with the salary." Critically examine. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The formulation captures a real change but understates two other replacements, and the examination turns on distinguishing them.

The case for the proposition is strong. Under court patronage a musician was **employed**: Tansen was retained first by Raja Ramchandra of Rewa and then by Akbar, who gave him his title; Sadarang was court musician to Muhammad Shah; Naubat Khan held a mansab; Lal Khan received a title from Shah Jahan; the Thanjavur Quartet were court musicians of the Thanjavur Maratha court, and Vadivelu of that Quartet was afterwards retained at Swathi Thirunal's court. After the courts, the state conferred **honour** rather than employment, and the recorded sequence of Bharat Ratna years - 1998, 1999, 2001 and 2008 - runs across two traditions and two instruments, which is a pattern of recognition rather than of retention.

But two other replacements were at least as important. The first is **enrolment**: the Gandharva Mahavidyalaya of 1901, Prayag Sangeet Samiti and Marris College of 1926 turned musical training into institutional education, backed by Bhatkhande's first modern treatise and his thaat system. The second is the **audience**: gharana lineages moved into urban concert, radio, teaching and recording, and later organisations - SPIC MACAY from 1977, with free-entry events - and festivals such as Chaturprahar built a public. A national academy for the performing arts, founded in 1952 for music, drama and dance and charged with promoting intangible heritage, sits across recognition and promotion together.

The critical qualification is that awards are episodic and confer no income, so the award replaced the patron's **status function** while the concert, the classroom and the recording replaced the patron's **economic function**.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the employment-to-recognition contrast with at least three named court appointments 5 + the dated honours sequence used as a pattern rather than as four facts 3 + the enrolment replacement with all three institutions and their years 5 + the audience replacement with outreach, festival, radio and recording 4 + the status-versus-economic-function qualification as the verdict 3.**

## Lesson 19 - Attribution, chronology and answer construction

Progress: 19/19 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the attribution problem and its discipline, the three chronologies revisited, the disclosed conflicts in this topic, the safe formulations, and the construction of 10-, 15- and 20-mark music answers

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the recorded attribution statements and their own hedges, the conflicting entries identified across the treatise, composer, rasa and folk banks, and the canonical factual-risk controls
CA search: "Not required - method and answer-construction lesson; the session's single current linkage is at Lesson 18"
CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the attribution ladder

```text
   A CLAIM OF THE FORM "X INVENTED Y" ARRIVES
                        |
                        v
   [1] Is there a CONTEMPORANEOUS source naming X as the originator?
        YES -> state it, cite the source, and give the date
        NO  -> go to [2]
                        |
                        v
   [2] Does the standing literature record the attribution AND a dispute?
        YES -> "credited with ... though the attribution is disputed"
               example: "Amir Khusrau has been credited as the originator
               of Qawwali but this has been widely disputed"
        NO  -> go to [3]
                        |
                        v
   [3] Does the literature record the attribution with a hedge of its own?
        YES -> reproduce the hedge
               examples: "the origin of this style was ATTRIBUTED to Amir
               Khusrau"; "it is BELIEVED that Amir Khusrau invented ... the
               Sehtar"; "the Ravanahatha is BELIEVED to have originated from
               the Hela Civilisation"; "HISTORIANS SUGGEST that Guru Nanak
               and Mardana were responsible for ... Shabad"
        NO  -> go to [4]
                        |
                        v
   [4] Is the claim a DATE for a form or an instrument?
        YES -> do not supply one. "The invention dates of the tabla and the
               sitar remain uncertain."
        NO  -> state the recorded fact without the invention language.
               "Tansen CREATED SEVERAL NEW RAGAS" is recorded.
               "Tansen invented the raga system" is not.
```

The ladder is not scepticism for its own sake. Every rung is a **recorded formulation** available in the standing literature; the discipline is simply to use the formulation the evidence supports rather than the strongest one available.

### 1. The attribution problem, stated once

Indian music is transmitted orally, and oral transmission produces legend-formation. Where a form's origin is genuinely unrecoverable, tradition attaches it to a memorable figure. This is why a single thirteenth-century figure, **Amir Khusrau (1253-1325)**, is credited in the standing material with:

- being the **Father of Qawwali** - with the same material recording that **this has been widely disputed**;
- **introducing the Ghazal, Tarana and Trivat**;
- the **invention of the sitar**;
- the **invention of a three-stringed instrument called the *Sehtar*** - recorded as "it is believed";
- the **origin of the Khyal style** - recorded as "attributed to";
- **inventing the tarana style in the thirteenth-fourteenth centuries**.

Six claims of origination for four instruments and genres in one lifetime. The correct posture is not to deny them - they are the recorded tradition and are examinable as such - but to **report them as attributions**. An objective item that asks what Amir Khusrau is credited with is answered from the list; a descriptive answer that asserts the list as settled fact has overstated its evidence.

The same discipline applies elsewhere in the topic: **Tansen** is recorded as having **created several new ragas**, which is a claim about composition and should not be inflated into invention of the raga system; **Purandara Dasa** is recorded as the **founding father** and **Pitamaha** of Carnatic music, which is a traditional honorific about pedagogy and repertoire rather than a claim that he originated the tradition; and **Annamacharya** is the **first known** Carnatic composer, where "known" is doing essential work.

### 2. The three chronologies, revisited with the topic's evidence

Lesson 1 introduced them; the session now has the material to show why they matter.

| Chronology | Reliable examples from this topic | The failure it prevents |
|---|---|---|
| **Text dates** | *Natyashastra* 2nd century BC; *Brihaddesi* 6th-8th centuries; *Sangita Ratnakara* 13th century; *Sangeeta Sudhakara* 14th century AD; *Chaturdandiprakashika* 17th century; *Ain-i-Akbari* 1593; *Yugala Shataka* 1294 CE | Treating an undated tradition as datable |
| **Person and patronage dates** | Annamacharya 1408-1503; Purandara Dasa 1484-1564; Man Singh Tomar 1486-1516; Swami Haridas 1478-1573; Ibrahim Adil Shah II 1580-1627; Sadarang 1670-1748; Swathi Thirunal 1813-1846; Bhatkhande 1860-1936; Paluskar 1872-1931; the four Bharat Ratna years | Confusing the life of a musician with the age of a form |
| **Form and instrument dates** | **None may be supplied.** Dhrupad's four anchors date its mention, its earliest surviving lyrics, its consolidation and its naming - not its invention. The invention dates of the **tabla** and the **sitar** remain **uncertain** | Converting a first mention into an origin |

The single most transferable rule in the topic: **the earliest source that contains a form and the earliest source that names it are different facts.** Dhrupad demonstrates it exactly - lyrics in the style in 1294 CE, the genre named in 1593.

### 3. The conflicts this session has disclosed

A hostile reader will test whether a script noticed the places where the evidence disagrees with itself. Six are recorded in this session, and each should be reported rather than smoothed.

| Conflict | The two positions | How to handle it |
|---|---|---|
| **The *Sangita Ratnakara* attribution** | The treatise bank gives it to **Sarngadeva, 13th century**; the patron bank lists a **Sangeet Ratnakar** among **Maharana Kumbha's** works | Attribute to **Sarngadeva**; if listing Kumbha's output, name **Sangeet Raja** and **Sangeet Mimansa** |
| **Malkauns's rasa** | The rasa table gives Malkauns as the example of **Raudra**, anger; the raga-time table gives Malkaush the mood **Vir or brave** | Report both; state mood attribution as convention, not property |
| **The performance-time rule** | Bhatkhande related correct performance time to **thaat and vadi**; other authorities in the same literature hold that the **time of a raga has no importance**, especially in learning, teaching or meditation | Report the dispute; do not adjudicate |
| **Carnatic improvisation** | The difference table records **less scope for improvisation**; the same body of material describes alapana, neraval, kalpana swara, tanam and ragam-tanam-pallavi at length | Convert the claim into one about **codification of style**, and say so |
| **Teejanbai's Padma Shri year** | The core source records **1987**; the official record gives **1988** | Report the conflict, or use the undisputed 2003 and 2019 awards |
| **The national academy's founding year** | The standing source records **1952**; a dated 2026 report gives **1953** | Report both, or write "the first national academy for the performing arts, set up in the early 1950s" |

A seventh item is not a conflict but a **recorded silence**, and it is handled the same way: the **Dhamar, Chaturang, Ragasagar and Sargam** styles are named among the ten Hindustani styles and are not separately described. Naming them is supported; describing them is not.

### 4. The safe formulations, written out

These are the sentences to reuse. Each replaces a common overstatement with a defensible claim that carries the same examinable content.

| Instead of | Write |
|---|---|
| "Amir Khusrau invented the sitar, tabla, khayal and qawwali." | "Amir Khusrau is credited in the tradition with introducing the ghazal, tarana and trivat, with the invention of the sitar, and with the origin of khayal; his credit as originator of qawwali is itself recorded as widely disputed." |
| "Hindustani music is Persian and Carnatic music is pure Indian." | "The two traditions record different histories of external contact, but both descend from a shared theoretical foundation, exchanged repertoire and classification, and in some cases shared patrons." |
| "Carnatic music has little improvisation." | "Carnatic practice is more codified in style, but not less improvisatory: manodharma - alapana, neraval, kalpana swara, tanam and ragam-tanam-pallavi - occupies most of a concert." |
| "Ragas create particular emotions." | "The tradition associates ragas with rasas, hours and seasons; these are recorded conventions of performance practice, and the attributions vary between formulations." |
| "Aurangzeb banned music." | "Singing was removed from the imperial court while instrumental music continued, royal women and nobles patronised music, Aurangzeb himself played the veena and substantial music literature was produced." |
| "A gharana is a school of music." | "A gharana is a musical lineage based on style and ideology that shapes both teaching and performance, and it functioned as a structure concentrating a scarce skill in a household attached to a patron." |
| "Folk music is simpler than classical music." | "Classical and folk traditions differ in function and transmission - codified theory and long apprenticeship against community transmission and occasion-bound performance - and the influence has run in both directions, as tappa's passage from camel-riders' songs to the court shows." |
| "Thaats and melakartas are the same thing." | "Both are parent-scale systems built on the same twelve pitches with Sa and Pa fixed, and Bhatkhande modelled his on the melakarta scheme; but a thaat is not a raga and is not sung, while a melakarta is a sampurna raga from which janya ragas are derived." |

### 5. Constructing the answer

**The universal opening.** Every music answer should begin by naming the **system level** it is operating at - theory, form, transmission, instrument or institution - and then state a thesis at that level. A first sentence that says "Indian music is one of the world's oldest traditions" has used its most valuable line on an unevidenced claim.

**Mark-scaled structures.**

| Marks | Structure |
|---|---|
| **10** | Thesis at the correct system level -> the demanded distinction stated precisely with its technical terms -> two named forms, gharanas, treatises or instruments with their specific contributions -> one qualification -> verdict |
| **10 (instrument classification)** | The four-fold **Tata-Sushira-Avanaddha-Ghana** scheme named in Sanskrit and in Western terms -> the sound mechanism that defines each -> two or three named instruments per family with tradition and region -> the membrane test separating Avanaddha from Ghana -> the family-versus-genre discipline -> verdict |
| **15** | Thesis -> theory spine, swara to thaat or melakarta to raga to tala -> tradition comparison on four axes -> the gharana or patronage mechanism -> one dated institutional anchor -> verdict |
| **15 (composer and patronage comparison)** | Thesis on **who paid and who was praised** -> the Carnatic composer bank with dates, forms and patrons -> the Hindustani court and gharana bank -> the kriti-and-sahitya against khayal-and-gharana form contrast -> the institutional-afterlife contrast, festival against lineage -> verdict |
| **20** | All of the 15-mark structure -> **plus** treatise chronology by contribution -> the medieval patronage case with its correction -> the folk-classical relationship without hierarchy -> modern institutionalisation with its costs -> verdict answering the exact wording |
| **20 (patronage across both traditions)** | The three-axis patronage table run in full -> temple and empire against court -> the ruler-composer cases -> the Aurangzeb correction -> what replaced patronage after the courts -> the safeguarding instruments and their limits -> verdict |

**The five directive demands, decoded.**

| If the question says | It is testing | Do not write |
|---|---|---|
| *Compare Hindustani and Carnatic music* | Grammar, classification system, improvisation format, repertoire and patronage ecology - **and the similarities** | "One is North Indian and the other South Indian" |
| *Discuss the gharana system* | Transmission as a patronage and labour structure, not a style label | "A gharana is a school of music" |
| *Classify Indian musical instruments* | The four-fold scheme, its Sanskrit names and its sound mechanisms | "String, wind and percussion" |
| *Music and patronage across political change* | Redirection of patronage - court, household, sabha, concert hall, recording, state recognition | "Aurangzeb banned music" |
| *Treatises and the theory of Indian music* | Which text contributed which concept | A list of titles and authors |

### Examination use

This lesson is the method layer, and it is what converts the preceding eighteen lessons into marks. Its directly usable content is the safe-formulation table, the mark-scaled structures and the directive decoder. Its examinable content is the attribution list, the three chronologies and the six disclosed conflicts - each of which is a place where an examiner can distinguish a script that has read carefully from one that has memorised a summary.

### Traps introduced here

- Asserting an attribution that the standing material itself hedges.
- Supplying an invention date for a form or an instrument because the topic supplies so many other dates.
- Smoothing over a recorded conflict instead of reporting it.
- Opening an answer with an evaluative claim about antiquity or greatness.
- Answering a comparison demand without similarities.
- Writing a treatise answer as a list of titles and authors.

### Revision notes

1. Attribution ladder: contemporaneous source -> recorded dispute -> recorded hedge -> refuse an invention date.
2. **Amir Khusrau** is credited with the ghazal, tarana and trivat, the **sitar**, the **Sehtar**, the origin of **khayal** and the **tarana style**, and as **Father of Qawwali** - the last **widely disputed** in the same material.
3. **Tansen created several new ragas**; this is not invention of the raga system. **Purandara Dasa** as founding father or **Pitamaha** is an honorific about pedagogy and repertoire. **Annamacharya** is the **first known** Carnatic composer.
4. Three chronologies: **text dates** (reliable), **person and patronage dates** (reliable), **form and instrument dates** (not to be supplied).
5. The transferable rule: **the earliest source containing a form and the earliest source naming it are different facts** - dhrupad's **1294 CE** lyrics against its **1593** naming.
6. Six disclosed conflicts: *Sangita Ratnakara* attribution; Malkauns's rasa; the performance-time rule; the Carnatic improvisation claim; Teejanbai's Padma Shri year; the national academy's founding year.
7. Recorded silence: **Dhamar, Chaturang, Ragasagar and Sargam** are named among the ten styles and not described.
8. Eight safe formulations for the eight commonest overstatements.
9. Mark-scaled structures for 10, 15 and 20 marks, with two specialised 10- and 15-mark variants and a specialised 20-mark patronage variant.
10. Five directive decodings, each with the phrase not to write.
11. Universal opening rule: name the system level - theory, form, transmission, instrument or institution - and state a thesis at that level.

### Concept check

**Question:** An examiner has written "Amir Khusrau, the inventor of the sitar, tabla, khayal and qawwali, transformed Indian music in the thirteenth century." Give the corrected sentence and justify every change you make.

**Model answer:** A corrected sentence: "Amir Khusrau (1253-1325) is credited in the tradition with introducing the ghazal, tarana and trivat, with the invention of the sitar, and with the origin of the khayal style; his credit as originator of qawwali is itself recorded as widely disputed, and he is separately believed to have invented a three-stringed instrument called the *Sehtar*. His period marks a significant phase in the formation of North Indian musical practice, though the invention dates of the tabla and sitar remain uncertain." Five changes, each justified. "Inventor" becomes "credited with", because the standing material itself uses the attributive forms "attributed to" for khayal and "it is believed" for the Sehtar. The **tabla is removed** from the list: no source in this session's evidence base credits him with it, and the invention dates of the tabla and sitar are recorded as uncertain. The **qawwali claim is retained with its dispute**, because the same sentence that records the credit records that it has been widely disputed - omitting the dispute misrepresents the evidence in the direction the examiner is testing. The **ghazal claim is made precise**: he is recorded as **one of the first expounders** of the art of ghazal in India, and the ghazal itself is recorded as originating in Iran in the tenth century and reaching South Asia in the twelfth, so he cannot be its inventor. And **"transformed Indian music"** is narrowed to North Indian practice, because nothing in the material attaches his influence to the southern tradition.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that hedged language is weak language. In this topic the hedge **is** the content: a script that writes "credited with, though disputed" has demonstrated that it knows the state of the evidence, which is exactly what the examiner set the trap to discover.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Claims of individual invention in Indian music should be treated with source-critical caution." Discuss with examples. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is sound, and its justification lies in how the tradition was transmitted.

Indian music was carried orally and through household lineages, so origins that cannot be recovered are attached to memorable figures. The pattern is visible in the material itself: **Amir Khusrau (1253-1325)** is credited with introducing the ghazal, tarana and trivat, with inventing the sitar, with originating khayal, with inventing a three-stringed *Sehtar*, and with fathering qawwali - six origination claims for one lifetime. The literature's own caution is decisive: his credit as originator of **qawwali is recorded as widely disputed**, and the khayal claim is recorded as "attributed to" and the Sehtar as "it is believed". Where the evidence hedges, the answer must hedge.

Three further instances confirm the discipline. **The invention dates of the tabla and the sitar remain uncertain**, so none should be supplied. **Tansen is recorded as having created several new ragas** - a claim about composition, not about the origin of the raga system. And **Purandara Dasa** is called the founding father and **Pitamaha** of Carnatic music, an honorific about pedagogy and repertoire rather than a claim of invention, just as **Annamacharya** is the **first known** Carnatic composer, where "known" limits the claim.

The contrast case shows what good evidence looks like: dhrupad's earliest surviving lyrics are dated to **Yugala Shataka, 1294 CE**, and the genre is first named in **Abu Fazl's *Ain-i-Akbari*, 1593** - two datable textual anchors, three centuries apart, neither of which is an invention date.

The qualification is that caution is not denial: the attributions are the recorded tradition and are examinable as such.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the oral-transmission explanation of legend-formation 3 + the Amir Khusrau cluster with at least four claims and the recorded dispute 5 + three further instances of inflated attribution correctly limited 4 + the dhrupad double-anchor as a contrast case 2 + the caution-is-not-denial qualification 1.**

## The exact owned Preliminary items

### 2026, Preliminary examination, General Studies Paper I - raga equivalence

> **1.** Which one of the following Carnatic music ragas is similar to Raga Bilawal in Hindustani music?
>
> (a) Nat Bhairavi
> (b) Kamavardhini
> (c) Hanumatodi
> (d) Dheera Shankarabharanam

*(Wording and response choices read directly from the locally held official Set-A paper. The scan's text layer degrades the label of the second option, which is recorded here as printed in the response list; no other reconstruction has been made.)*

**Key status.** A **provisional** 2026 Set-A key is held locally. **No answer letter is recorded or inferred here**, and no option is marked, eliminated or corrected.

**Demand.** The item tests whether a candidate knows that the two classificatory systems, built on the same twelve pitches with Shadja and Panchama fixed, produce corresponding parent scales, and whether the candidate can identify a specific correspondence rather than guess from a shared syllable.

**What this session supplies.** Lesson 5 teaches the correspondence question in full: that Bhatkhande modelled the thaat scheme on the Carnatic melakarta classification; that a correspondence therefore exists at the level of the **parent scale and not of the raga**; that five correspondences are established within this session's verification - **Bilawal with Dheera Shankarabharanam, Bhairav with Mayamalavagowla, Kafi with Kharaharapriya, Bhairavi with Hanumatodi, and Poorvi with Kamavardhini, also called Pantuvarali**; and that the shared-syllable trap is the Hindustani **Todi** thaat, whose Carnatic counterpart is **not** Hanumatodi, since Hanumatodi corresponds to Hindustani **Bhairavi**. Lesson 5 also records that the remaining five thaats were **not** assigned counterparts within the bounded check and are therefore left unstated.

Lesson 11 also teaches the general rule the item depends on: **gharana affiliation follows training lineage, not birthplace, mother tongue or region of residence**. No option is marked here and no answer letter is recorded; the teaching is supplied and the key is left where it stands.

**What this session supplies, and the unresolved point.** Lesson 14 carries the complete recorded Tansen entry: a **dhrupad master** of the sixteenth century, one of Akbar's **Navratna**, who **created several new ragas**, whose compositions were mainly related to **Hindu gods and goddesses and his patrons**, who was patronised **first by Raja Ramchandra of Rewa and later by Akbar**, whose works include ***Sangita Sara*** and ***Sri Ganesh Stotra***, whose **tomb is at Gwalior**, and to whom **the title "Mian" was given by Akbar**. ⚠️ **A genuine tension must be recorded rather than resolved.** The owned material supports the substance of more than one of the four printed statements - it states both that the title **Mian** was given by Akbar and that Tansen **created several new ragas** - so the item's intended discrimination cannot be settled from the evidence base this session uses. Lesson 19's attribution discipline is the relevant teaching: **"created several new ragas" is a claim about composition and must not be inflated into invention**, and the personal name against the conferred title is exactly the kind of distinction a negative stem is built on. **No option is marked and no answer is inferred.**

**What this session supplies.** Lesson 14 carries the exact material each statement turns on. **Annamacharya is dated 1408-1503**, is recorded as the **first known Carnatic composer**, composed **sankirtanas in praise of Lord Venkateswara**, was **patronised by Vijayanagara rulers** and is remembered as the **"Grandfather of Telugu songwriting"**. **Tyagaraja** is recorded only as a saint-composer whose **samadhi at Thiruvaiyaru** is commemorated by the **Tyagaraja Aradhana**, and as one of the **Trinity of Carnatic music** with **Muthuswami Dikshitar and Syama Sastri**; **no birth or death years are recorded for any of the three**, and **no composition, raga or addressee is attributed to Tyagaraja in the owned material**. Lesson 14 also states the prohibitions this item exists to test: **do not make Tyagaraja and Annamacharya contemporaries**, since Annamacharya's dates are fifteenth-century and the Trinity belongs to a much later period; and **do not supply dates for any member of the Trinity**. No statement is marked true or false here and no answer letter is recorded.

### CSE 2012 - Dhrupad

> With reference to Dhrupad, one of the major traditions of India that has been kept alive for centuries, which of the following statements are correct?
>
> (i) Dhrupad originated and was developed in the Rajput kingdoms during the Mughal period.
> (ii) Dhrupad is primarily a devotional and spiritual music.
> (iii) Dhrupad Alap uses Sanskrit syllables from mantras.
>
> Select the correct answer using the codes given below:
>
> (a) (i) and (ii)
> (b) (ii) and (iii)
> (c) (i), (ii) and (iii)
> (d) None of these

**Demand and what this session supplies.** A three-statement item on the origin, character and technique of a single form. Lesson 8 carries each element: dhrupad's **temple origin**, its **mention in the *Natyashastra***, its evolution from ***Prabandha* and *Dhruvapada***, its **consolidation as a classical form in the thirteenth century** and its **zenith at Akbar's court**; its **use of Sanskrit syllables**; and the fact that it **begins with a wordless Alap** in which the tempo gradually rises to evoke the raga's mood. Lesson 8 also supplies the dating discipline the first statement depends on - the difference between the earliest source that **contains** the style, *Yugala Shataka* of **1294 CE**, and the earliest that **names** it, the ***Ain-i-Akbari*** of **1593**. No statement is marked true or false here.

## The reproduced Main Examination items

All five are reproduced from the core art source's own list of previous years' Main Examination questions, with that source's year attributions. **No official paper is held locally for any of them, no marks or word limit is printed in that list, and none is supplied here. No model answer is given for any of them.**

### CSE 2012 - the classification of instruments

> **What are the groups into which musical instruments in India have traditionally been classified?**

**Directive.** A direct descriptive demand for a classification, with the word *traditionally* excluding the Western three-way division.

**Conceptual demand.** The four Sanskrit families, their Western equivalents and the mechanism that defines each - not a list of instruments.

**Answer approach.** Run Lesson 17. Name the four families in Sanskrit with their Western equivalents and quote the recorded mechanism for each: **Tata Vadya**, chordophones, string instruments, with the three sub-types bowed, plectral and struck; **Sushira Vadya**, aerophones, all the wind instruments; **Avanaddha Vadya**, membranophones, percussion instruments which one has to strike; **Ghana Vadya**, idiophones, non-drum percussion instruments which do not require tuning. Give two or three named instruments per family with tradition and region, keeping tabla with Hindustani and mridangam with Carnatic. State the membrane as the discriminator between the two struck families, and the struck chordophones - gotuvadyam and swaramandal - as proof that the vibrating body rather than the method of striking fixes the family. Close on the family-versus-genre discipline. No model answer is supplied here.

### CSE 2009 - the two classical styles

> **What are the important similarities and differences between the Hindustani and Carnatic styles of classical music?**

**Directive.** *Similarities and differences* - a two-sided demand in which an answer of differences alone forfeits half the marks.

**Conceptual demand.** The shared theoretical foundation and the specific grammatical divergences, stated without converting the comparison into an opposition.

**Answer approach.** Run Lesson 13. Open on the shared root: both traced to Bharata's *Natyashastra*, diverging in the fourteenth century, with the names first attested in Haripala's *Sangeeta Sudhakara*; both based on raga and tala; the *Sangita Ratnakara* definitive for both; flute and violin common to both. Then take the differences on named axes: ten thaats that are not sung against seventy-two sampurna melakartas that are, with janya derivation; named tala cycles with teen-tal at sixteen beats against laghu, dhrutam and anudhrutam generating one hundred and seventy-five talas; alap, taan and layakari against alapana, neraval, kalpana swara and tanam; dhrupad, khayal and thumri against kriti, varnam, padam and tillana; the gharana against guru-shishya parampara and a composer-centred canon; court networks against temple and sabha networks. Handle the two recorded claims explicitly rather than repeating them - the influence row as contact history and not essence, and the "less scope for improvisation" row as codification of style. No model answer is supplied here.

### CSE 1997 - khayal

> **Write about Khayal music.**

**Directive and scale.** A short-form *write about* demand. No marks or word limit is printed in the reproduction and none is supplied here.

**Answer approach.** At short length only the spine fits. Run Lesson 9: the word from Persian meaning idea or imagination; the origin attributed to Amir Khusrau; the *bandish* of two to eight lines and the consequence that each singer renders the same bandish differently while keeping text and raga constant; the frequent use of taans and the consequent reduction of alap relative to dhrupad; the two-song design of bada khyal in slow tempo, comprising most of the performance, and chhota khyal in fast tempo; the romantic thematic range; the most generous fifteenth-century patronage by Hussain Shah of the Jaunpur Sultanate and the seventeenth-century emergence of the style; and two or three named gharanas with their distinguishing emphases. Omit the full gharana table unless length allows. No model answer is supplied here.

### CSE 1990 - the characteristics of classical music

> **What are the chief characteristics of classical music?**

**Directive.** A general descriptive demand whose difficulty is its breadth; the scoring turns on choosing a frame rather than listing impressions.

**Answer approach.** Use the definitional contrast rather than an adjective list. Classical traditions are marked by **codified theory** - the *Lakshanagranthas*, the three pillars of raga, tala and swara, twenty-two shrutis, parent-scale classification - by **long apprenticeship**, through gurukul and later gharana or guru-shishya parampara, and by **concert or temple settings**. Set that against the recorded folk contrast - the free voice of the people, community transmission, occasion-bound performance - without ranking the two, and note the recorded intermingling that produces the fusion forms. Then supply the internal architecture: raga with its aaroha, avaroha, vadi, samvadi and pakad; tala with its cycle and laya; and the improvisational apparatus of each tradition. No model answer is supplied here.

### CSE 1982 - an instrument identification

> **Which instrument does Ustad Amjad Ali Khan play?**

**Directive and scale.** A one-line identification item.

**Recorded limit, stated openly.** ⚠️ **This session's evidence base does not name Ustad Amjad Ali Khan, and does not assign him an instrument.** The instrument classification of Lesson 17 does not include the sarod in its four-family table; the instrument appears in the core source only as a picture caption within the chapter, unattached to any performer. **No answer is supplied here**, and none should be reconstructed from memory. What the session does supply is the method the item belongs to: Lesson 17's family-and-mechanism classification, and Lesson 11's prohibition on attaching a musician to an instrument, a gharana or an honour the sources do not attach them to.

### 2024, Preliminary examination, General Studies Paper I - the intangible-heritage item

**Ledger record.** 2024, question **60**, recorded demand "Latest Indian inclusion in the UNESCO Intangible Cultural Heritage List", routed to the **dance owner** in this subject. An official 2024 key is held locally and **no answer letter is recorded or inferred**. The item's wording is not reproduced here, because it is not owned by this topic.

**Why it is recorded, and what this topic contributes.** It is recorded because the intangible-heritage boundary is a live one for a music answer. What this topic supplies is the **national** instrument and its limits: the first national academy for the performing arts, set up in 1952, with a remit across music, dance and theatre, including traditional, folk and tribal forms and puppetry, and allied performing arts, and with the stated task of promoting the enormous intangible heritage of India as demonstrated through its music, dance and drama. Lesson 18 states the discipline explicitly: **UNESCO intangible-cultural-heritage inscription is a separate international instrument conferred by a different body under different criteria, and it must never be equated with national academy recognition.** Inscription lists, award lists and recognition policy are routed to the heritage and cultural-policy owner.
