# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

## Block A - The material, the melody and the time, Lessons 1-4

**A1. Question:** Reconstruct the route from an unbroken sound to a performable raga, naming every intervening concept, and say which step is the selection and which is the ranking.

**Model answer:** The route has five steps. The octave is first counted into **twenty-two shrutis**, the smallest audible gradations of pitch, a count fixed in Bharata's *Natyashastra* and confirmed in *Dattilam* as the only humanly possible sounds. Of those twenty-two, **twelve are described as audible**, and those twelve comprise the **seven suddha and five vikrit swaras** - this is the **selection**. The seven suddha swaras are named and given functions, Shadja as tonic through Nishada as subtonic, with **Shadja and Panchama fixed as achala** and the remaining five variable as **chala**; taken together they form the **saptak** or **sargam**. From the older tonal apparatus - the two **grams**, Shadja and Madhyama, each generating **seven murchhanas**, and the **eighteen jatis** of the *Natya Shastra* defined by note-count and by arrangement in ascent and descent - the **raga** emerges, defined by its **arohana and avarohana**, by its **vadi, samvadi, anuvadi and vivadi**, and by its **pakad or chalan**. The assignment of vadi and samvadi is the **ranking**: within a raga the notes are not equal, the samvadi standing generally a fourth or a fifth from the vadi. Selection makes the material finite; ranking makes the melody characteristic; and the pakad makes it identifiable.

**A2. Question:** Explain why the independence of tala from melody is a stronger fact than any list of tala names, and evidence the claim from both traditions.

**Model answer:** Because it is the fact from which the rest follows. Tala is the rhythmic grouping of beats into cycles ranging from three to one hundred and eight beats, and its recorded uniqueness is that **it is independent of the music that accompanies it and has its own divisions**. Independence means the cycle is a parallel structure rather than a derivative of the melody, so a performer can depart from the line across several beats and resolve on the first beat of the cycle - there is something fixed to return to. A list of names cannot generate that argument. The evidence runs on both sides. Hindustani practice names whole cycles - dadra, kaharba, rupak, ektal, jhaptal, teen-tal at sixteen beats, chautal - and records that although musicologists have argued for more than a hundred talas, only about thirty are known and only ten to twelve are actually used; *layakari*, rhythmic improvisation, is the named specialisation of the Darbhanga dhrupad lineage. Carnatic practice specifies components - **laghu, dhrutam and anudhrutam** - and generates **thirty-five original talas, each split into five ghatis, making one hundred and seventy-five**, with **aditala** most prominent; *Swara-Kalpana* is improvised with a drummer at medium and fast paces. Two methods, one principle. And *laya*, the tempo of the tala, is what varies in the bada-to-chhota khayal design, not the cycle.

**A3. Question:** State the difference between a thaat and a melakarta at four levels, and explain why the difference makes the two numbers non-comparable.

**Model answer:** At the level of **status**: a thaat is a scale that carries no vaadi or samvaadi, has no emotional quality and **is not sung**, while a melakarta is itself a raga. At the level of **completeness**: a thaat must contain seven of the twelve notes in **ascending order only**, while a melakarta is *sampurna*, using all seven notes in **both ascent and descent**. At the level of **derivation**: Hindustani ragas are said to **belong to** a thaat, a relation of grouping, while janya ragas are **produced from** a melakarta by omitting notes or by **vakra** movement, a relation of operation, as Mayamalavagowla gives rise to Malahari. At the level of **naming**: thaats are named after popular ragas, while ragas are named after the emotion they evoke. The numbers are therefore non-comparable because they count different things - ten **selected** parent scales against seventy-two **generated** parent ragas. Both schemes operate on twelve pitches with Shadja and Panchama fixed, so the arithmetic is shared; Bhatkhande modelled his classification on the melakarta system, his analysis yielded thirty-two possible thaats, and he chose to highlight ten as those prominent in his time.

## Block B - The repertoire and its carriers, Lessons 7-11

**B1. Question:** Write the four-sentence account of the fourteenth-century divergence that an examiner cannot fault, and identify the single piece of evidence that does most of the work.

**Model answer:** "The historical roots of both Indian classical traditions are traced to Bharata's *Natyashastra*, and they diverged in the fourteenth century. The earliest attestation of the two names is Haripala's *Sangeeta Sudhakara*, a fourteenth-century text in which the terms Carnatic and Hindustani are found for the first time. Sarangadeva's thirteenth-century *Sangita Ratnakara*, which defined two hundred and sixty-four ragas and classified microtones, is regarded as a definitive text by both traditions, and therefore stands before the separation. Subsequent divergence was of practice, ornament, classification and repertoire, not of theoretical foundation." The evidence that does most of the work is the ***Sangita Ratnakara***'s dual status. A date for the divergence can be asserted or disputed, but a single thirteenth-century text that **both** later traditions treat as definitive is direct evidence that the separation occurred downstream of the theory, which is precisely the claim the paragraph makes.

**B2. Question:** Explain how dhrupad and khayal differ on five recorded variables, and show that the differences form one coherent pattern rather than a list.

**Model answer:** On **etymology and character**: dhrupad from *dhruva*, fixed, and *pada*, verse, against khayal from a Persian word meaning idea or imagination. On **opening**: dhrupad begins with a long wordless alap in which the tempo gradually rises to evoke the raga's mood, while in khayal alap is given much less room and taans are used frequently. On **the composed text**: dhrupad compositions usually have four to five stanzas, while a khayal bandish is a short song of two to eight lines. On **rendering**: dhrupad's text is fixed, while each khayal singer renders the same bandish differently, keeping text and raga constant. On **theme and accompaniment**: dhrupad uses Sanskrit syllables, is of temple origin, is performed by a duo of generally two male vocalists with tanpura and pakhawaj, while khayal themes are usually romantic and may praise God or a king, and its performance is designed as a slow **bada khyal** comprising most of it and a fast **chhota khyal**. The pattern is single and coherent: every variable moves attention from the **composition** to the **performer**. A shorter text, less wordless elaboration, more display, a wider emotional range and a rendering that varies with the singer all point the same way - which is why khayal's rise coincides with the gharana becoming the organising institution of North Indian music.

**B3. Question:** Set out the gharana's classification principles and show that the naming principle itself carries information.

**Model answer:** There are three. The dhrupad lineages are named by **vani**, a manner of delivery: **Dagar Vani** in the Dagar gharana of Delhi and UP, **Khandar and Gauhar Vani** in the Darbhanga gharana of Bihar, **Gauhar and Khandar Vani** in the Bettiah gharana of Bihar, **Khandar Vani** in the Talwandi gharana of Punjab, now in Pakistan, with the fourth vani, **Nauhar**, surviving as the recorded origin of the Agra gharana. The khayal and semi-classical gharanas are named by **place**: Gwalior, Kirana, Agra, Patiala, Bhendibazaar, with Varanasi and Lucknow for thumri. And the instrumental lineages are named by **instrument**: the sitar gharanas of **Jaipur, Varanasi and Etawah**. The naming principle carries information because it records what the lineage was organised around. A vani-named lineage was distinguished by a **style of delivery** shared across households - Khandar Vani appears in three of the four dhrupad rows - so the differences among them fall on one internal axis of the form, running from Dagar's alaap emphasis through Darbhanga's balance to Bettiah's bandish core. A place-named lineage was distinguished by **where its skill was concentrated and who patronised it**, which is why the Patiala gharana is recorded with a named patron and why the Talwandi gharana could be nearly extinguished by a territorial event.

## Block C - The systems compared, and the evidence banks, Lessons 12-16

**C1. Question:** Argue the Hindustani-Carnatic comparison through the question "who was being addressed?", and state the two recorded claims that must be handled rather than repeated.

**Model answer:** On the Carnatic side the addressee is a deity, and the patron is a temple or an empire: Annamacharya's sankirtanas praise **Venkateswara** under **Vijayanagara** patronage; Purandara Dasa is a devotee of **Krishna** under the same empire; Bhadrachala Ramadasu writes Telugu devotional compositions for **Rama**; Venkatamakhin composes twenty-four Ashtapadis for the deity **Tyagesha at Tiruvarur**. The recorded summary is that Carnatic music remains closely tied to the temple and spiritual traditions of South India, unlike Hindustani music, which flourished under Mughal courts. On the Hindustani side the addressee includes the patron: Tansen's compositions are recorded as mainly related to Hindu gods and goddesses **and his patrons**; khyal themes may praise God **or a particular king**; Lal Khan is titled by Shah Jahan and Sadarang serves Muhammad Shah. The institutional afterlife follows the addressee: a **festival at a saint's samadhi** in the south, a **lineage tied to a household** in the north. The two claims to be handled are the influence row - Arab, Persian and Afghan against indigenous, which records contact history and must not become an essence, since both descend from the *Natyashastra*, share the *Sangita Ratnakara*, exchanged classification and repertoire, and shared patrons such as Ibrahim Adil Shah II - and the freedom row, whose "less scope for improvisation" must be read as codification of style, since manodharma occupies most of a Carnatic concert.

**C2. Question:** Explain the recorded mechanism by which classical and folk music meet, and test it against a case that does not fit.

**Model answer:** The recorded mechanism is institutional: classical and folk music intermingle over time and other forms emerge which take elements from both, and **it usually takes devotional music to bring the two together, because both royalty and commoners patronise the deities**, so the songs combine both genres. A shared addressee produces a shared repertoire. The forms confirm it. The bhajan sets lyrics to simple melodies generally sung in one or more raga, accompanied by chimta, dholak, dafli and manjira - raga vocabulary with household instruments. The qawwali is composed of a single raga and performed in Sufi shrines by two lead singers with about eight members. The shabad is sung in gurudwaras, and the Guru Granth Sahib is organised by chapter in ragas. Abhanga, Bhatiali, Tevaram, Kirtan and Sopana Sangeetham complete the group. The case that does not fit is **tappa**, which originated in the folk songs of the camel riders of north-west India and gained legitimacy as a semi-classical vocal specialty when it was brought to the Mughal court of Muhammad Shah, with no devotional intermediary at all - the meeting was produced by **patronage**, not by a shared deity. **Rabindra Sangeet** is a second exception, mixing classical elements with Bengali folk strains through a single named composer. The mechanism is therefore the usual route, not the only one.

**C3. Question:** Show, using three different kinds of evidence, that the raga system operated well beyond the classical concert.

**Model answer:** First, **scriptural organisation**. The Guru Granth Sahib is recorded as organised **by chapter in ragas**, with each chapter containing many shabads of that raga - a musical category functioning as the filing principle of a sacred text. Second, **textual prescription**. Jayadeva's twelfth-century *Gita Govinda*, a song text rather than a treatise, **indicates the exact raga and tala in which to sing each of its songs**, which shows the raga-tala system operating prescriptively four centuries before Bhatkhande's thaats and Venkatamakhin's melakartas. Third, **popular and semi-classical description**. Bhajan lyrics are described as set to simple melodies **generally sung in one or more raga**; the semi-classical register is defined as using **lighter versions of ragas** such as Bhoopali or Malkaush; the qawwali is **composed of a single raga**; thumri is based on **mixed simple ragas**. A fourth kind of evidence closes the argument in the present: **Chaturprahar**, an annual classical music festival at the National Centre for Performing Arts in Mumbai, is programmed **on the concept of association of time with the ragas** - so even the *samay* convention is an operating principle of a contemporary concert calendar.

## Block D - Instruments, institutions and method, Lessons 17-19

**D1. Question:** Write the decision procedure for placing an unfamiliar Indian instrument, and state the two rules that govern its use.

**Model answer:** First, ask what vibrates. If a **string** vibrates, the instrument is **Tata Vadya**, a chordophone, and the sub-type follows from how the string is set in motion - bowed, as with sarangi, esraj and violin; plectral, as with sitar, veena and tanpura; or struck with hammers or sticks, as with gotuvadyam and swaramandal. If no string vibrates, ask whether an **enclosed air column** produces the sound; if so the instrument is **Sushira Vadya**, an aerophone, since that family includes all the wind instruments - bansuri, shehnai, pungi. If the instrument is **struck**, ask whether there is a **stretched membrane over a resonator**; if there is, it is **Avanaddha Vadya**, a membranophone, the family whose recorded definition is that one has to strike to generate musical sound - tabla for Hindustani music, mridangam for Carnatic. If it is struck and has **no membrane**, it is **Ghana Vadya**, an idiophone, recorded as non-drum percussion which does not require tuning - manjira, jaltarang, ghatam, khartal, chimta. The two governing rules are that **what vibrates fixes the family while how it is set vibrating fixes only the sub-type**, which is why a struck swaramandal remains a chordophone; and that **the procedure yields a family, a tradition and a region, never an inventor or an invention date**.

**D2. Question:** State the five-stage account of what replaced princely patronage, and evidence each stage.

**Model answer:** **Employment**: a musician retained by a court - Tansen under Raja Ramchandra of Rewa and then Akbar, who gave him his title; Sadarang as court musician of Muhammad Shah; Naubat Khan as mansabdar; Lal Khan titled Gunsamundra by Shah Jahan in 1637; the Thanjavur Quartet as court musicians of the Thanjavur Maratha court, with Vadivelu of that Quartet later at Swathi Thirunal's Travancore court. **Elite household**: the *baithaki* style of tappa, evolved under the direct patronage of the zamindari elites of the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries in their *baithak khanas* and *jalsaghar*. **Enrolment**: the Gandharva Mahavidyalaya of 1901, opened in Lahore and moved to Mumbai in 1915 with a remit covering Hindustani and Carnatic forms; Prayag Sangeet Samiti at Allahabad in 1926; Marris College at Lucknow in 1926, teaching theory as well as practice and later becoming the Bhatkhande Music Institute Deemed University - all resting on Bhatkhande's first modern treatise, his thaat system and Paluskar's raga-classification scheme. **Recognition**: the first national academy for the performing arts, set up in 1952 for music, drama and dance with the task of promoting India's intangible heritage, and the recorded Bharat Ratna sequence of 1998, 1999, 2001 and 2008 across two traditions and two instruments. **Audience**: SPIC MACAY from 1977 with free-entry events and global chapters; Chaturprahar at the National Centre for Performing Arts; and the recorded relocation of gharana lineages into urban concert, radio, teaching and recording.

**D3. Question:** List the conflicts this topic's evidence contains and state the general rule for handling them.

**Model answer:** Six. The ***Sangita Ratnakara*** is attributed to **Sarngadeva** in the treatise bank and a **Sangeet Ratnakar** is listed among **Maharana Kumbha's** works in the patron bank. **Malkauns** appears as the example of *Raudra*, anger, in the rasa table and is given the mood **brave** in the raga-time table. The **performance-time rule** is related by Bhatkhande to thaat and vadi and denied importance by other authorities in the same literature. **Carnatic improvisation** is recorded as having "less scope" in a comparison table and is described at length elsewhere in the same material through alapana, neraval, kalpana swara, tanam and ragam-tanam-pallavi. **Teejanbai's Padma Shri** is dated 1987 in the core source and 1988 in the official record. The **national academy's founding year** is 1952 in the standing source and 1953 in a dated 2026 report. A seventh item is a **recorded silence** rather than a conflict: Dhamar, Chaturang, Ragasagar and Sargam are named among the ten Hindustani styles and not described. The general rule is the same in every case: **report both positions, state which is being used and why, and never resolve a conflict by choosing the more convenient formulation**. Where a silence exists, name what is supported - membership of the list - and refuse what is not - a structure for the form.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

> These six questions are original to this session. None reproduces or closely restates a recorded official demand; the recorded demands and their answer approaches are kept separately in the PYQ section above, without model answers.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

**"The word *shruti* does more work in Indian music than the word *note*." Examine.**

### Model

The claim holds, because the two words operate at different levels of the system.

A *note* names a usable pitch. *Shruti* names the **smallest audible gradation of pitch, representing the quality of frequency**, out of which notes are selected: the seven basic swaras are derived from specific groupings of shrutis. The counting is fixed and textual - Bharata divided the swaras into a scale of twenty-two, and *Dattilam* confirmed twenty-two shrutis per octave as the only humanly possible sounds - of which twelve are described as audible, comprising seven *suddha* and five *vikrit* swaras.

Three consequences follow that "note" cannot carry. The system is explicitly a **selection** from a finer measurement, so its seven names are a choice rather than a discovery. Because Shadja and Panchama are fixed as *achala* and the other five vary as *chala*, the scale is movable yet anchored. And the surviving microtonal space is what ornament - *gamaka*, the subject of a thirteenth-century treatise - actually inhabits.

The qualification is that only twelve of the twenty-two are described as audible, so shruti is a measuring unit rather than a performing one.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the shruti-versus-swara definition with the frequency-quality wording 3 + the twenty-two count with both textual authorities 2 + the selection, movable-tonic and ornament consequences 4 + the audible-twelve qualification 1.**

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

**Assess the value of the *pakad* in defining a raga.**

### Model

The *pakad* or *chalan* is the characteristic phrase or pattern that **identifies** a raga, and its value is that it is the only element of the raga's anatomy that performs identification rather than description.

The other elements describe. *Arohana* and *avarohana* give the ascending and descending order of notes; *vadi* and *samvadi* give the most and second-most important notes, the latter generally a fourth or fifth from the former; *anuvadi* gives subsidiary notes and *vivadi* the foreign ones used sparingly. Two ragas can coincide on all of these and remain distinct, because a raga is defined as a melodic form with its own peculiar movement rather than as a set of notes.

The consequence is structural: the number of ragas is not limited by the number of arithmetically possible note-sets. It also explains why a raga is described as neither a scale nor a mode - a scale has no identifying phrase.

The qualification is that the pakad works with the other elements, not instead of them; it identifies what the rest has already shaped.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the pakad defined as an identifier with the alternative term 2 + the other anatomy elements shown to be descriptive 3 + the consequence for the number of possible ragas 3 + the neither-scale-nor-mode link 1 + the works-with-not-instead-of qualification 1.**

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

**"Indian music's institutional history is a history of solving the transmission problem." Discuss.**

### Model

The proposition is sound if "transmission problem" is read as the question of how a skill held in persons survives the loss of the persons or their patrons.

The first solution was **residence**. The gurukul or hermitage system embodied the guru-shishya relationship: students lived in the hermitage for twelve years, gaining knowledge by serving their master, the hermitages receiving patronage from kings and wealthy persons, with prince and commoner treated alike. Knowledge passed by direct experience and was not written down.

The second was **lineage**. A gharana - a musical lineage based on style and ideology, shaping teaching and performance - concentrated a scarce skill in a household, attached it to a patron, and reproduced it by descent or long apprenticeship. It solved transmission efficiently and created a new vulnerability: the Talwandi gharana is recorded as nearly extinct after historical decline and partition, and tappa as facing extinction with very few exponents left.

The third was **writing and enrolment**. Bhatkhande composed the first modern treatise on Hindustani classical music and introduced the thaat system; Paluskar introduced a scheme of raga classification. On that base came schools: the Gandharva Mahavidyalaya in 1901, opened in Lahore and moved to Mumbai in 1915; Prayag Sangeet Samiti at Allahabad in 1926; and Marris College at Lucknow in 1926, teaching theory as well as practice and later becoming a deemed university.

The fourth was **public institution**: a national academy for the performing arts in 1952, and outreach from 1977 building an audience.

The qualification is that each solution cost something - writing standardised what lineages had kept plural.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the transmission problem defined rather than assumed 2 + the gurukul with its twelve-year residence and no-discrimination clause 3 + the gharana as solution and vulnerability with Talwandi and tappa as evidence 4 + notation and the three schools with exact years and cities 4 + the academy and outreach stage 1 + the standardisation cost 1.**

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

**"Folk, semi-classical and classical are registers of one musical culture rather than three separate musics." Examine.**

### Model

The proposition is well supported, provided "register" is understood as a difference of treatment and setting rather than of substance.

Three kinds of evidence support it. **Movement between registers is documented in both directions.** Tappa originated in the folk songs of the camel riders of north-west India and became a semi-classical vocal specialty when it was brought to the Mughal court of Muhammad Shah - an upward passage. Dhrupad is of temple origin and reached its zenith at a court. Hori appears simultaneously among the ten Hindustani styles, among the lighter forms for which thumri is a generic name, and as a Radha-Krishna Holi folk song of Uttar Pradesh.

**The shared apparatus is the raga.** Semi-classical music is defined as still based on swara, using lighter versions of ragas such as Bhoopali or Malkaush, a lighter tala and a faster laya, with more weight on bhava and lyrics. Bhajan lyrics are set to simple melodies generally sung in one or more raga; qawwali is composed of a single raga.

**The stated mechanism is institutional.** Classical and folk music intermingle over time because devotional music brings them together, both royalty and commoners patronising the deities.

The qualification is that the registers are genuinely distinct in function and transmission: codified theory, long apprenticeship and concert or temple settings on one side; community transmission, occasion-bound performance and regional language on the other. The distinction is real; the hierarchy is not.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the register framing stated as treatment rather than substance 2 + two-way movement with tappa, dhrupad and the hori case 5 + the shared raga apparatus with the semi-classical definition 4 + the devotional mechanism 2 + the function-and-transmission qualification without hierarchy 2.**

## Question 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

**"In Indian music, classification schemes tell us more about their makers than about the music." Critically examine.**

### Model

The proposition is provocative and half true; testing it requires separating what a scheme records from what it decides.

The case for it is real. Bhatkhande's analysis yielded **thirty-two possible thaats**, and he chose to highlight **ten** - a selection governed by what was popular in his time, not by the arithmetic. The Carnatic scheme takes the opposite decision, generating all **seventy-two** *sampurna* parent ragas by rule, many of them theoretical rather than current. The **raga-ragini** system, one of the oldest, arranges five or six main ragas with six raginis each and offspring called ragaputras - a familial metaphor that says more about an inherited aesthetic imagination than about melodic generation. The **rasa** scheme carries an empty cell, Bibhatsya being recorded as rarely used in practice, which is what one expects of a dramaturgical vocabulary applied to melody; and Malkauns appears as *Raudra* in one table and as brave in another.

The case against it is equally strong. Classifications constrain practice. **Ragas are named after the emotion they evoke and thaats after popular ragas**, so the scheme records real usage. The *Guru Granth Sahib* is organised **by chapter in ragas**, and Jayadeva's *Gita Govinda* prescribes **the exact raga and tala** for each song. Chaturprahar programmes a modern festival on the *samay* system.

The verdict: schemes are **interpretations with consequences**. Their boundaries reveal their makers; their operation shapes the music. Treating them as neutral descriptions is the error the proposition usefully corrects.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the record-versus-decide distinction as the thesis 3 + the thirty-two-to-ten selection and the seventy-two generation contrasted 5 + the raga-ragini metaphor and the empty Bibhatsya cell with the Malkauns instability 4 + the counter-case from the naming rule, the scriptural organisation and the Gita Govinda prescription 5 + a verdict naming schemes as interpretations with consequences 3.**

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

**Evaluate the proposition that Indian music has always adapted its patron rather than its substance.**

### Model

The proposition is largely borne out, with two significant limits.

The adaptation record is strong. Under Aurangzeb, **singing was removed from the imperial court while instrumental music continued, royal women and nobles patronised music, the emperor himself played the veena, and substantial music literature was produced**; under Muhammad Shah, **Sadarang and Adarang strengthened khayal**. Regional patronage substituted for imperial: **Hussain Shah of the Jaunpur Sultanate** gave khayal its most generous fifteenth-century patronage, and **Man Singh Tomar** advanced Gwalior as a centre of music and compiled the *Mankautuhal*. Below the court, the **baithaki** style of tappa survived in the *baithak khanas* and *jalsaghar* of the zamindari elite. After the princely states, gharana lineages moved into **urban concert, radio, teaching and recording**, while the Carnatic tradition kept the **Tyagaraja Aradhana** at Thiruvaiyaru as an annual professional gathering at a saint's samadhi. The state then supplied **recognition** rather than employment, the recorded Bharat Ratna sequence of 1998, 1999, 2001 and 2008 spanning two traditions and two instruments.

The first limit is that adaptation is not guaranteed: **Talwandi** is nearly extinct after decline and partition, and **tappa** is recorded as facing extinction with very few exponents. The second is that substance did change - dhrupad declined in the eighteenth century as khayal rose, the khayal bandish is a short song of two to eight lines against dhrupad's four to five stanzas, and modern notation and pedagogy standardised what lineages had kept plural.

The verdict: the tradition survives by changing patron **and** by changing form; the proposition is true of institutions and only partly true of music.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the Aurangzeb correction with all four components 4 + regional, household and post-princely substitution with named patrons and venues 5 + the Carnatic festival and the state-recognition stage with the dated sequence 4 + the Talwandi and tappa limit 3 + the substance-changed limit with the dhrupad-to-khayal formal evidence 3 + a two-part verdict 1.**

# REMEDIATION

## Error map

| # | The error | Why it happens | The correction, with the evidence that settles it |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | "There are twenty-two swaras" | The number 22 is memorised without its unit | **22 shrutis**, of which **12 are audible** = 7 suddha + 5 vikrit; the seven named swaras are the suddha set |
| 2 | Tala and laya used interchangeably | Both are translated as "rhythm" | **Tala** = the grouping of beats into a cycle of 3 to 108; **laya** = the tempo of that tala. A tala does not speed up; a laya does |
| 3 | "A thaat is a kind of raga" | The thaat is named after a raga | A thaat has **no vaadi or samvaadi, no emotional quality and is not sung**; it is stated in ascent only. Ragas are grouped under it |
| 4 | Ten thaats compared with seventy-two melakartas as rival counts | Both look like parent-scale totals | Thaats are **selected** (32 possible, 10 highlighted); melakartas are **generated** (all 72, each sampurna). A thaat is not a raga; a melakarta is |
| 5 | Hindustani Todi equated with Carnatic Hanumatodi | Shared syllable | Verified correspondence: **Hanumatodi corresponds to Hindustani Bhairavi**. The Todi thaat is not assigned a counterpart in this session |
| 6 | "Ragas create emotions" | The rasa table is read as causal | The tradition **associates** ragas with rasas, hours and seasons. **Malkauns is Raudra in one table and brave in another**; the attributions vary between formulations |
| 7 | Poorva and Uttar Bhaag reversed | Both are 12-hour halves | **Poorva Bhaag = 12 AM to 12 PM**; Uttar Bhaag = 12 PM to 12 AM. Saptak: Poorvang Sa-to-Ma, Uttarang Pa-to-Sa |
| 8 | *Raga* attributed to the *Natyashastra* | The *Natyashastra* is the oldest music text | The *Natyashastra* gives the **octave and 22 shrutis**; **Brihaddesi (Matanga, 6th-8th c.) focused on defining the term *raga*** |
| 9 | Divergence date asserted without evidence | The date is memorised alone | The 14th-century divergence is evidenced by **Haripala's *Sangeeta Sudhakara***, in which the terms Carnatic and Hindustani first appear |
| 10 | "Persian influence created Hindustani music" | The influence row is read as origin | Dhrupad is **of temple origin**, mentioned in the *Natyashastra*, evolved from *Prabandha* and *Dhruvapada*; both traditions share the *Natyashastra* root and the *Sangita Ratnakara* |
| 11 | Dhrupad dated to 1593 | The naming source is read as an origin | **1294 CE** *Yugala Shataka* contains dhrupad-style lyrics; **1593** *Ain-i-Akbari* is the earliest source that **names** the genre |
| 12 | Tabla attached to dhrupad | Tabla is the default Hindustani drum | Dhrupad uses the **pakhawaj**, entering only after the wordless alap; tabla is Hindustani but not dhrupad's drum |
| 13 | Bada and chhota khyal reversed | Both are khayal songs | **Bada Khyal = slow and comprises most of the performance**; **Chhota Khyal = fast** |
| 14 | "Semi-classical" read as inferior | The prefix invites a ranking | Four recorded departures - lighter ragas, lighter tala, faster laya, more bhava and lyrics - on a retained swara foundation |
| 15 | Tappa treated as a court creation | Court legitimation is read as origin | It **originated in the folk songs of the camel riders of north-west India** and was legitimated at Muhammad Shah's court |
| 16 | Ghazal given a continuous theme | It is read as a poem | Its couplets, **bayt or sher**, seven to twelve in number, **need no common theme or continuity** |
| 17 | Bhimsen Joshi placed in Benaras, or Bismillah Khan in Kirana | Both are famous and Benaras and Kirana are adjacent in the tables | **Bhimsen Joshi = Kirana; Bismillah Khan = Benaras**, which produced Bismillah Khan, Sitara Devi and Girija Devi |
| 18 | A gharana attributed to Ravi Shankar | Every other named performer has one | **No gharana is recorded for him.** Supply none |
| 19 | "Carnatic music has little improvisation" | A single comparison-table row | Alapana, neraval, kalpana swara, swara-kalpana, tanam and ragam-tanam-pallavi; three of five concert stages. The claim concerns **codification of style** |
| 20 | Tanam and Ragam reversed | Both are Carnatic improvisation terms | **Tanam is with mridangam; Ragam is without** |
| 21 | Pallavi, Anu Pallavi and Charana confused | The three names resemble one another | Pallavi first and repeated; **Anu Pallavi exactly two lines**, not always repeated; **Charana final and longest** |
| 22 | Manipuri Sankirtana confused with Carnatic sankirtana | One word, two objects | Manipur's **Sankirtana** is ritual singing, drumming and dancing by the Vaishnava community; **sankirtana** also names Annamacharya's devotional songs |
| 23 | Ghatam or jaltarang filed as a membranophone | Both are struck | Both are **Ghana Vadya** - non-drum percussion, **no tuning required**, no membrane |
| 24 | Gotuvadyam or swaramandal filed as percussion | Both are struck with hammers | Both are **Tata Vadya, struck sub-type**. The **string**, not the striking, fixes the family |
| 25 | Mridangam called Hindustani | Both drums are famous | **Tabla - Hindustani; Mridangam - Carnatic** |
| 26 | Sufiana kalam treated as an instrument | It appears in the santoor note | It is a **genre**; the **santoor accompanies** it. The santoor is a **100-stringed** instrument of **Jammu and Kashmir** |
| 27 | The two 1926 institutions merged | Same year, both in the north | **Prayag Sangeet Samiti, Allahabad** and **Marris College, Lucknow** (Bhatkhande), later the Bhatkhande Music Institute Deemed University |
| 28 | Gandharva Mahavidyalaya attributed to Bhatkhande | Both are systematisers | **Paluskar, 1901**, opened in **Lahore**, moved to **Mumbai in 1915**. The **thaat system** is Bhatkhande's |
| 29 | Academy recognition equated with UNESCO inscription | Both are "heritage" statuses | Different bodies, different criteria, different instruments. The academy's founding year is recorded as **1952** in the standing source and **1953** in a dated 2026 report |
| 30 | "Amir Khusrau invented the sitar, tabla, khayal and qawwali" | Attribution treated as fact | The material itself hedges - "attributed to", "it is believed", and for qawwali "**widely disputed**". Tabla and sitar **invention dates remain uncertain** |

## Targeted drills

**Drill 1 - the two-level test.** For each pair, say which member is the classification and which is the thing classified: thaat and raga; melakarta and janya; gharana and style; family and instrument; rasa and raga; occasion and folk form. Then, for the first two pairs, say whether the classification is itself performable.

**Drill 2 - unit discipline.** Attach the correct unit to each number: 22; 12; 7; 5; 10; 32; 72; 3 to 108; 16; 35; 175; 264; 18; 2 to 8; 4 to 5; 100; 108. One of these belongs to two different items in the topic - identify it.

**Drill 3 - the vani and place sort.** Sort into vani-named and place-named: Dagar, Gwalior, Bettiah, Kirana, Talwandi, Agra, Darbhanga, Patiala, Bhendibazaar. Then say which single gharana belongs on both lists and why, and name the four vanis.

**Drill 4 - date-kind sort.** Classify each as a text date, a person or patronage date, or an inadmissible form or instrument date: 1294 CE; 1593; 1408-1503; 1484-1564; 1486-1516; 1670-1748; 1813-1846; 1860-1936; 1872-1931; 1901; 1926; 1952; 1977; the invention of the tabla; the invention of the sitar; the origin of khayal.

**Drill 5 - the family decision.** Place each instrument and state the deciding question: ghatam; santoor; idakka; swaramandal; algoza; kamaicha; chimta; gogona; dotara; bhumi dundubhi; jaltarang; tumbi; titti; gotuvadyam; andelu; sarinda.

**Drill 6 - occasion recall.** Name the folk form for each occasion and its state: childbirth; the end of a marriage ceremony; women fetching water; farmers in the fields; women going to the forest; a coronation; a named 1891 battle; Basant Panchami; the Dusshera procession; a song accompanied entirely by clapping.

**Drill 7 - hedge restoration.** Restore the recorded hedge to each claim: Amir Khusrau originated qawwali; Amir Khusrau invented the Sehtar; khayal originated with Amir Khusrau; the Ravanahatha originated in the Hela Civilisation; Guru Nanak and Mardana developed the shabad; the Ghana classical instruments are Harappan; Bhumi Dundubhi is an ancient earth drum.

**Drill 8 - conflict reporting.** For each of the six disclosed conflicts, write the single sentence you would put in an answer that reports both positions without adjudicating: the *Sangita Ratnakara* attribution; Malkauns's rasa; the performance-time rule; the Carnatic improvisation claim; Teejanbai's Padma Shri year; the national academy's founding year.

**Drill 9 - boundary routing.** For each, say what this topic owns and what it routes: Lavani; Dollu Kunitha; thumri and Kathak; Kshetrayya's padams; Manipuri Sankirtana; the ghazal as verse; qawwali's Sufi doctrine; a recent academy award list; a UNESCO inscription; the acoustics of the santoor.

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Master causal chain: from a vibrating string to a state honour

```text
   [1] SOUND
        22 shrutis counted | 12 audible | 7 suddha + 5 vikrit
        Sa and Pa achala (fixed) | the other five chala
                 |
   [2] MELODY BECOMES REPRODUCIBLE
        Gram (Shadja, Madhyama) -> Murchhana (7 per gram) -> Jaati (18 in the
        Natya Shastra; audava 5 / shadava 6 / sampurna 7)
                 |
   [3] MELODY BECOMES IDENTIFIABLE
        RAGA = aaroha + avaroha + vadi/samvadi + anuvadi/vivadi + PAKAD + rasa
        -> the pakad is why two ragas with identical notes remain distinct
        -> therefore the repertoire is not capped by arithmetic
                 |
   [4] TIME BECOMES INDEPENDENT
        TALA, 3-108 beats, "independent of the music that accompanies it"
        -> therefore departure and return are possible
        -> therefore layakari, swara-kalpana and rhythmic play exist at all
                 |
   [5] THE REPERTOIRE NEEDS FILING
        HINDUSTANI: 32 possible thaats -> 10 selected (Bhatkhande)
        CARNATIC:   72 melakartas generated (Venkatamakhin) -> janya by
                    omission or vakra movement
        -> Bhatkhande MODELLED his scheme on the melakarta scheme
                 |
   [6] THE TRADITION SPLITS
        shared root: Natyashastra | shared authority: Sangita Ratnakara, 13th c.
        divergence: 14th c. | evidence: Sangeeta Sudhakara names both for the
        first time
                 |
        +--------+--------------------------------+
        |                                         |
   [7a] NORTH: the addressee is a patron     [7b] SOUTH: the addressee is a deity
        Dhruvapad -> DHRUPAD (temple origin,      kirtana/sankirtana -> KRITI
        Sanskrit syllables, pakhawaj, duo,        (fixed raga and tala, sahitya
        4-5 stanzas, alap first)                  primacy, pallavi/anupallavi/
        zenith at Akbar's court; named in the     charana)
        Ain-i-Akbari, 1593; declines 18th c.      composer-centred canon:
                 |                                Annamacharya 1408-1503 ->
        KHAYAL: bandish of 2-8 lines; taans;      Purandara Dasa 1484-1564 ->
        bada (slow, most of the performance)      Kshetrayya -> Ramadasu ->
        + chhota (fast); each singer renders      Venkatamakhin -> the Trinity
        the same bandish differently                       |
                 |                                 concert order fixed:
        -> attention moves from COMPOSITION        varnam -> alapana -> neraval
           to PERFORMER                            -> kalpana swaras -> tillana
                 |                                         |
   [8a] THEREFORE THE LINEAGE IS THE UNIT    [8b] THEREFORE THE COMPOSER IS
        GHARANA: concentrate a scarce skill        THE UNIT
        -> attach to a patron -> differentiate     guru-shishya parampara;
        -> relocate                                 sabha and festival
        vani-named (dhrupad) | place-named
        (khayal) | instrument-named (sitar)

        +--------+-----------------------------------------+

   [9] THE PATRON DISAPPEARS
        Aurangzeb: court singing removed, instrumental music continued, nobles
        and royal women patronised, the emperor played the veena, music
        literature produced -> Sadarang strengthens khayal under Muhammad Shah
        princely states contract; Talwandi nearly extinct after partition;
        tappa survives in baithak khanas and jalsaghar, then faces extinction
                 |
   [10] THREE REPLACEMENTS
        WRITE IT DOWN   Bhatkhande's treatise and thaat system; Paluskar's
                        raga classification
        BUILD A SCHOOL  1901 Gandharva Mahavidyalaya (Lahore -> Mumbai 1915);
                        1926 Prayag Sangeet Samiti; 1926 Marris College
        BUILD A PUBLIC  1952 national academy for the performing arts;
                        1977 outreach movement; the Chaturprahar festival,
                        programmed on the samay system

   [11] RECOGNITION REPLACES EMPLOYMENT
        Bharat Ratna 1998, 1999, 2001, 2008 - two traditions, two instruments
        the academy's own remit: music, dance and theatre, including folk,
```
