## Master comparison 1: the two classical traditions, on six axes

| Axis | Hindustani | Carnatic |
|---|---|---|
| Parent-scale system | **10 thaats**, selected from 32 possible; a thaat is **not a raga** and is **not sung** | **72 melakarta / Janaka ragas**, each **sampurna**; **janya** derived by omission or vakra movement |
| Rhythmic method | Named whole cycles; **teen-tal 16 beats** most used; about 30 known, 10-12 in use | **Laghu + dhrutam + anudhrutam**; **35 x 5 ghatis = 175**; **aditala** most prominent |
| Improvisational grammar | Alap, taan, murki, alankar, layakari; bada-chhota khyal slow-fast design | **Manodharma**: alapana, neraval, kalpana swara, tanam, RTP; three of five concert stages |
| Repertoire | Dhrupad, dhamar, hori, khayal, tappa, chaturang, ragasagar, tarana, sargam, thumri; ghazal semi-classical | Kriti, varnam, padam, javali, tillana, ragam-tanam-pallavi |
| Transmission unit | **Gharana** - vani-, place- or instrument-named lineage | **Guru-shishya parampara**; the **composer's repertoire** as canon |
| Institutional ecology | North Indian temple, devotional, Sultanate, Mughal and regional court networks; later concert, radio, recording | South Indian temple, court and **sabha** networks; the **festival at a saint's samadhi** |
| **Shared** | **Raga and tala; shruti, swara, saptak; the *Natyashastra* root; the *Sangita Ratnakara*; flute and violin; *sampurna* as a term; devotional foundations on both sides** | |

## Master comparison 2: the three registers

| | Classical | Semi-classical | Folk |
|---|---|---|---|
| Foundation | Codified theory; raga, tala, swara | **Still based on swara** | Rhythm-led, dance-oriented, thematically diverse |
| Raga treatment | Full raga structure | **Lighter versions** of ragas - Bhoopali, Malkaush | Not stated in raga terms in the bank |
| Tala and laya | Full tala; vilambit to drut | **Lighter tala**; **madhyam or drut laya** | Set on beats so as to be dance-oriented |
| Emphasis | Structural elaboration - alap-jor-jhala | **Bhava and lyrics** | Theme and occasion |
| Transmission | Gurukul, gharana, guru-shishya, later schools | Gharanas - Varanasi, Lucknow, Patiala | Community; Langha, Manganiar, Baul, Bhopa |
| Setting | Court, temple, concert | Court, elite household - baithak khana, jalsaghar | Occasion - wedding, birth, harvest, war, work |
| Named instances | Dhrupad, khayal, kriti | Thumri, tappa, ghazal | Baul, Pandavani, Panihari, Lavani and the regional bank |
| Movement between | Dhrupad: temple origin -> court zenith | Tappa: camel-riders' songs -> Muhammad Shah's court | Hori: folk, semi-classical and classical registers at once |

## Master comparison 3: the four vocal-form families of the Hindustani repertoire

| | Dhrupad | Khayal | Semi-classical | Tarana |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Name means | Fixed verse (*dhruva* + *pada*) | Idea or imagination (Persian) | - | - |
| Composed text | 4-5 stanzas | Bandish, 2-8 lines | Lighter, bhava- and lyric-led | Short melody repeated with variations, many words |
| Opening | Wordless **alap**, tempo rising | Alap given **much less room** | Faster tempo from the outset | - |
| Signature device | Overt raga elaboration | Frequent **taans** | Quick turns of phrase (tappa); sensuality (thumri) | **Rhythmic manipulation** at fast tempo |
| Drum | **Pakhawaj** | - | Dholki for lavani; tabla, dholak for related forms | - |
| Performers | **Duo**, generally two male vocalists | - | Thumri usually sung by a **female** | Specialised rhythmic training |
| Lineage naming | By **vani** | By **place** | Varanasi, Lucknow, Patiala, Rampur-Sahaswan | Mewati, through a named exponent |

## Master comparison 4: the four instrument families

| | Tata | Sushira | Avanaddha | Ghana |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Western equivalent | Chordophones | Aerophones | Membranophones | Idiophones |
| Recorded mechanism | **String instruments** | **All the wind instruments** | **Percussion; one has to strike** | **Non-drum percussion; no tuning required** |
| Deciding question | Does a string vibrate? | Is there an enclosed air column? | Is there a stretched membrane? | Struck, with no membrane? |
| Sub-types | Bowed, plectral, **struck** | Classical, folk | Classical, folk | Classical, folk |
| Classical instances | Sarangi, violin; sitar, veena, tanpura; gotuvadyam, swaramandal, santoor | Bansuri, shehnai, pungi, ninkirns | **Tabla (Hindustani), mridangam (Carnatic)**, dhol, bhumi dundubhi | Manjira, jaltarang, kanch-tarang, ghungroo, ghatam, khartal |
| Folk instances | Chikara, sarinda, tingteila, **kamaicha**; tumbi, ektara, **dotara**, onavillu | Pungi/been, algoza, tangmuri, titti, mashak, gogona, ejuk tapung | Ghumot, idakka, udukai, sambal, tamak, diggi | Chimta, gharha, andelu |
| The trap it carries | Struck strings misfiled as percussion | Genre mistaken for instrument | - | Pots and bowls misfiled as drums |

## Argument map: what this topic actually establishes

```text
   CLAIM 1  Indian classical music is a system, not a style.
   BECAUSE  pitch is measured (22 shrutis), selected (12 audible, 7 named),
            ranked (vadi, samvadi, anuvadi, vivadi) and identified (pakad);
            and time is grouped independently of melody (tala, 3-108 beats).
   SO       "ancient and divine" is not an answer; the vocabulary is.
   LIMIT    The associations layered on top - rasa, samay, ritu - are
            conventions, and they vary between formulations.

   CLAIM 2  The two traditions are two grammars of one inheritance.
   BECAUSE  shared root (Natyashastra), shared authority (Sangita Ratnakara),
            shared elements (raga and tala), shared instruments (flute,
            violin), a modelled classification (thaat on melakarta), shared
            patrons (Ibrahim Adil Shah II), a ruler-composer in both styles
            (Swathi Thirunal), and traffic in repertoire (Bhendibazaar's
            Carnatic ragas; Kirvani, Charukeshi, Ahir Bhairav).
   SO       comparison is of classification, rhythm method, improvisation
            format, repertoire and transmission unit.
   LIMIT    Two recorded claims must be handled, not repeated: the influence
            row and the "less scope for improvisation" row.

   CLAIM 3  Transmission is an institution, and institutions can fail.
   BECAUSE  gurukul (12 years, residence, no discrimination) -> gharana
            (style and ideology, teaching and performance, household,
            patron) -> notation and schools -> academy and audience.
   SO       the gharana is a labour and patronage structure, not a label.
   LIMIT    Talwandi nearly extinct after partition; tappa facing extinction.
            Relocation is possible, not guaranteed.

   CLAIM 4  Patronage redirects rather than disappears.
   BECAUSE  Aurangzeb's court singing removed but instrumental music,
            elite patronage and music literature continued; Sadarang
            strengthened khayal under Muhammad Shah; regional courts
            (Jaunpur, Gwalior) substituted; zamindari drawing rooms
            sustained tappa; lineages moved to concert, radio and recording;
            the state conferred recognition (1998, 1999, 2001, 2008).
   SO       "Aurangzeb banned music" is not merely wrong, it is the wrong
            kind of claim.
   LIMIT    Substance changed too: dhrupad declined as khayal rose, and
            notation standardised what lineages had kept plural.

   CLAIM 5  Attribution in this topic is evidence, not decoration.
   BECAUSE  the material itself hedges - "attributed to" for khayal,
            "it is believed" for the Sehtar and the Ravanahatha, "historians
            suggest" for the shabad, "widely disputed" for qawwali - and
            records the tabla and sitar invention dates as uncertain.
   SO       "credited with" is the correct register, and the earliest source
            containing a form is not the earliest source naming it
            (1294 CE against 1593).
   LIMIT    Caution is not denial: the attributions are the recorded
            tradition and are examinable as such.
```

## Answer spines, side by side

| Demand | Spine |
|---|---|
| **Compare the two traditions** | Shared root and shared authority -> four or five shared elements -> six axes of difference -> handle the influence and improvisation rows explicitly -> verdict as "two grammars" |
| **Discuss the gharana** | Definition with style, ideology, teaching and performance -> the four-step mechanism -> vani, place and instrument naming with named instances -> Talwandi as the limit -> verdict on social organisation |
| **Classify the instruments** | Four Sanskrit families with Western equivalents -> recorded mechanism of each -> two or three named instruments per family with region -> membrane discriminator -> struck chordophones -> family against genre -> verdict |
| **Trace the development of Indian music** | Material and Vedic base -> later Vedic genres -> treatise ladder by contribution -> gurukul transmission -> fourteenth-century divergence with its textual evidence -> the two repertoires -> verdict on continuity |
| **Music and patronage** | Who was addressed -> temple and empire against court -> ruler-composers on both sides -> the Aurangzeb correction -> what replaced the patron in five stages -> Talwandi and tappa as limits -> verdict |
| **Folk and classical** | Function-and-transmission distinction without hierarchy -> the devotional meeting mechanism -> tappa as upward movement -> hori across three registers -> occasion-based folk evidence -> verdict on one culture in registers |
| **Treatises and theory** | Contribution, not title-and-author -> Natyashastra measures, Brihaddesi names, Sangita Ratnakara counts, Chaturdandiprakashika generates -> Sangeeta Sudhakara as the divergence evidence -> the Odissi pair as a third textual tradition -> verdict |
| **Modern institutions** | The transmission problem stated -> notation and pedagogy -> the three schools with years and cities -> the academy and its remit -> outreach and festival -> recognition rather than employment -> the standardisation cost |

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## 1. Scope, the four analytical questions and the boundaries

- Owned: the musical **system**, its **repertoire**, its **transmission**, its **instruments**, its **institutions**.
- Four questions: **system** (how pitch and time are organised), **form** (the shape of the composition), **carrier** (who holds and transmits the skill), **instrument** (what makes the sound). Value judgements are not evidence and come last.
- Three pillars of Indian classical music: **raga, tala, swara**. Treatises on musical grammar are **Lakshanagranthas**.
- Three chronologies kept apart: **text dates** (safe), **person and patronage dates** (safe), **form and instrument dates** (not to be supplied).
- Routed: choreography and codified dance forms; dramaturgy and puppetry; Bhakti, Sufi, Sikh and Pushtimarg doctrine; the ghazal's literary history; acoustics and instrument engineering; award administration, recognition policy and UNESCO inscription.

## 2. Pitch: shruti, swara, saptak

- **Shruti** = smallest audible unit of sound, the microtone; the smallest gradation of pitch, representing the quality of frequency. Swaras are derived from groupings of shrutis.
- **Bharata divided the swaras into a scale of 22**; ***Dattilam* confirmed 22 shrutis per octave as the only humanly possible sounds**. **12 are audible = 7 suddha + 5 vikrit.**
- *Swara*: in the ancient period associated with **Vedic recitation**; later the **note or scale degree**.
- Seven with functions: **Shadja-tonic, Rishabha-supertonic, Gandhara-mediant, Madhyama-sub-dominant, Panchama-dominant, Dhaivata-sub-mediant, Nishada-subtonic**; abbreviated Sa, Re, Ga, Ma, Pa, Dha, Ni.
- **Sa and Pa = achala (fixed)**; the other five **chala**, with alternate forms. The seven together = **Saptak / Sargam**.
- Functional swaras: **Graha** (starting note; in modern practice Sa for all ragas), **Vadi** (king note), **Samvadi** (second, generally the 4th or 5th from the vadi), **Anuvadi** (harmonising, often the third), **Vivadi** (dissonant, close to a main note, sparingly used).

## 3. Melody: gram, murchhana, jaati and the raga

- **Raga** from ***Ranj*** = to delight, to satisfy. **Neither a scale nor a mode**; a melodic form with its own peculiar ascent and descent, of a full octave or five, six or seven notes.
- **Ragas = basis of melody; tala = basis of rhythm.**
- Anatomy: **Arohana, Avarohana, Vadi, Samvadi, Anuvadi, Vivadi, Pakad/Chalan, Rasa**. The **pakad identifies**; the rest describes.
- **Gram** = earliest tonal system; two - **Shadja Gram** and **Madhyama Gram**. **Murchhana** = modal shift of the tonic; **seven per gram**. **Jaati** = earliest prototype of raga, by note-count and ascent-descent arrangement; **18 jatis in the *Natya Shastra***.
- Note-count: **Audav/Odava 5 (pentatonic), Shadava 6 (hexatonic), Sampurna 7 (heptatonic)**.
- **Raga bhed**: **Shuddha** - form unchanged by foreign notes (Bhairav); **Chhayalag** - form changes (Jaijaiwanti); **Sankeerna** - a combination of two or more ragas (Mishra Pilu).
- **Raga-Ragini**: one of the oldest schemes; **5 or 6 main ragas x 6 raginis = about 30-36**; offspring **Ragaputra / Ragaputri**. The familial vocabulary is a classificatory metaphor.
- **Laya speeds: Vilambit (slow), Madhya (medium), Drut (fast).**

## 4. Time: tala and laya

- **Tala** = rhythmic grouping of beats; cycles of **3 to 108 beats**; musical time divided into simple and complicated metres.
- The decisive claim: tala is **independent of the music that accompanies it and has its own divisions**. The theory of time measurement **differs between the two traditions**.
- **Laya** = the tempo of the tala, keeping the time span uniform.
- Hindustani: **dadra, kaharba, rupak, ektal, jhaptal, teen-tal, chautal**; **teen-tal = 16 beats**, most used; **more than 100 argued, about 30 known, 10-12 used**.
- Carnatic: **laghu + dhrutam + anudhrutam**; **35 original talas x 5 ghatis = 175**; **aditala** most prominent.

## 5. Two parent-scale systems

- **Thaat** = classification of ragas into groups; **10-thaat classification currently adopted**; **V.N. Bhatkhande (1860-1936)**.
- Thaat rules: **seven of the twelve notes**; **necessarily ascending**; sung only in **aarohi**; **no vaadi/samvaadi**; **no emotional quality**; **not sung**.
- The ten: **Bilawal, Khamaj, Kafi, Asavari, Bhairavi, Bhairav, Kalyan, Marwa, Poorvi, Todi.**
- Thaat-versus-raga, six rows: origin; **7 notes against at least 5**; **aaroha only against both**; not melodious against melodious; **no vaadi/samvaadi against vaadi/samvaadi**; **named after popular ragas against named after the emotion evoked**.
- **Melakarta**: **Venkatamakhi, 17th century**; **72 parent or Janaka ragas**; each **sampurna**; **janya** ragas by **omitting notes or vakra (zigzag) movement**; **Mayamalavagowla -> Malahari**, taught to beginners.
- The deepest difference: a thaat **is not a raga**; a melakarta **is**.
- Verified correspondences: **Bilawal-Dheera Shankarabharanam; Bhairav-Mayamalavagowla (15th); Kafi-Kharaharapriya; Bhairavi-Hanumatodi; Poorvi-Kamavardhini / Pantuvarali (51st)**. Bhatkhande **modelled** the thaat scheme on the melakarta scheme; **32 possible thaats, 10 highlighted**.
- The trap: Hindustani **Todi** is **not** Carnatic **Hanumatodi**; Hanumatodi corresponds to **Bhairavi**. Five thaats are left without a verified counterpart.
- Ragas outside the thaat system, several Carnatic-derived: **Kirvani, Nat Bhairav, Charukeshi, Madhuvanti, Ahir Bhairav**.

## 6. Rasa, samay and ritu - stated at the correct strength

- Rasas = emotions evoked in performer and audience; described as **aesthetic delight**, free from the limitations of personal feelings. **Eight initially; *shanta* added = nine, the *Nauras***.
- Pairs: Shringara-Kafi/Desh; Hasya-Hansadhwani; Karuna-Bhairavi; Raudra-Malkauns; Bhayanak-Todi; Veer-Deshkar; Adbhuta-Darbari Kanada; **Bibhatsya - rarely used in practice, no example**; Shant-Shankara/Yaman.
- **After the 15th century, Bhakti** was widely accepted into the corpus; **some musicologists argue bhakti and shanta are the same**.
- **Samay**: **Poorva Bhaag 12 AM-12 PM -> Poorva ragas; Uttar Bhaag 12 PM-12 AM -> Uttar ragas**. Saptak: **Poorvang Sa-to-Ma; Uttarang Pa-to-Sa**.
- Raga-time-season: Bhairav-dawn-any-peace; Hindol-morning-spring; Deepak-night-summer-compassion; Megh-late night-rainy-courage; Shree-evening-winter-gladness; Malkaush-midnight-winter-brave. Seasons: **Megh and Malhar - monsoon; Basant and Bahar - spring; Deepak - summer; Hemant - winter**.
- **Two disclosed items**: Malkauns is **Raudra** in the rasa table and **brave** in the time table; and the performance-time rule is related by Bhatkhande to **thaat and vadi** while other authorities hold that **time has no importance**. Report both; do not adjudicate.
- The framework is **shared with dance and dramaturgy**; the time-and-season specification is stated for **Hindustani** practice.

## 7. The historical spine to the fourteenth-century divergence

- **Seven-holed flute recovered from Indus Valley Civilisation sites**; **Ravanahatha believed to have originated in the Hela Civilisation, Sri Lanka**, one of the oldest known.
- Vedic anchors: **Sama Veda notes, Gandharva Veda, Veena in the Aitareya Aranyaka, Om, Panini, Bharata's Natyashastra**.
- Later Vedic ritual genres: **Sangama** (verse chanting), **Jatigan** (narrative music).
- **Gurukul**: ashram or hermitage system; **guru-shishya**; **12 years** of residence and service; **patronage from kings and wealthy persons**; rigorous and pensive; knowledge by direct experience; **prince and commoner treated alike**.
- Both traditions traced to the **Natyashastra**; **diverged in the 14th century**; the terms **Carnatic and Hindustani appear for the first time in Haripala's *Sangeeta Sudhakara*** (14th century AD).
- **Persian and Islamic influence transformed North Indian music from devotional Dhruvapad to Dhrupad by the 15th century; Khayal emerged by the 17th century; folk singing styles also developed.** Recorded influence summary: Hindustani - **Arab, Persian, Afghan**; Carnatic - **indigenous**. State as contact history, never as essence.
- Continuity evidence against a foreign-versus-pure reading: dhrupad's **temple origin** and Sanskrit syllables; the shared *Natyashastra* root; the *Sangita Ratnakara*'s dual authority; thaat modelled on melakarta; Bhendibazaar's Carnatic ragas; Swathi Thirunal composing in both styles; Ibrahim Adil Shah II patronising both.

## 8. Dhrupad and the vani lineages

- **Ten Hindustani styles: Dhrupad, Dhamar, Hori, Khayal, Tappa, Chaturang, Ragasagar, Tarana, Sargam, Thumri.** The branch focuses on **musical structure and the possibilities of improvisation**; it adopted the **Shudha Swara Saptaka**, the Octave of Natural Notes.
- **Dhrupad** = ***dhruva*** (fixed) + ***pada*** (verse); evolved from ***Prabandha*** and ***Dhruvapada***; **mentioned in the *Natyashastra***.
- Four anchors: ***Yugala Shataka*, Shri Shribhatta, 1294 CE** (earliest known source containing dhrupad-style lyrics); **consolidation as a classical form, 13th century**; **zenith at Akbar's court**; ***Ain-i-Akbari*, 1593** (earliest source that **names** the genre); **decline in the 18th century**.
- **Swami Haridas -> Tansen and Baiju Bawra**; Tansen one of Akbar's **Navaratna**; both major proponents, 15th-16th centuries.
- Performance: **wordless alap first, tempo rising to evoke the raga's mood**; then the main dhrupad with **pakhawaj**; **Sanskrit syllables**; **temple origin**; **4-5 stanzas**; a **duo**, generally two male vocalists, with **tanpura and pakhawaj**.
- Four vani lineages: **Dagar** (Delhi/UP, Dagar Vani linked to Gauhar; alaap emphasis, meditative, often in pairs; Gundecha Brothers, Dagar family); **Darbhanga** (Bihar, Khandar + Gauhar Vani; alaap and bandish equally; **layakari**; Ram Chatur Mallick, Pt. Siyaram Tewari, Mallick family); **Bettiah** (Bihar, Gauhar + Khandar Vani; **bandish as core**; Mishra family, Indra Kishore Mishra); **Talwandi** (Punjab, now Pakistan, Khandar Vani; forceful; **nearly extinct after decline and partition**).
- The internal scale: **Dagar -> Darbhanga -> Bettiah** runs from alaap emphasis to bandish core. The fourth vani, **Nauhar**, survives in the Agra gharana's recorded origin.
- **Dhamar** is named among the ten and linked to the dhrupad family through Agra's **Dhrupad-Dhamar** blend; **no separate structure is recorded**. **Chaturang, Ragasagar, Sargam**: named only. **Hori** appears as one of the ten, as a lighter thumri-type form, and as a **Radha-Krishna Holi folk song of Uttar Pradesh**.

## 9. Khayal, tarana and the khayal gharanas

- **Khyal** from Persian = **idea or imagination**; origin **attributed to Amir Khusrau**.
- **Bandish** = a khyal composition; **short songs of two to eight lines**; **each singer renders the same bandish differently, keeping text and raga constant**.
- **Hussain Shah, Sharqi ruler of the Jaunpur Sultanate: the most generous 15th-century patronage to Khyal.** Khayal emerged as a new style by the **17th century**; shaped at Muhammad Shah's court by **Sadarang**.
- **Frequent taans**; **alap given much less room than in dhrupad**. **Bada Khyal - slow, comprises most of the performance; Chhota Khyal - fast.** Themes usually **romantic**; praise of God or a king; exceptional compositions in praise of **Krishna**.
- **Gwalior** - among the oldest khyal gharanas; **equal emphasis on melody and rhythm**; simple ragas, *sapaat* taans; Natthu Khan, V.D. Paluskar.
- **Kirana** - founded by **Nayak Gopal**, popularised by **Abdul Karim Khan and Abdul Wahid Khan**; **precise tuning, slow tempo, melody over rhythm**, clear pronunciation; **Bhimsen Joshi, Gangubai Hangal**.
- **Agra (Rangila)** - origin in **Nauhar Vani**; **Haji Sujan Khan / Khudabuksh**, 19th century; khyal blended with **Dhrupad-Dhamar**; stress on **bandish**; Faiyyaz Khan, Vilayat Hussain Khan.
- **Patiala** - **Bade Fateh Ali Khan and Ali Baksh Jarnail Khan**, 19th century; **patronised by the Maharaja of Patiala**; **khyal, thumri and ghazal**; intricate taans, **gamak**, **tarana gayaki**; **Bade Ghulam Ali Khan**.
- **Bhendibazaar** - Mumbai, 19th century; **Chhajju Khan, Nazir Khan, Khadim Hussain Khan**; **breath control**, long passages in one breath; **includes Carnatic ragas**.
- **Tarana**: rhythm crucial; short melody repeated with variations; one contrasting higher-pitched melody; many words at fast tempo; credited to **Amir Khusrau, 13th-14th centuries**; used by **Guru Gobind Singh**; **Pandit Rattan Mohan Sharma, Mewati Gharana, "Tarana ke Baadshah"**.

## 10. The semi-classical register

- Definition: **based on swara**; **lighter versions of ragas** such as **Bhoopali or Malkaush**; **lighter tala**; **madhyam or drut laya**; **greater emphasis on bhava and lyrics than on alap-jor-jhala**.
- **Thumri**: **Uttar Pradesh**; **mixed simple ragas**; romantic or devotional; inspired by the **Bhakti movement**, text usually about a girl's love for **Krishna**; **Hindi in Braj Bhasha**; usually sung by a **female**; inherent sensuality; **linked to Kathak**; generic name for **Dadra, Hori, Kajari, Saavan, Jhoola, Chaiti**; gharanas at **Varanasi and Lucknow**; **Begum Akhtar, Girija Devi, Chhannulal Mishra** of the **Purab Ang Thumri**.
- **Benaras Gharana** of Hindustani classical music produced **Bismillah Khan, Sitara Devi, Girija Devi**.
- **Tappa**: rhythm central; fast, subtle, knotty constructions; **originated in the folk songs of the camel riders of north-west India**; legitimated at the Mughal court of **Muhammad Shah**; quick turns of phrase; **baithaki** style under **zamindari** patronage of the late 19th-early 20th centuries in **baithak khanas** and **jalsaghar**; **facing extinction**; **Laxmanrao Pandit** (Gwalior), **Shanno Khurana** (Rampur-Sahaswan).
- **Ghazal**: rhyming couplets called **Bayt or sher**; **7 to 12 bayts**; **couplets need no common theme or continuity**; **Iran, 10th century -> South Asia, 12th century** through Sufi mystics and the courts of the new Islamic sultanate -> **zenith in the Mughal period**; **Amir Khusrau (1253-1325)** said to be among the first expounders in India; one subject - **love**, unconditional and superior, the object **open to interpretation, divine or human**; simplified over time, reaching wider audiences; **Muhammad Iqbal, Mirza Ghalib, Kazi Nazrul Islam** (Bengali ghazals); **Ghulam Ali** named as a performer.
- Three routes into one register: **devotion (thumri), folk occupation (tappa), imported poetic form (ghazal)**.

## 11. The gharana as an institution

- **Gharana** = a musical lineage or school, based on **style and ideology**, shaping **teaching and performance**.
- **Famous Hindustani gharanas: Agra, Gwalior, Indore, Jaipur, Kirana, Patiala.**
- Three naming principles: **vani** (dhrupad), **place** (khayal, semi-classical), **instrument** (**sitar gharanas: Jaipur, Varanasi, Etawah** - the complete list).
- Named-only: **Mewati** (Rattan Mohan Sharma), **Rampur-Sahaswan** (Shanno Khurana), **Indore** (no detail recorded; left undescribed).
- Mechanism: **concentrate a scarce skill -> attach to a patron -> differentiate to compete -> relocate to concert, radio, teaching and recording**. Limit: **Talwandi nearly extinct after decline and partition**.
- Gurukul against gharana: entry by residence and service with no discrimination against entry by lineage and household; a fixed twelve-year term against no fixed term; patronage **received** against patronage **served**.
- **Jaipur-Atrauli**, founded by **Alladiya Khan**; **Mallikarjun Mansur (1910-1992)** of Dharwad district, Karnataka, trained under **Manji Khan and Bhurji Khan** after earlier Gwalior training. **No honours are stated.** Gharana affiliation follows **training lineage**, not birthplace or mother tongue.
- Carnatic contrast: **guru-shishya parampara**, less rigidly gharana-labelled; the **composer's repertoire** is the organising canon.
- Attribution rules: **no gharana for Ravi Shankar**; **Bhimsen Joshi = Kirana, Bismillah Khan = Benaras**; do not extend recorded lists.

## 12. Carnatic music

- Mainly **South India**; main emphasis on **vocal music**; languages **Telugu, Kannada, Tamil, Sanskrit**; **kriti-based**, focused on **sahitya**; **based, like Hindustani music, on raga and tala**; rooted in the **Bhakti movement**, tied to **temple and spiritual traditions** of the south.
- **Kriti** = a **highly evolved musical song set to a certain raga and fixed tala**.
- Anatomy: **Pallavi** - first or second thematic lines, often repeated in each stanza, the "Piece de Resistance" in **Ragam Thanam Pallavi**; **Anu Pallavi** - **two lines** after the Pallavi, sung at the beginning and sometimes towards the end, not necessarily repeated after every Charanam; **Charana** - the **final and longest** verse.
- Other forms: **Varnam** (beginners and concert openers), **Padam**, **Javalis**, **Tillanas**.
- Concert order: **Varnam -> Alapana -> Neraval -> Kalpana swaras -> lighter pieces such as a Tillana.**
- Improvisation vocabulary: **Alapana** = raga improvisation; **Neraval** = improvisation on **one line**; **Swara-Kalpana** = improvised with a drummer at **medium and fast** paces; **Tanam** = melodic improvisation in **free rhythm with mridangam**; **Ragam** = pieces **without** mridangam. Carnatic music is usually played with **mridangam**.
- **Kirtana / sankirtana** is used for the earlier devotional song type; **no formal structural definition and no dated kirtana-to-kriti sequence is asserted**. The Manipuri **Sankirtana** is a different object.

## 13. Treatises, read as contributions

- **Samaveda**, Vyasa, ~1000 BC - scriptures set to music. **Dattilam**, Dattila - ritual chants to **gandharva** music; 22 shrutis confirmed.
- ***Natyasastra***, **Bharata, 2nd century BC** - **earliest literature about music in Sanskrit**; **Shruti, Swara, Jati, instruments**; the octave and 22 shrutis.
- ***Silappadikaram***, Illango, **2nd century AD** - Tamil; the three southern kingdoms **Chera, Chola, Pandya**.
- ***Brihaddesi***, **Matanga, 6th-8th centuries** - **defines *raga***; also Nada, Shruti, Swara, Tana, Alankara.
- ***Sangita Makaranda***, Narada, 9th century - ragas as **male, female, neuter**. ***Sarasvati Hridyalankara***, Nanyadeva, **1080 CE** - theory of music.
- ***Abhjilashitarta Chintamani***, **Someshwara, Chalukyan king, 12th century** - qualities of singer, composer, **auditorium**, voice, **Alapana, Gamaka**.
- ***Gita Govinda***, **Jayadeva, 12th century** - Krishna, Radhika and the gopikas; the **Ashta Nayika**; **indicates the exact raga and tala for each song**.
- ***Sangita Samayasara***, Parsvadeva, **1200 CE** - the theory of **gamakas**.
- ***Sangita Ratnakara***, **Sarngadeva, 13th century** - **"Ocean of Music and Dance"**; **264 ragas**; microtones classified; **definitive for both traditions**.
- ***Sangeeta Sudhakara***, **Haripala, 14th century AD** - **the terms Carnatic and Hindustani appear for the first time**.
- ***Swaramela-Kalanidhi***, Ramamatya, 16th century - ragas. ***Sangita Darpana***, Damodara Misra, 17th century - **"Mirror of Music"**, seven chapters.
- ***Chaturdandiprakashika***, **Venkatamakhin, 17th century** - the **melakarta** system. ***Sangraha Choodamani***, Govindacharya, 18th century - the current system of Carnatic music.
- Odissi pair: ***Gita Prakasa***, Krusnadasa Badajena Mahapatra, **16th-17th century**; ***Sangita Narayana***, Purusottama Misra, **17th century**, attributed to **King Gajapati Narayana Deva**.
- **Disclosed conflict**: *Sangita Ratnakara* is attributed to **Sarngadeva** in the treatise bank while a *Sangeet Ratnakar* is listed among **Maharana Kumbha's** works. Attribute to Sarngadeva; for Kumbha, name **Sangeet Raja** and **Sangeet Mimansa**.

## 14. Composers, patrons and the patronage argument

- **Jayadeva**, 12th century, **Sena dynasty** patronage - the *Gita Govinda*.
- **Amir Khusrau, 1253-1325** - **Father of Qawwali**; **introduced Ghazal, Tarana, Trivat**; **credited with the invention of the sitar**; descendants became **Qawwals** and **Kalawants**.
- **Maharana Kumbha, 1417-1468**, Mewar - commentary on the *Gita Govinda*; **Sangeet Raja, Sangeet Mimansa**; **played the veena**.
- **Swami Haridas, 1478-1573** - spiritual poet, dhrupad; **patronised by Akbar**; **guru of Tansen and Baiju Bawra**; poems centred on **Radha**.
- **Mian Tansen (Ramtanu Pandey)**, 16th century, **Navratna** - dhrupad master; **created several new ragas**; compositions on **Hindu gods and goddesses and his patrons**; patronised **first by Raja Ramchandra of Rewa, then Akbar**; ***Sangita Sara***, ***Sri Ganesh Stotra***; **tomb at Gwalior**; **title "Mian" given by Akbar**.
- **Baiju Bawra (Baijnath Mishra)**, 15th-16th century - pupil of Swami Haridas; court musician of **Man Singh Tomar**; **samadhi at Chanderi**.
- **Raja Man Singh Tomar, 1486-1516**, Gwalior - patron of dhrupad; compiled the ***Mankautuhal***; advanced Gwalior as a centre of music.
- **Naubat Khan (Ali Khan Karori)**, Akbar's court - **rudra veena** maestro, **mansabdar**, **son-in-law of Tansen**.
- **Ibrahim Adil Shah II, 1580-1627**, Bijapur - ***Kitab-i-Nauras***; called **Sangeet Samrat**; **patron of both traditions**.
- **Lal Khan Gunsamundra**, Shah Jahan's court, **1637** - dhrupad; **title conferred by Shah Jahan**.
- **Sadarang (Nyamat Khan), 1670-1748** - khyal composer; **court musician of Muhammad Shah**; shaped khyal music.
- **Annamacharya, 1408-1503** - **first known Carnatic composer**; **sankirtanas in praise of Venkateswara**; **Vijayanagara** patronage; **"Grandfather of Telugu songwriting"**.
- **Purandara Dasa, 1484-1564** - **founding father** and **Pitamaha** of Carnatic music; **Dasa Sahitya**; Vijayanagara patronage; **devotee of Krishna**.
- **Kshetrayya, 1600-1680** - **Padams and Keertanas**; travelling poet; **Padams still sung in Bharatanatyam and Kuchipudi**.
- **Bhadrachala Ramadasu, 1620-1680** - **Vaggeyakara**; Telugu devotional compositions for **Rama**; **imprisoned by the Golconda Sultan**.
- **Venkatamakhin**, 17th century - musicologist; **Melakarta** system; ***Chaturdandiprakashika***; **24 Ashtapadis for Tyagesha at Tiruvarur**.
- **Swathi Thirunal Rama Varma, 1813-1846**, Maharaja of **Travancore** - **composer in both Hindustani and Carnatic styles**; patron of **Vadivelu of the Thanjavur Quartet**; his death mourned by the **Royal Asiatic Society of Great Britain**.
- **Thanjavur Quartet - Chinnaiah, Ponniah, Sivanandam, Vadivelu**, 19th century - **codified the Bharatanatyam repertoire**; **court musicians of the Thanjavur Maratha court**; **Vadivelu, the youngest, later served at Travancore under Swathi Thirunal**. The standing account's compression of all four into Swathi Thirunal's court musicians is corrected here and must not be reproduced.
- **Vaggeyakara** = a musician and poet who composes lyrics as well as setting them to music.
- **Trinity of Carnatic music: Tyagaraja, Muthuswami Dikshitar, Syama Sastri**; **Tyagaraja Aradhana at Thiruvaiyaru, Tamil Nadu**, commemorating his samadhi. **No dates for any of the three**; **no composition, raga or language attributed to Dikshitar or Syama Sastri**; **Tyagaraja and Annamacharya are not contemporaries**.
- Modern: **Bhimsen Joshi 1922-2011**, khyal, **Kirana**, **Bharat Ratna 2008**, "Mile Sur Mera Tumhara"; **Bismillah Khan 1916-2006**, **shehnai**, **Bharat Ratna 2001**; **Ravi Shankar 1920-2012**, **sitar**, **Bharat Ratna 1999**, **no gharana recorded**; **M.S. Subbulakshmi 1916-2004**, Carnatic vocal, **Bharat Ratna 1998**.
- **Patronage argument**: temple and empire against court; ruler-composers on both sides; institutional afterlife as **festival** against **lineage**; the deepest axis is **who was addressed**.
- **Aurangzeb correction**: court singing removed, **instrumental music continued**, **royal women and nobles patronised music**, **Aurangzeb played the veena**, **substantial music literature produced**; **Sadarang and Adarang strengthened khayal under Muhammad Shah**. **Tabla and sitar invention dates remain uncertain.**

## 15. Devotional and fusion forms

- **Mechanism**: classical and folk intermingle over time; **devotional music brings them together because both royalty and commoners patronise the deities**.
- **Bhajan**: north India; **Bhakti movement** origin; **simple melodies, generally in one or more raga**; subjects from the lives of gods and goddesses and from the Mahabharata and Ramayana; **chimta, dholak, dafli, manjira**; **Meerabai, Tulsidas, Surdas, Kabir**; **Anup Jalota, Anuradha Paudwal**.
- **Shabad**: Sikhism; sung in **Gurudwaras**; **historians suggest Guru Nanak and Mardana** were responsible for its development and popularity; **three types - raga-based, traditional as in the Adi Granth, lighter**; **the Guru Granth Sahib is organised by chapter in ragas**.
- **Qawwali**: praise of **Allah, the Prophet Muhammad or a Sufi or Islamic saint**; **single raga**; **Urdu, Punjabi or Hindi** with Brajbhasa and Awadhi words; **performed in Sufi shrines**; solo or in groups with **two lead singers and about eight members**; **tabla, dholak, harmonium**; **tempo builds up to induce a transcendental state**; **Amir Khusrau credited as originator - widely disputed**; **Sehtar**, a three-stringed instrument, believed invented by him; **Sabri Brothers, Nusrat Fateh Ali Khan, Aziz Warsi**.
- **Abhanga** - Maharashtra, **Vithobha**, Tukaram and Namdev; **Bhatiali** - West Bengal, nature and daily life, **boat drivers**; **Tevaram** - Tamil Nadu, Shaivite community, **Oduyars**; **Kirtan** - West Bengal, singing and dancing, inspired by the ***Gita Govinda***; **Sopana Sangeetham** - Kerala, originated in temples.
- **Rabindra Sangeet**: Bengal; recreates Tagore's music; **classical elements with Bengali folk strains**; **more than 2,000** presently sung; themes of God, nature, love, celebration of life and **patriotism**.
- **Gana Sangeet**: sung in **choirs or groups in large numbers**; **patriotic and protest** songs carrying a **social message**; example **Vande Mataram**.
- **Haveli Sangeet**: **Rajasthan and Gujarat**; originally sung **in temples**, now performable outside; practised by the **Pushtimarg Sampradaya**.

## 16. Folk music, occasion and community

- Frame: **classical bound by tradition, folk the free voice of the people**; diverse themes, **full of musical rhythm**, **set on beats so as to be dance-oriented**; notable traditions in every state. The distinction is of **function and transmission**, not of value.
- **Baul**: a Bengali **religious sect and a music type**; **Baul Sangeet / Baul Gaan**; lyrics carry **Hindu Bhakti and Sufi (Suphi)** influence, exemplified by the songs of **Kabir**; **West Bengal, Assam, Tripura**; **Vaishnava Hindu or Sufi Muslim**; **Yotin Das, Purno Chandra Das, Lalon Fakir, Naboni Das, Sanatan Das Thakur Baul**; recorded as **one of the classes of Kartabhaja**.
- **Pandavani**: Mahabharata with **Bhima as hero**; **gayan (singing) + vadan (playing)**; rhythm of the **tambura**; **Teejanbai** of Chhattisgarh - **Padma Shri recorded as 1987 in the core source and 1988 in the official record**, Padma Bhushan **2003**, Padma Vibhushan **2019**.
- **Panihari**: Rajasthan; water; women fetching water in **matkas**; **scarcity and distance**; village-well sociability; romance; **mother-in-law and daughter-in-law** relations.
- **Lavani**: Maharashtra; **dance and song combined**, to the **dholki**; powerful rhythm.
- Occasion keys - marriage: **Wanawan** (J&K), **Ovi** (Maharashtra and Goa), **Ja-jin-ja** (Arunachal, during), **Nyioga** (Arunachal, at the end), **Kamar** community song (Chhattisgarh). Birth: **Sohar** (Bihar). War and valour: **Powada** (Shivaji), **Alha**, **Hereileu** (Nagaland), **Saikuti Zai** (Mizoram), **Dhadi** (Punjab), **Khongjom Parba** (Manipur, **Battle of Khongjom, 1891**). Water and season: **Panihari**, **Lotia** (Chaitra), **Kajri** (UP, rains), **Pai Songs**, **Basant Geet** (Basant Panchami), **Jhumair** (tea-tribes of Assam). Work: **Pankhida** (farmers), **Ghasiyari Geet** (women going to forests, importance of labour). Kingship: **Maand** (Rajput rulers), **Sana Lamok** (Manipur, coronation, by the **Maaiba** priest), **Ammanaivari** (Tamil Nadu, Chola monarch). Epic and narrative: **Pandavani**, **Pala and Daskathia** (Odisha), **Villu Pattu** (Tamil Nadu), **Neuleu** (Nagaland), **Chhakri** (Kashmir), **Manganiars** (Alexander, local kings, battles). Devotion and ritual: **Borgeet** (Assam, **Sankardeva and Madhavdeva, 15th-16th c., Ekasarana Dharma**), **Zikir** (Assam, teaching of Islam), **Dihanaam** (Assam, praise of Sankardev), **Sankirtana** (Manipur, Vaishnava), **Lai Haraoba Ishei** (Manipur, **Meitei**, **Sanamahism**), **Bhuta song** (Kerala), **Veeragase** (Karnataka, Dusshera), **Sukar ke Biah** (Bihar, Shukra and Brihaspati). Love and reflection: **Tamang Selo** (Sikkim), **Bhavageete** (Karnataka and Maharashtra), **Mando** (Goa, Indo-Western blend), **Jhoori** and **Laman** (Himachal), **Oi Nitom** (Assam, **Mising**), **Hekaileu** (Nagaland), **Hori** (UP, Holi). Dance-defined: **Lavani**, **Dollu Kunitha** (Karnataka, **Kuruba** community), **Kolannalu / Kolattam** (Andhra), **Heliamleu** (Nagaland), **Chai hla** (Mizoram). Other: **Nattupura Paattu** (Tamil Nadu), **Khubakeshei** (Manipur, **entirely by clapping**).
- Communities: **Langha** (Gujarat and Rajasthan, praise Muslim Rajputs); **Manganiar or Merasi** (Rajasthan, **Kamaicha**); **Karta Bhajas** (West Bengal, four classes); **Bauls**; **Jogi** (**Nath sect**); **Bhopa and Bhopi** (religious hymns); **Barot, Charan, Gadhvi** (Gujarat, storytelling); **Muria** (**Relo**); **Kamar** (marriage song); **Baghela** (**Hardaul** deity); **Pulayar** (**talams**); **Idu Mishmis**; **Moken** (Andaman and Nicobar, ancestor worship); **Bazigar / Goaar** (Punjab).
- **Fusion caution**: classical and folk intermingle over time and other forms emerge - the classical-folk line is a working distinction, not a barrier.
- **Music-dance boundary**: own the song, the metre, the instrument and the community; route the choreography.

## 17. Instruments

- Four families with recorded mechanisms: **Tata Vadya (chordophones)** - string instruments; **Sushira Vadya (aerophones)** - all the wind instruments; **Avanaddha Vadya (membranophones)** - percussion instruments, one has to strike; **Ghana Vadya (idiophones)** - **non-drum percussion which do not require tuning**.
- The discriminator between the two struck families is the **membrane**. The **string**, not the striking, fixes Tata.
- **Avanaddha** classical: **Tabla, Drum, Dhol, Congo, Mridangam, Bhumi Dundubhi** (ancient earth drum) - **Tabla Hindustani, Mridangam Carnatic**. Folk: **Ghumot** (Goa, Ganesh festival), **Idakka** (Kerala), **Udukai** (Tamil Nadu), **Sambal** (Maharashtra, Konkan), **Tamak** (Santhals), **Diggi** (UP).
- **Sushira** classical: **Bansuri, Shehnai, Pungi, Ninkirns** - **Shehnai: Bismillah Khan; Flute: Krishna, Hariprasad Chaurasia**. Folk: **Pungi/Been** (snake charmers), **Algoza** (Punjab, double flute), **Tangmuri** (Meghalaya), **Titti** (South India, bagpipe), **Mashak** (Uttarakhand, Rajasthan, UP), **Gogona** (Assam, Bihu), **Ejuk Tapung** (Assam, rare).
- **Ghana** classical: **Manjira/Jhanj, Jaltarang, Kanch-tarang, Ghungroo, Ghatam, Khartal** - rhythm-keepers, **ancient (Harappan)** as a gloss only. Folk: **Chimta** (Punjab, fire tongs), **Gharha** (Punjab, earthen pots), **Andelu** (Burra-Katha, hollow metal rings).
- **Tata (a) bowed**: classical - **Sarangi, Esraj/Dilruba, Violin**; **Sarangi pioneers - Bhangash family**. Folk - **Chikara, Dilruba/Esraj, Sarinda, Tingteila, Kamaicha (Rajasthan, Manganiyars)**.
- **Tata (b) plectral**: classical - **Sitar, Veena, Tanpura, Guitar**; **sitar gharanas Jaipur, Varanasi, Etawah**; **Veena - Saraswati's instrument**. Folk - **Tumbi** (Punjab, Bhangra), **Ektara/Tun Tuna** (wandering monks), **Dotara** (**Bauls**), **Onavillu** (Kerala, bamboo).
- **Tata (c) struck**: **Gotuvadyam, Swaramandal** - played with hammers or sticks.
- **Santoor**: **100-stringed**; traditional instrument of **Jammu and Kashmir** since ancient times; **Sufiana kalam is accompanied by the Santoor** - the kalam is a **genre**, not an instrument.
- Major instruments by tradition: **Hindustani - tabla, sarangi, sitar, santoor**; **Carnatic - veena, mridangam, mandolin**; **common - flute and violin**.
- Prohibitions: no inventor, no invention date, no string count other than the santoor's 100, no sitar gharana beyond the three, no archaeological conversion of the Harappan or earth-drum glosses, no performer attribution beyond those recorded.

## 18. Notation, schools, broadcasting and recognition

- **Bhatkhande (1860-1936)**: musicologist; **first modern treatise on Hindustani classical music**; **modern thaat system**; ***Hindustani Sangeet Paddhati, Sreemallaksya Sangeetam, Abhinava Raga Manjari, A Short Historical Survey of the Music of Upper India***.
- **Paluskar (1872-1931)**: Hindustani musician; sang the original version of ***Raghupati Raghava Raja Ram***; set up the **Gandharva Mahavidyalaya in 1901**; credited with arranging ***Vande Mataram*** in **Raga Kafi**; introduced a **scheme of raga classification**; also a **Gwalior gharana** exponent.
- **Gandharva Mahavidyalaya, 1901** - express purpose of teaching and transmitting Indian classical music and dance to future generations; **opened in Lahore, moved to Mumbai in 1915**; focus on **Hindustani and Carnatic** forms.
- **Prayag Sangeet Samiti, 1926, Allahabad (Prayagraj)** - Hindustani classical music.
- **Marris College of Music, 1926, Lucknow**, founded by Bhatkhande - **theory and practice**; later **Bhatkhande Music Institute Deemed University**.
- **Sangeet Natak Akademi, 1952** - the **first national academy for the performing arts set up by the Government of India**, the **National Academy of Music, Dance and Drama**; remit across **music, dance and theatre, including traditional, folk and tribal forms and puppetry, and allied performing arts**; primary body for showcasing the performing arts; task of **promoting the enormous intangible heritage of India**. The standing source's compressed formula is "music, drama and dances".
- **SPIC MACAY, 1977, Dr Kiran Seth** - **Society for the Promotion of Indian Classical Music and Culture Amongst Youth**; voluntary youth movement; classical music, dance, yoga, meditation; **free-entry events**; global chapters; **headquarters in Delhi**.
- **Chaturprahar** - annual Indian classical music festival at the **National Centre for Performing Arts, Mumbai**, **based on the association of time with the ragas**.
- The five-stage argument: **employment -> elite household -> enrolment -> recognition -> audience**. Recognition evidence: **Bharat Ratna 1998, 1999, 2001, 2008** across two traditions and two instruments.
- **Boundary**: award administration, awardee lists and recognition policy are routed; **UNESCO intangible-heritage inscription is a separate international instrument** and is never equated with national academy recognition.
- **Dated current record**: a report of **11 June 2026** states that on **10 June 2026** the Sangeet Natak Akademi selected **seven Akademi Fellows (Akademi Ratna)** and awarded **108 artistes** from music, dance and other performing arts for **2024-25**; the same report describes the Akademi as the National Academy of Music, Dance and Drama, **established in 1953** and functioning under the **Ministry of Culture**, dedicated to **preserving, promoting and documenting** intangible cultural heritage. **The 1952 / 1953 conflict with the standing source is recorded and not resolved.**
- Cost of institutionalisation: transmissible outside hereditary lineages - **wider access and standardisation of what had been plural**.

## 19. Attribution, chronology and answer method

- Attribution ladder: **contemporaneous source -> recorded dispute -> recorded hedge -> refuse an invention date.**
- **Amir Khusrau** credited with the ghazal, tarana and trivat, the **sitar**, the **Sehtar**, the origin of **khayal**, the **tarana style**, and as **Father of Qawwali** - the last **widely disputed** in the same material.
- **Tansen created several new ragas** - composition, not invention of the system. **Purandara Dasa** as founding father or **Pitamaha** - an honorific about pedagogy and repertoire. **Annamacharya** - the **first known** Carnatic composer.
- The transferable rule: **the earliest source containing a form and the earliest source naming it are different facts** - dhrupad's **1294 CE** against **1593**.
- **Six disclosed conflicts**: the *Sangita Ratnakara* attribution; Malkauns's rasa; the performance-time rule; the Carnatic improvisation claim; Teejanbai's Padma Shri year; the national academy's founding year. **One recorded silence**: Dhamar, Chaturang, Ragasagar and Sargam are named and not described.
- Eight safe formulations replace the eight commonest overstatements - on Amir Khusrau, on Persian influence, on Carnatic improvisation, on raga and emotion, on Aurangzeb, on the gharana, on folk music, and on thaats against melakartas.
- Mark-scaled structures for 10, 15 and 20 marks, with specialised variants for instrument classification, composer-and-patronage comparison and patronage across both traditions.
- Five directive decodings: compare the traditions; discuss the gharana; classify the instruments; music and patronage across political change; treatises and theory.
- Universal opening rule: name the **system level** - theory, form, transmission, instrument or institution - and state a thesis at that level.

## Gaps

- **Dhamar, Chaturang, Ragasagar and Sargam** are named in the owned list of ten Hindustani styles but no structural description exists in the owned base or the core source. They are taught as a declared silence in L8 §6; no structure is supplied.
- **Indore gharana** is named in the list of famous gharanas with no characteristics, founders or exponents recorded. Declared undescribed in L11 §2.
- **Five of the ten thaats** (Khamaj, Asavari, Kalyan, Marwa, Todi) have no verified melakarta counterpart from the bounded material-fact pass. They are left unassigned in L5 §6; no correspondence is invented.
- **The Carnatic Trinity** is named without dates, and **Dikshitar and Syama Sastri** without any composition, raga or language. No supply is made.
- **Kirtana-to-kriti sequence**: no formal structural definition of kirtana and no dated transition are recorded. L12 §2 states this limit rather than narrating a sequence.
- **Tabla and sitar invention dates** are recorded as uncertain in the owned base. No date is supplied anywhere.
- **Bibhatsya rasa** has no raga example in the owned bank and is stated as rarely used in practice. No example is invented.
- **The 2025 Prelims Q18 fourth option** is degraded in the scanned source and is reproduced with the degradation marked; it is not reconstructed.
- **Sarod, nadaswaram, tavil, kanjira and morsing** are absent from both canonical owners. None of the five is named in the four-family instrument bank or anywhere else in the owned material, so none is taught, classified or attributed here, and no family placement, region, construction or exponent is supplied for any of them. The sarod's absence is already recorded separately at the incidental CSE 1982 item, where the session states that its evidence base neither names Ustad Amjad Ali Khan nor assigns him an instrument and supplies no answer; that note stands unchanged. The other four are recorded here for the first time, with no detail invented for any of them.

## Evidence, discrepancy and prohibition controls

- **Attribution discipline.** Where the owned base hedges, the hedge is carried: "attributed to" for khayal, "credited with" for the sitar, "it is believed" for the Sehtar and the Ravanahatha, "historians suggest" for the shabad, "widely disputed" for the qawwali origin. No hedge is upgraded to a statement.
- **Chronology discipline.** Text dates and person or patronage dates are used; form and instrument invention dates are refused, including for the tabla and the sitar. The distinction between the earliest source containing a form and the earliest source naming it is taught explicitly.
- **Composer discipline.** No date is supplied for any member of the Carnatic Trinity; no composition, raga or language is attributed to Dikshitar or Syama Sastri; no contemporaneity is asserted between Tyagaraja and Annamacharya. Where the standing account compressed the Thanjavur Quartet's patronage into Swathi Thirunal's court, the compression is corrected to the Thanjavur Maratha court with Vadivelu alone at Travancore, and no biography is expanded in the course of correcting it.
- **Gharana discipline.** Only recorded gharanas are named; the sitar gharana list is not extended beyond three; no gharana is attached to Ravi Shankar; affiliation is stated by training lineage rather than birthplace or language.
- **Instrument discipline.** Only the recorded mechanism, sub-type, region and exponent are stated; no inventor, no invention date, no string count other than the santoor's hundred; the Harappan and earth-drum mentions are carried as glosses and not converted into archaeological argument.
- **Award and status discipline.** Only the four Bharat Ratna years already in the owned base and the recorded Padma years for Teejanbai are stated, with her Padma Shri conflict disclosed. No musician award is asserted from the material-fact pass or the current report.
- **Speculation prohibitions observed.** No raga-emotion claim is made beyond the recorded pairings, and those are stated as conventions with their internal conflict disclosed. No origin legend is narrated. No simplistic Persian-versus-Indian causation is offered; the recorded influence statement is presented as contact history and set against six continuity facts.
- **Boundary discipline.** Choreography, dramaturgy, puppetry, religious doctrine, the ghazal's literary history, acoustics, instrument engineering, award administration and UNESCO inscription are declared routed in Lesson 1 and are not taught.
- **Prohibited-source exclusion.** No artifact under the final learner-package directory and no learner-v2 artifact was read or cited. The vector store was treated as optional and non-blocking and contributed nothing to this file.
- **Learner-facing cleanliness.** No generation, extraction or tooling machinery appears in the teaching text; provenance is confined to this ledger.
